pick over `tmux` when discoverability + KDL layouts + wasm plugins beat `tmux`'s ubiquity + plugin-catalog maturity (pick `tmux` for a server you SSH into once, pick `zellij` for the workstation you live in), over `screen` when literally any modern feature is in scope, over [`atuin`](clis/atuin/) / [`harlequin`](clis/harlequin/) / [`yazi`](clis/yazi/) / [`bottom`](clis/bottom/) when the question is the multiplexer not what runs inside the panes (those compose — run any of them in a zellij pane); caveat — sessions are per-host (no built-in sync across machines, pair with `mosh` for network-resilience on top), KDL is its own grammar (read `zellij setup --dump-config` output before hand-rolling complex layouts), default keybinds (`Ctrl+P` / `Ctrl+N` / `Ctrl+S`) collide with `bash` / `zsh` readline (the `LOCKED` mode `Ctrl+G` is the escape hatch but easy to forget in the first week), plugin ecosystem is dozens-not-hundreds versus `tmux`'s decade-deep catalog (check that a specific `tmux` plugin you depend on has a zellij equivalent before switching), and floating panes do not always survive detach / reattach the way tiled panes do (keep load-bearing panes tiled) | [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) |
| Want **a single deterministic root for every git repo on every machine you touch — `~/ghq/<host>/<owner>/<repo>` for every clone, no more `~/code/` vs `~/src/` vs `~/Projects/` vs `~/Desktop/oneoff/` archaeology, and `ghq list | fzf` as the one-keystroke jump to any of your 400 cloned repos** — `ghq` (x-motemen, MIT, v1.10.1) is the Go-implemented remote-repo organiser that imposes the convention so "do I already have that repo cloned?" stops being a question: `ghq get https://github.com/dbcli/pgcli` clones to `$(ghq root)/github.com/dbcli/pgcli` (deterministic from the URL — same path on every machine, every shell, every container), `ghq list` prints every cloned repo as one line per repo (relative to `ghq.root` — `ghq list -p` for absolute paths) for piping into `fzf`, the canonical `Ctrl+G` binding (`ghq list | fzf` then `cd` into the pick) becomes the most-pressed shortcut for many of the people who use it, and `ghq get --look-mirror github.com/<owner>` bulk-clones every public repo of a user / org for offline browsing or org-migration prep; v1.10.1 makes `ghq rm` worktree-aware so removing a colocated [`jj`](clis/jj/) / git-worktree repo no longer leaves orphan dirs; pick over [`gh repo clone`](https://cli.github.com/) when the goal is *layout discipline* across hosts (gh-cli is GitHub-only and clones into `pwd` with no organising convention; `ghq` works against GitHub / GitLab / Gitea / Forgejo / Bitbucket / arbitrary git URLs and always lands at the same path), over shell aliases over `git clone` for the `ghq list` index that makes fuzzy-jump possible (no list, no jump), over [`zoxide`](clis/zoxide/) for the *guarantee that the path exists* (zoxide ranks frequently-visited directories so it learns over time; `ghq` makes the directory exist at the predictable path the first time you clone — they compose: `ghq` decides where the repo lives, `zoxide` decides which one you visit most), over a hand-rolled `~/.repos.txt` for the auto-population (every `ghq get` updates the index for free); for an LLM-CLI workflow the deterministic path means a `CLAUDE.md` / `AGENTS.md` can reference `~/ghq/github.com/foo/bar` literally without "well, depends where the user cloned it", and agents that spider across N related repos (monorepo + satellite microservices) get a predictable filesystem layout to walk; caveat — adoption is all-or-nothing on a given machine (mixing `ghq`-managed repos with a separate `~/code/` tree defeats the "one place" promise — pick a migration day and `ghq import < <(git config --global --get-all ghq.root.alias.*)` the existing tree), `ghq.root` is per-machine config (synchronise via dotfiles or every machine drifts to a different default), the SSH-vs-HTTPS choice is per-`ghq get` invocation (clone via the form your CI / push workflow expects — re-cloning via the other form lands at the same path but only one origin is set), `ghq look <pattern>` requires the shell-function integration to actually `cd` (without it, it just prints the path — `eval "$(ghq look --shell)"` in your rc), and the bulk `--look-mirror` clone of a large org (1000+ repos) takes hours and several GB — script it with `--parallel` and run overnight | [`ghq`](clis/ghq/) |
| Want **encrypted, deduplicated, compressed snapshot backups to any object store (S3 / B2 / GCS / Azure Blob / SFTP / WebDAV / rclone-remote / local FS / network share) from one Go binary that is also the maintenance worker and the optional WebUI — content-defined chunking with a rolling hash for dedup that survives renames + moves + small edits where fixed-block schemes lose the dedup, AES-256-GCM / ChaCha20-Poly1305 per-chunk encryption under a key derived from your repository password (the server never sees plaintext or the key), declarative per-source retention policies in `kopia policy set` instead of shell-loops over `forget --keep-*`, and snapshot mounts (`kopia mount <id> /mnt/snap`) for ad-hoc restore browsing without a full extract** — `kopia` (kopia, Apache-2.0, v0.22.3) is the install-once-and-forget backup engine for the homelab and small-team workload that wants `restic`-class dedup with a real WebUI shipped in the same binary: `kopia repository create s3 --bucket=mybackup --access-key=...` initialises the repo once, `kopia snapshot create ~/Documents` from any machine that knows the repo password snapshots and uploads only the changed chunks (re-snapshotting the same 200 GB tree the next day uploads MB not GB), `kopia policy set --global --keep-latest=10 --keep-hourly=24 --keep-daily=14 --keep-weekly=8 --keep-monthly=12` declares the retention shape and the maintenance worker prunes on a schedule (no `cron` orchestration of `forget` calls), `kopia snapshot restore <id> /tmp/restored/` extracts a snapshot or `kopia mount <id> /mnt/snap` FUSE-mounts it read-only for browsing a single file out of a multi-TB tree without staging the whole thing, and `kopia server start --address=127.0.0.1:51515 --tls-generate-cert` boots the WebUI for non-CLI teammates to browse / restore without a separate product; pick over [`restic`](clis/restic/) when the WebUI + per-source YAML-shaped policies + broad object-store backends (any rclone remote works directly without a wrapper) matter more than `restic`'s longer track record and larger community (both have the same dedup-encrypt-snapshot shape; pick `restic` for "the OG with the biggest install base", pick `kopia` for "WebUI + policy DSL out of the box"), over `borg` when remote-storage flexibility matters more than `borg`'s Linux-first SSH-target sweet spot (borg shines on a dedicated SSH backup host; `kopia` shines on object storage), over [`rclone`](clis/rclone/) when the workload is *snapshot backup with dedup + encryption + retention* not *file sync* (rclone is `rsync` for the cloud — no dedup across snapshots, no rolling-hash chunking, no policy engine — the two compose: rclone moves files around, kopia takes versioned snapshots of them), over a hand-rolled `tar | gpg | aws s3 cp` script for the dedup + incremental + browse-without-restore that script never gives you; caveat — repository password is the *only* thing protecting the data and there is no built-in recovery flow if you lose it (write it to your password manager *and* a paper backup *before* you create the repo), the maintenance worker (compaction, GC, index rebuild) is heavy on a busy repo (schedule it on a quiet machine and let the others snapshot through it; `kopia maintenance set --owner=...` decides which client owns full maintenance), the WebUI binds on the address you tell it and ships with no auth (`--server-username` / `--server-password` is htpasswd-grade — front it with a reverse proxy with real auth before exposing beyond `127.0.0.1`), object-store egress is the cost ceiling (a full restore of a multi-TB repo from S3 costs real money — `kopia mount` + selective copy is the cheaper path for "I just need one file"), and content-defined chunking means changing `--block-hash`, `--encryption`, or splitter parameters is a *new repo* (you cannot retrofit; pick the chunker on day one) | [`kopia`](clis/kopia/) |
| Want **a `ranger`-class terminal file manager rewritten async-first — three-column TUI (parent dir / current dir / preview) where cursoring onto a 100k-entry directory or a slow network mount never freezes the UI (loading state + update-when-ready instead of block-the-event-loop), inline image previews via the terminal's native graphics protocol with no `ueberzug` daemon, bulk-rename through `$EDITOR` (select with `space`, hit `:` rename, edit the names in vim, save, the file manager applies the diff), and a Lua plugin runtime + on-disk keybind / opener DSL so customization is a config file not a recompile** — `yazi` (sxyazi, MIT, v26.1.22) is the file manager for operators whose `ranger` / `nnn` / `lf` is finally costing more keystrokes than it saves: navigation is `vim`-flavored (`h` / `j` / `k` / `l`, `gg` / `G`, `/` for in-dir search, `Ctrl+f` / `Ctrl+b` for page up / down), selection is `space` to toggle and `v` for visual mode, `y` / `x` / `p` are yank / cut / paste with full undo via the trash on `d`, `r` renames in place, `:` opens the command palette, `Tab` toggles preview-pane focus, `M` toggles hidden files, `s` opens the sort menu (name / size / mtime / ctime / extension), `f` is a live regex filter on the current dir, image previews use `kitty` / `iterm2` / `sixel` / `chafa` fallback (no X11, no separate daemon), archives extract with `x` and create with `:archive`, and the `ya` companion CLI is the IPC surface for `ya emit reveal /path` to focus a path from another pane; the optional shell wrapper that `cd`s the parent shell to wherever yazi exited turns "navigate visually, then drop back to the right shell prompt" into one keystroke; pick over [`ranger`](https://github.com/ranger/ranger) when async-no-block + native graphics-protocol image previews + Lua plugins beat Python's plugin power (ranger blocks on slow IO; yazi does not), over `nnn` / `lf` when the preview pane + bulk-rename + plugin DSL are load-bearing, over a GUI file manager when the workflow already lives in `tmux` / `zellij` / SSH, over `find` + `mv` + `mv` + `mv` when the bulk-rename + visual triage + delete-the-wrong-ones combo is faster as a TUI; caveat — image previews require a graphics-capable terminal (`kitty` / `wezterm` / `iterm2` / `ghostty` / sixel-capable; `Terminal.app` / `gnome-terminal` fall back to `chafa` unicode blocks which is functional but ugly), the `ya` CLI is a separate binary (Homebrew installs both, `cargo install` needs `yazi-fm` AND `yazi-cli`), bulk-rename via `$EDITOR` is line-order-sensitive (yazi pairs Nth original with Nth edited line; reorder a line and the mapping shifts), trash on Linux requires `trash-cli` for `d` (without it `d` errors and only `D` permanent-delete works; macOS uses the built-in trash directly), plugin API is pre-1.0-stable (calendar-versioned releases iterate fast — pin plugins by commit in `~/.config/yazi/package.toml`), and there is no first-class git integration in core (git-status icons in the listing live in the `git.yazi` plugin) | [`yazi`](clis/yazi/) |
| Want **a cross-platform graphical `htop` — CPU per-core / memory / swap / network rx-tx / temperature / disk IO / battery rendered as braille-character sparklines that look fine in any terminal (no graphics protocol needed, the curve answers "is this CPU spike a runaway, a workload, or a brief blip" before you have to think about it), runs natively on Windows where `htop` does not with the same keybinds across Linux / macOS / Windows / FreeBSD, a sortable filterable process table with tree mode + grouped mode, and the dashboard layout is a declarative TOML file declaring a tree of widgets with width ratios so multiple layouts are different config files swapped via `--config /path/to/layout.toml`** — `bottom` (ClementTsang, MIT, 0.12.3, binary name `btm`) is the system monitor for operators who want to *see* CPU spike across the last 60 seconds, watch memory grow as a curve, correlate a network burst against a process spawn, without opening Grafana: default layout is six widgets (CPU graph, memory + swap graph, network graph with rx / tx + cumulative, temperature panel, disk IO graph, process table), `e` expands the focused widget to fullscreen, `f` freezes the display for reading transient state, `dd` kills a process with a signal picker (default `SIGTERM`), `/` live-filters the process table (regex by default, prefix `\` for literal), `t` toggles tree mode (process hierarchy with parent / child indentation), `g` toggles grouped mode (collapse same-name processes into one row with child count), `+` / `-` zoom the time axis on any graph, `Ctrl+r` reloads config without restart, and `--basic` collapses graphs into plain numeric panels (the `htop` look) for low-bandwidth SSH; pick over [`htop`](https://htop.dev/) when graphs over time + Windows support + declarative TOML layouts matter (pick `htop` when a stock CCS-only Linux box and a process list is all you need), over `glances` when graph rendering + speed + cross-platform consistency beat plugin breadth (pick `glances` when you want Docker stats / sensor plugins / GPU panels via plugins), over `top` for the obvious reasons, over a separate dashboard (Grafana / Prometheus node-exporter) when single-pane terminal fits the workflow and the question is "is this one host healthy right now"; pairs with [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) (run `btm` in a small dedicated pane to watch a long-running fine-tune / batch eval / training job at a glance); caveat — process names on macOS sometimes truncated by kernel API (full path / argv in expanded view via `e`), GPU panels limited to NVIDIA via NVML on Linux / Windows (AMD / Apple Silicon GPUs are not first-class — pair with `asitop` / `mactop` for Apple Silicon GPU stats), sampling at fixed intervals (default 1s, `--rate 250` samples every 250ms with proportional CPU cost — not a replacement for `perf` / `bpftrace` for sub-second observation), `dd` to kill is two keystrokes and the signal picker is small (read the picker title row name before pressing enter), battery panel reads vendor-specific APIs and shows blanks on some non-mainstream BIOSes (toggle off with `b`), and config schema has had breaking changes between 0.x minor versions (re-run `btm --generate_config` after a major upgrade and port customizations) | [`bottom`](clis/bottom/) |
| Want **statistically rigorous wall-clock benchmarking with one command and no harness code — runs any shell command N times, auto-tunes the run count by initial-run duration, accepts `--warmup N` to discard cache-cold iterations, detects statistical outliers, and prints a mean ± σ summary with min / max range, then for multiple commands prints a sorted ranking with relative-speed ratios (`Command A ran 1.42 ± 0.06 times faster than Command B`); `--parameter-scan threads 1 16 -D 1` sweeps a parameter and `--export-{markdown,json,csv,asciidoc}` drops the result into a PR description, a CI regression gate, or a downstream plot** — `hyperfine` (sharkdp, MIT or Apache-2.0, v1.20.0) is the cheapest tool that gives you the σ to answer "did this change make things faster or did I just get lucky on the dice roll": the hard problem is *not* timing one run but knowing whether a 1.4× delta is real, and `hyperfine` solves it with `--warmup 3 --runs 30 'rg "TODO" .' 'grep -r "TODO" .'` in 30 seconds; `--prepare 'sync; echo 3 > /proc/sys/vm/drop_caches'` flips to cold-cache benchmarking by running a setup command between every measured run; pick over `time` / a hand-rolled `bash time` loop for the auto-tuned run count + warmup + outlier detection + comparison ranking + export shapes (those are exactly what you would re-implement otherwise), over `criterion` (Rust) / `pytest-benchmark` (Python) when you want language-agnostic shell-level timing not in-process micro-benchmarks (pair them — micro-benchmarks for hot loops, `hyperfine` for end-to-end), over [`bottom`](clis/bottom/) when the workload is a discrete N-iteration measurement not a continuous live monitor, pairs naturally with [`ttok`](clis/ttok/) (token-budget pre-flight) and any LLM CLI for "is this prompt template + model combo actually faster end-to-end"; caveat — wall-clock only (does not break down into per-syscall / per-function profiles — pair with `perf` / `samply` / `cargo-flamegraph` when the answer is "slow, why?"), sub-millisecond commands need `-N` to skip the ~1-2ms shell-spawn floor (the trade-off is losing pipe / redirect / `&&` parsing in the command string), `--warmup` (timed-but-discarded iterations) and `--prepare` (setup command run *between* iterations) are different things and mixing them up gives the wrong cache posture, parameter scans multiply runs by parameter count (1..16 × default 10 runs = 160 invocations — for a 3-minute build that's an 8-hour benchmark; tune `--runs 5`), the outlier warning is heuristic (catches GC pauses, not subtle bimodal distributions — export to JSON and inspect the histogram for high-stakes decisions), and Windows process-creation overhead is higher than Linux / macOS so sub-10ms commands have a wider σ baseline (prefer `--runs 50` on Windows or wrap the iteration into the program under test) | [`hyperfine`](clis/hyperfine/) |
| Want **a customisable terminal dashboard for `gh` — a Bubble Tea TUI that turns the GitHub CLI into a multi-section pull-request and issue inbox where each section is a saved GitHub search query declared in `~/.config/gh-dash/config.yml` (e.g. "PRs awaiting my review", "my open PRs", "issues I'm assigned in repo X", "PRs failing CI in my org", "stale PRs older than 14 days") and the result is a keyboard-navigable table with live status, CI checks, review state, lines added / removed per row, and one-keystroke actions (checkout, comment, approve, merge, open-in-browser) — including `command:` template keybinds with `{{.PrNumber}}` / `{{.RepoName}}` / `{{.HeadRefName}}` interpolation so "checkout the PR *and* open VS Code in the worktree" is one custom key** — `gh-dash` (dlvhdr, MIT, v4.23.2) installs as a `gh` extension (`gh extension install dlvhdr/gh-dash` then `gh dash`) and reuses the existing `gh auth` token, so the dashboard config lives in dotfiles and moves between machines; collapses the daily "what do I owe people on GitHub today" loop into a single TUI you pin in a [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) / `tmux` pane; pick over `gh pr list` / `gh search` for the multiple-parallel-saved-search-sections-in-one-view + one-keystroke-actions-on-the-row-under-the-cursor + live-CI-status-per-row combo (raw `gh` is for scripting, `gh dash` is for the day-to-day inbox), over the GitHub web UI's "Pulls" tab for the keyboard-first persistent multi-query view that lives in the same terminal as your editor, over [`lazygit`](clis/lazygit/) when the question is the *remote* GitHub state (PRs / issues / reviews / CI) not the *local* git repo (they compose — `gh dash` shells out to `gh pr checkout` so the PR you pick lands as a local branch `lazygit` then operates on), over [`pr-agent`](clis/pr-agent/) / [`code-review-gpt`](clis/code-review-gpt/) which produce inline AI review comments at PR time orthogonal to a human triage surface (a common workflow: AI reviewer drops `/review` summaries in CI, then `gh dash` is where you walk the queue and decide which suggestions to act on); caveat — GitHub-only (no GitLab / Bitbucket / Gitea / Forgejo backends — built on `gh`'s GraphQL surface), each section is a separate search query refreshed on `R` and on a configurable interval so 10+ sections × auto-refresh × secondary GraphQL fetches for CI / reviewer state can hit GitHub primary / search rate limits (keep total sections ≤ ~10 and tune `defaults.refetchIntervalMinutes`), the detail pane is read-mostly (writing a multi-paragraph review with suggestions is still nicer in the browser or via `gh pr review --body-file`; `gh-dash` shines at triage), `command:` template keybinds shell-execute interpolated PR / repo fields so treat the YAML the same way you treat a shell rc file (a hand-rolled `command:` with unquoted `{{.HeadRefName}}` is a shell injection foothold if someone names a branch creatively), and write actions need `gh auth` scopes (`a` / `m` / `c` use the existing token; `gh auth refresh -s repo` if approve / merge silently fails) | [`gh-dash`](clis/gh-dash/) |
| Want **`httpie`-grade ergonomics with `curl`-grade portability — a single static Rust binary that reimplements the `httpie` UX (intuitive `xh GET example.com name=value` syntax where `key=value` is JSON body field, `key:=value` is non-string JSON, `key==value` is query param, `Header:value` is request header, `@file` reads a body / field from disk, coloured + pretty-printed JSON / XML / HTML responses, sessions, downloads with progress) without the Python runtime — cold-starts in 5-15 ms, runs on a busybox / scratch container with no `pip`, ships as a ~6 MB binary on `brew` / `cargo` / `scoop` / `winget` / `apt` / Nix, and respects existing `~/.netrc`** — `xh` (ducaale, MIT, v0.25.3) is what to reach for when `curl`'s `-H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"name":"alice"}'` ergonomics start eating your day and `httpie`'s ~700 ms Python cold start (or its "requires `pip` and a Python runtime on this server") becomes friction in CI / minimal containers / SSH targets; the syntax is *deliberately* `httpie`-compatible so muscle memory transfers, output is coloured + pretty-printed by default (JSON indented, headers highlighted, content type auto-detected, binary content elided rather than dumped), `--download` saves to a file with a progress bar, `--session=name` persists cookies / `Authorization` / hostname between calls in `~/.config/xh/sessions/<name>.json`; pick over `curl` for everyday HTTP API debugging when nicer JSON ergonomics + sessions + prettier defaults pay back the install step (keep `curl` for protocol breadth — SFTP / IMAP / RTSP — and obscure flags), over `httpie` itself when the Python runtime is the friction (pick `httpie` when you need its plugin ecosystem like `httpie-jwt` / `httpie-aws-auth`), over [`posting`](clis/posting/) when the question is one-shot CLI scripting and ad-hoc shell-pipe debugging not interactive multi-pane TUI exploration with collections-as-YAML (pick `posting` for the inverse), composes naturally with [`mods`](clis/mods/) / [`llm`](clis/llm/) / `jq` for "agent calls API, parses response, decides next action" pipelines via `xh --pretty=none --body`; caveat — does not load `httpie` plugins (no AWS SigV4 / JWT auth / OAuth2 helpers shipped as `httpie-*` packages — pre-mint the token and paste the `Authorization` header, or stay on `httpie` for those flows), HTTP/3 / QUIC support inherits whatever `reqwest` / `hyper` provide at build time (prefer a recent `curl --http3` for HTTP/3 testing), output detection is heuristic and an API returning JSON with `Content-Type: text/plain` lands as plain text (force `--pretty=all` or pipe through `jq`), sessions store cookies + headers in plaintext (fine for dev tokens; use a wrapper script pulling from a secrets manager for production credentials), default does not follow redirects (intentional, mirrors `httpie` — pass `--follow` or `--max-redirects N`), and binary responses are detected and elided to keep the terminal sane (pass `--download` to save them or `--pretty=none --body` to stream the raw bytes through a pipe) | [`xh`](clis/xh/) |
| Want **a background Rust code-checker that lives in a side pane and re-runs `cargo check` / `clippy` / `clippy --all-targets --all-features` / `test` / `nextest` / `doc` on every save, distilling the result into a single-screen view (warnings collapsed by default, errors expanded with file:line links, build-stage progress at the bottom) instead of a scrolling firehose, with `c` / `w` / `t` / `d` keystrokes hot-swapping between jobs without restart so "is this still clippy-clean and does the test pass?" is two keystrokes** — `bacon` (Canop, AGPL-3.0, v3.22.0) sits in a small `tmux` / `zellij` / `kitty`-split pane next to the editor and watches the file tree of a Rust project, running the configured `cargo` job on every save and re-rendering a one-screen distilled view of the current error set deduplicated and sorted by severity (so a multi-file refactor's 40-warning storm is readable instead of a `cargo-watch` firehose that scrolled the second error off-screen the moment the third fired); per-project `bacon.toml` (or `.bacon-locations.toml`) declares custom jobs (`cross check`, `cargo +nightly clippy`, `cargo nextest run -E 'package(my-crate)'`, scoped `cargo test --doc -p my-crate`), keybindings to invoke them, and on-success hooks (`on_change_strategy = "kill_then_restart"` for long jobs that should not pile up); the `--headless` mode emits structured output for CI / agents; pick over `cargo-watch` for the *distilled-error-pane* UX (cargo-watch re-prints raw compiler output that scrolls; bacon keeps the current error set on one screen), over `rust-analyzer`'s editor diagnostics when you want the *real `cargo` invocation's* output (rust-analyzer's check command is configurable but its output goes to the editor's problems pane, not a side terminal you also use for `clippy --all-targets`), over [`watchexec`](clis/watchexec/) when the watched command is specifically `cargo` (watchexec is the language-agnostic primitive — bacon is the Rust-aware UI on top, with the right output renderer); pairs naturally with [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) (one pane runs `bacon`, one runs the editor, one runs `git status` / `lazygit`) and with LLM-CLI workflows that ask an agent to refactor Rust (bacon in a sibling pane is the human-side feedback loop that catches "agent's diff compiles but trips clippy" before the next agent turn); caveat — AGPL-3.0 (the *binary* is fine to install and use commercially without obligation; the obligation triggers if you redistribute a *modified* bacon or expose modified bacon as a network service — neither shape applies to "I run it on my laptop"), Rust-only by design (the `[jobs]` table is parameterised but the output renderer assumes `cargo`-shaped JSON / textual diagnostics; for non-Rust projects, drop to `watchexec`), the always-on file watcher consumes a small amount of CPU on huge worktrees (tune `default_job` to the cheapest one — usually `check`, not `clippy --all-targets` — and bind heavier jobs to keystrokes), and in `kill_then_restart` strategy a long-running job that's killed mid-build can leave incremental-compilation artifacts in a slightly degraded state (recover with `cargo clean -p <crate>` if compile times start drifting upward; rare in practice) | [`bacon`](clis/bacon/) |
| Want **a Conventional-Commits-aware changelog generator that walks `git log` between two refs, parses commit messages against a configurable regex grammar, groups commits by type (feat / fix / docs / perf / breaking) ordered by precedence, and renders the result through a Tera template with shipped presets for Keep-a-Changelog / GitHub releases / GitLab / Gitea / Bitbucket / Cocogitto formats — plus auto-semver-bump detection, monorepo per-package scoping via `--include-path`, and a one-step GitHub Action for "tag → render → attach to release" pipelines** — `git-cliff` (orhun, Apache-2.0 OR MIT, v2.13.1) sits in the gap between hand-curated changelogs (rot the moment shipping cadence picks up) and `gh release create --generate-notes` (a flat list with no grouping or breaking-change call-outs): the *commits* are still hand-written so quality is bounded by commit-message discipline, but the *structuring + rendering* is one command (`git cliff --tag v2.0.0 --output CHANGELOG.md`) and one TOML file (`cliff.toml`) checked into the repo; modes are `git cliff` (full history), `git cliff --unreleased` (since last tag — the "what's about to ship" preview), `git cliff --bump` (auto-detect next semver from breaking-change footers + commit types and emit it), `git cliff --context` (dump the parsed JSON shape for downstream templating), `--include-path packages/api/**` for monorepo per-package scoping; pick over `release-please` / `semantic-release` when you want a *standalone* changelog command without adopting a release-automation framework that also wants to control versioning, branching, and tagging (use git-cliff for the changelog, keep your existing release script), over `gh release create --generate-notes` when the flat list with no grouping is the readability problem, over `auto-changelog` / `conventional-changelog-cli` for the Tera-template flexibility + Rust binary distribution (no Node runtime, one-binary install), pairs with [`gptcommit`](clis/gptcommit/) / [`opencommit`](clis/opencommit/) (LLM-generated Conventional Commits at write time, deterministic changelog at release time) and [`gh-dash`](clis/gh-dash/) (browse PRs going into the next release in the dashboard, run `git cliff --unreleased` to draft the notes); caveat — output quality is bounded by commit-message discipline (a repo full of "wip" / "fix stuff" commits gets a changelog full of "wip" / "fix stuff" — adopt a commit-msg `pre-commit` hook with `commitlint` or [`gptcommit`](clis/gptcommit/) before adopting `git-cliff`), `--bump` reads breaking-change semantics from `!` in the type (`feat!:`) or a `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer (commits that break the API but neither shape are not detected — review the bumped version before tagging), monorepo per-package scoping via `--include-path` is path-based not package-graph-aware (a commit touching shared infrastructure under a different path won't appear in the package's changelog even if it affected the package — no clean fix; document the convention), and `cliff.toml`'s commit-parser regex list is order-sensitive (the first match wins — put more-specific patterns before catch-alls) | [`git-cliff`](clis/git-cliff/) |
| Want **a TUI runner for multiple long-lived shell processes — process list on the left (name + status colour: running / stopped / exited-ok / exited-fail), focused process's combined stdout+stderr scrollback with ANSI colour preserved on the right, declared as one `mprocs.yaml` with per-process `cmd` / `cwd` / `env` / `autostart` / `autorestart` / `stop` (signal name or literal keystroke string for programs that own a TTY), graceful shutdown on `q` so the local Postgres container actually stops instead of leaking, and isolated per-process scrollbacks switchable in one keystroke** — `mprocs` (pvolok, MIT, v0.9.2) is the dedicated answer when the dev-loop spawns five terminals (`pnpm dev` for the frontend, `cargo run` for the backend, `docker compose up postgres redis`, a `wrangler tail` log stream, `pytest --watch` for the tests) and the existing options are unsatisfying — `tmux` solves it but the per-pane setup is muscle memory you can't share with a teammate, a Procfile under `foreman` / `overmind` interleaves the streams into one unreadable wall when two services are noisy at once, and shell-job-control + `&` + named log files is the workflow nobody actually wants to live in; mprocs's `mprocs.yaml` is one declarative file in the repo root that boots the whole local stack with one command, each process gets its own scrollback (so the database's verbose connect logs don't drown the application's request log), `r` restarts a single process without disturbing the others, `x` sends the configured stop signal then `SIGKILL` after timeout, and the per-process `stop` config supports literal keystroke strings (`q\n`) for programs that own a TTY and need a specific quit input rather than `SIGTERM`; pick over `tmux` + `tmuxinator` when you want declarative-YAML over Ruby-DSL with isolated scrollbacks instead of a grid of panes (use tmux when you also need full PTY interactivity in each pane — mprocs is process-runner-shaped not terminal-multiplexer-shaped), over `foreman` / `overmind` when interleaved log output is the readability problem (mprocs gives each process its own scrollback), over [`pueue`](clis/pueue/) when the workload is *long-lived dev processes you want to see live* not *a queue of one-shot batch jobs*, over [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) when the question is "which processes are running and what are they printing" not "how do I lay out my terminals" (they compose naturally — run `mprocs` in one zellij pane alongside the editor and a shell, different layers); caveat — process state lives in-memory in the running `mprocs` instance (kill mprocs and the children are SIGKILLed by default — the YAML's `stop` config controls graceful shutdown if you `q` cleanly, but a parent crash skips it), the focused-process scrollback is a fixed-size ring buffer (long noisy processes lose oldest output — pipe to a real log file via the `cmd` if you need full retention), `autorestart: true` on a process that crashes immediately on startup is a tight loop that pegs a CPU (add a backoff or fix the underlying crash, mprocs has no built-in exponential backoff), and `a` send-keys-to-PTY mode (so `npm run dev`'s `r` to restart works) is a single-keystroke send not a full PTY hijack — for full interactive TTY work, mprocs is the wrong layer (pick `tmux` / `zellij`) | [`mprocs`](clis/mprocs/) |
| Want **a full-screen vim-flavoured TUI for live Kubernetes operations — a typed command bar (`:pods`, `:deployments`, `:svc`, `:nodes`, `:cm`, `:secrets`, `:events`, `:ns`, `:ctx`, plus the same for any installed CRD) opens a sortable, filterable, auto-refreshing view of the resource, and from the row under the cursor one keystroke tails logs (`l`), execs into a container (`s`), describes (`d`), edits live YAML (`e`), port-forwards (`Shift+F`), or deletes with confirmation (`Ctrl+D`); `metrics-server` data renders inline as CPU/mem columns, pulse mode shows real-time cluster activity, and YAML configurable skins / hotkeys / plugins / per-context views live in `~/.config/k9s/`** — `k9s` (derailed, Apache-2.0, v0.50.18) is the canonical answer to "I need to triage what is wrong in this cluster right now": the single-screen, auto-refresh, one-keystroke-action loop collapses 4-6 `kubectl get` / `logs -f` / `describe` / `exec` invocations into one TUI session, and the `--readonly` flag plus per-context RBAC make it safe to keep open against production while you investigate; pick over raw `kubectl` for the interactive triage loop (keep `kubectl` for one-shot scripted CI applies), over [`kdash`](clis/kdash/) when you want the deeper per-resource action surface and editing posture (kdash is the lighter Rust overview-first alternative — use kdash for at-a-glance health, k9s for the working session), over [`kubectl-ai`](clis/kubectl-ai/) when the question is direct human-driven inspection not natural-language translation to `kubectl` commands (orthogonal layers — they compose); caveat — mutating actions are two keystrokes deep (`Ctrl+D` delete, `e` live edit) and easy to fire on the wrong context, so set up read-only RBAC contexts for production and pin a high-contrast skin per-cluster as a visual safety belt, the `metrics-server` columns silently show blanks if the cluster does not have it installed (not an error — a missed signal), and plugin / skin YAML schema has shifted between minor releases (re-run `k9s info` after a major upgrade and re-validate custom plugins) | [`k9s`](clis/k9s/) |
| Want **a modern `dig` replacement that speaks every wire format out of the box — plain UDP/TCP, DNS-over-TLS, DNS-over-HTTPS, DNS-over-QUIC, and DNSCrypt, picked automatically from the resolver URL scheme (`@tls://1.1.1.1`, `@https://dns.google/dns-query`, `@quic://dns.adguard.com`, `@sdns://...`), with colourised + column-aligned default output and switchable `--format json|yaml|short` modes so the same invocation shells into a `jq` pipeline or a script without re-learning a different tool, plus parallel multi-resolver queries in one command for "do these resolvers agree on this record" checks** — `doggo` (mr-karan, GPL-3.0, v1.1.5) is the right primitive when the daily loop is "resolve this name over my preferred encrypted resolver and let me grep / jq the result": the DoH/DoT/DoQ-without-extra-flags story is the differentiator over `dig` (one URL scheme vs an extra `kdig` / `+https` / `+tls` flag set), and `--json` output beats `dig +short` / `awk` parsing fragility for shell scripts; pick over [`dig`](https://bind9.readthedocs.io/) for the encrypted-resolver-first ergonomics + JSON-out + multi-resolver parallel checks, over [`dog`](clis/dog/) when the licence (GPL vs MIT) and DoQ support matter (dog is the closer-to-pure-replacement Rust alternative, no DoQ — pick whichever fits your distribution constraints), over `nslookup` for any non-trivial use; skip for deep DNSSEC chain validation, AXFR zone transfers, raw EDNS option inspection, or sub-millisecond per-packet timing where `dig +trace +dnssec +stats` and `kdig +bufsize=1232 +nsid` still expose more knobs (pick `dig` for those); caveat — GPL-3.0 is fine for end-user CLI use but a non-starter for vendoring source into a closed-source product (pull from the binary release in those cases), system-resolver fallback inherits `/etc/resolv.conf` so a corporate VPN's split-DNS quirks affect default behaviour (force a specific `@resolver` to bypass), and the JSON schema is stable but minor releases occasionally add fields (pin tooling that depends on exact key sets) | [`doggo`](clis/doggo/) |
| Want **a polyglot YAML-defined task runner with `make`-style file-fingerprint incremental skip-if-up-to-date built in, no Tab-vs-space DSL trap, and Windows-without-bash portability via an embedded POSIX-shell interpreter (`mvdan/sh`) so the same `Taskfile.yml` runs on Linux, macOS, and Windows contributors' laptops without `bash` installed — declared tasks compose via `deps:` (parallel by default), `sources:` + `generates:` (file-glob fingerprints for skip-if-up-to-date), `vars:` + `env:` + `dotenv:` (Go-template `{{.VAR}}` interpolation), `preconditions:`, `requires:`, and `includes:` for monorepo per-package Taskfiles; CLI exposes `task --list` (documented tasks), `task --watch <name>` (re-run on file change), `task --parallel a b c`, `task --dry`, `task --summary <name>`** — `task` (go-task, MIT, v3.50.0) is the right answer when the project is genuinely polyglot (Node frontend + Go backend + Python data pipeline + Terraform module + Docker compose stack) and you want one command surface (`task lint`, `task test`, `task deploy:staging`) that works on every contributor's laptop and in CI; pick over `make` for the Windows-without-bash portability + YAML-not-Makefile DSL + readable file-fingerprints, over [`just`](clis/just/) when YAML readability + `includes:` for monorepos + file-fingerprint incremental skip beat `just`'s smaller `make`-shaped recipe DSL (pick `just` over `task` for DSL minimalism, ad-hoc one-liner recipes, and recipe-as-script ergonomics), over `npm scripts` / `cargo aliases` / `poetry scripts` when more than one language is in play (a single-language project should stay on its native runner); pairs with [`mprocs`](clis/mprocs/) (long-lived dev processes the way `task` is short-lived recipes) and [`watchexec`](clis/watchexec/) (`task --watch` covers the common case but watchexec handles non-Taskfile triggers); caveat — the embedded shell is `mvdan/sh` not the host's `bash` so a recipe that depends on bash-isms (process substitution `<(...)`, `[[ ]]` extended test, arrays beyond basics) needs explicit `cmds:` with `bash -c '...'`, file-fingerprint incremental skip relies on the `sources:` / `generates:` globs being accurate (a recipe that touches a file outside its declared globs becomes stale silently — prefer `--force` in CI), `includes:` from a remote URL is a supply-chain consideration (pin by tag or vendor the included Taskfile), and the Go-template interpolation overlaps awkwardly with shell `${}` syntax (single-quote shell strings in `cmds:` to avoid double-evaluation) | [`task`](clis/task/) |
| Want **a fast Rust-implemented Node.js version manager that auto-switches on `cd` via `.node-version` / `.nvmrc` files but doesn't add `nvm`'s ~600 ms shell-startup tax — single static binary instead of a multi-thousand-line bash script, per-shell ephemeral `PATH` so different terminal tabs can run different Node versions, deterministic checksum-verified tarball installs under `~/.local/share/fnm/node-versions/`, first-class Windows / PowerShell / winget support, `eval "$(fnm env --use-on-cd)"` shim is the only shell-side install** — `fnm` (Schniz, GPL-3.0, v1.39.0) is the right answer when Node is the only language you need to multi-version per project and `nvm`'s shell-startup cost is the friction; pick over `nvm` for the startup-time win + single-binary install + Windows-first support, over [`mise`](clis/mise/) when you don't need Python / Ruby / Go in the same tool (mise is broader; fnm is sharper), over [`volta`](clis/volta/) when you prefer sidecar `.node-version` files to Volta's `package.json`-embedded toolchain pins; caveat — GPL-3.0 is fine for end-user dev tools but worth noting if your distribution model embeds source, the `--use-on-cd` shim is the only path that gives auto-switching (forget the eval and you have a manual `fnm use` workflow), and per-shell-only activation means a `cron` job needs to source the env explicitly | [`fnm`](clis/fnm/) |
| Want **a Rust-implemented project package manager that reuses the conda-forge binary ecosystem (CUDA, BLAS, GDAL/PROJ, ffmpeg, R, OpenJDK, Postgres clients) plus PyPI in one cross-platform `pixi.lock` lockfile (`linux-64` + `osx-arm64` + `osx-64` + `win-64` resolved together so Linux CI, Mac dev's M-series, and Windows contributors all materialise identical environments), Cargo-shaped UX (`pixi.toml` declares deps + tasks + features, `pixi run <task>` activates and runs, `pixi install` materialises into `.pixi/envs/`), multi-environment composition via `[feature.dev]` / `[feature.cuda]` flag-and-feature arithmetic, file-fingerprint task `inputs:` / `outputs:` for skip-if-up-to-date, single Rust binary instead of an Anaconda distribution** — `pixi` (prefix-dev, BSD-3-Clause, v0.67.2) is the right answer when the Python project genuinely needs conda-forge's binary system-library coverage (ML with CUDA pytorch, geospatial with GDAL/PROJ, scientific with R alongside Python) and pure-pip workflows hit "pip can't satisfy this OS dep" walls; pick over [`uv`](clis/uv/) when system libs matter (uv is faster but pure-Python wheels only), over `conda` / `mamba` for the lockfile-backed solver + single-binary install + Cargo-shaped tasks, over `poetry` / `pdm` / `hatch` for the system-library reach; skip for non-conda-forge ecosystems where the package coverage isn't there, and for pure-Python projects where uv is faster and smaller; caveat — conda-forge is the win and the lock-in (a project tied to pixi is also tied to conda-forge availability for its deps), the multi-platform lockfile is generated against declared platforms (a contributor on `linux-aarch64` not in the platform list still hits a solve), and `[pypi-dependencies]` mixes wheels into the conda env which can occasionally produce binary-incompat surprises (prefer the conda-forge build of the same package when one exists) | [`pixi`](clis/pixi/) |
| Want **a template-free Kubernetes manifest customizer where every YAML in the source tree stays a valid `kubectl apply`-able object (no Go-template DSL, no `helm template` render-then-apply step, the editor's YAML LSP works on inputs because they ARE the inputs) and per-environment variation is layered as `overlays/staging/` + `overlays/prod/` `kustomization.yaml` files patching a shared `base/` via strategic-merge / JSON-6902 patches, `namePrefix` / `nameSuffix`, namespace overrides, image-tag swaps, label / annotation injection, and content-hashed `configMapGenerator` / `secretGenerator` (a ConfigMap value change auto-rolls the consuming Deployment because the resource name changed) — `kustomize build overlays/prod` produces deterministic concatenated YAML, `kubectl apply -k` ships it in-cluster** — `kustomize` (kubernetes-sigs, Apache-2.0, kustomize/v5.8.1) is the canonical choice when the manifests are simple enough that templating is overkill but per-environment variation is required (most internal services), and when GitOps tools (Argo CD `Application`, Flux `Kustomization`) need a manifest-rendering primitive they speak natively; pick over `helm` when you are *consuming* manifests for one team's services, not *publishing* a reusable component for outside consumers (helm is the right shape for distributable charts), over `jsonnet` / `tanka` / `cdk8s` when the variation across environments is small enough that overlays beat full programmatic generation, over `envsubst` for any non-trivial use; caveat — overlays are *patches* not full programmatic logic (looping over a list of services to generate manifests is awkward; pick `cdk8s` / `jsonnet` for that), the `kubectl`-bundled kustomize tracks behind the standalone CLI by 1-2 minor versions (use the standalone for newer features), and content-hashed generator names can confuse external tools that expect stable resource names (`disableNameSuffixHash: true` in the generator opts out, at the cost of losing the auto-roll behaviour) | [`kustomize`](clis/kustomize/) |
| Want **a single Rust binary that is the missing TOML toolchain — opinionated formatter (`taplo format` / `--check` for CI), semantic linter (duplicate keys, mixed-type arrays under strict mode, dotted-key collisions), JSON-Schema-aware validator (`taplo check` against an embedded schema store with up-to-date schemas for `Cargo.toml`, `pyproject.toml`, `rustfmt.toml`, `dprint.json`, `.cargo/config.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml` and dozens more, custom schemas via URL or path), and a Language Server (`taplo lsp stdio`) that powers the "Even Better TOML" VS Code extension and works in Helix / Neovim / Zed — same parser, same engine, same rules, so the editor diagnostic and the CI gate cannot drift** — `taplo` (tamasfe, MIT, 0.10.0) is the right answer when the repo accumulates `Cargo.toml`, `pyproject.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `.cargo/config.toml`, `pre-commit-hooks.toml`, agent / MCP server manifests in TOML, and the alternatives are partial (`dprint` formats but does not validate against schemas, `cargo fmt` only knows Rust source, IDE plugins format but do not run in CI); pick over [`dprint`](clis/dprint/) when you specifically need schema-aware *validation* not just formatting (dprint's `dprint-plugin-toml` actually uses taplo's parser under the hood — they compose: dprint as the unified multi-language formatter, taplo for the schema-validation pass), over `cargo fmt` / per-IDE-plugin formatters when one binary + one config + one CI gate beats a per-file-type patchwork, over hand-rolled `jq`-on-toml when the schema validation is the load-bearing part; caveat — opinionated formatter has fewer alignment knobs than `dprint-plugin-toml`'s full surface (column-perfect inline-table alignment will sometimes re-flow), schema-aware checks only catch what the schema declares (an out-of-date embedded schema lets invalid keys through silently — pin schema URLs in `taplo.toml` for reproducibility), the LSP does not resolve cross-file refs like `Cargo.toml` workspace inheritance (`workspace = true` does not "go to definition"), and MIT-only license (not the Rust dual MIT/Apache) is worth noting if your distribution policy requires Apache-2.0 | [`taplo`](clis/taplo/) |
| Want **a plain-text Zettelkasten-style notebook CLI that turns a directory of Markdown files into a queryable, link-aware knowledge base — `zk init` writes a `.zk/` SQLite index + `config.toml`; `zk new` creates a note from configurable Handlebars templates with timestamp / slug / ULID filenames and per-group templates (daily / meeting / literature / project) each with their own dir + body shape; `zk list` filters by tag (`--tag inbox,!archived`), link relationship (`--linked-by`, `--link-to`, `--orphan`, `--mentioned-by`), full-text FTS5 search (`--match "embedding model"`), date (`--created-after "last week"`), or path glob, with `--interactive` piping into fzf and a `--format` flag for piping paths / JSON / templated rows downstream; `zk graph --format json` dumps the full link graph for visualisation or LLM context-building; `zk lsp` is a Language Server that gives editors completion on `[[wiki links]]`, completion on `#tags`, hover preview of linked notes, "go to definition", and broken-link diagnostics — one Go binary, no daemon, no server, files stay plain Markdown on disk** — `zk` (zk-org, GPL-3.0, v0.15.3) is the practical middle between heavy proprietary PKM apps (Notion / Obsidian's `.obsidian` / Logseq's graph DB) and bare-Markdown directories with no tooling: notes stay plain Markdown in a git repo (so [`ripgrep`](clis/ripgrep/), [`fzf`](clis/fzf/), [`bat`](clis/bat/), every other tool in this catalog still works), but you get a real link graph, a real query language, and a real LSP — and the queryable on-disk shape is exactly what [`khoj`](clis/khoj/), [`fabric`](clis/fabric/), [`files-to-prompt`](clis/files-to-prompt/), [`repomix`](clis/repomix/), and [`code2prompt`](clis/code2prompt/) need to slurp in as LLM context (`zk graph --format json` plus `zk list --linked-by current-note --format path` give an agent the "what does this note transitively touch" view without parsing Markdown by hand); pick over Obsidian / Logseq when you want the notes in a git-friendly plain-Markdown shape with no proprietary database (and no GUI app in the loop), over a bare directory + `grep` when the link graph + tag query + LSP completion on wiki-links pay back the `zk init` step, over [`khoj`](clis/khoj/) when the question is the *substrate* not the *RAG / chat layer* (they compose — point khoj at a zk notebook); caveat — GPL-3.0 is the strictest license in this catalog (linking as a library into a closed-source product is a non-starter; the CLI as an external process is fine), no built-in sync (you git-commit / Syncthing / iCloud the directory yourself — feature for terminal-native users, hard cliff for "open the iOS app and the note appears"), the LSP needs editor configuration (works out of the box in Neovim with `nvim-lspconfig`, manual server entry in Helix's `languages.toml`, ships as a VS Code extension), Handlebars filename templates have a learning curve, and single-user by design (multi-author notebooks work via git but no merge-conflict-aware UI) | [`zk`](clis/zk/) |
| Want **a Conventional-Commits-native git toolkit in one Rust binary — `cog install-hook commit-msg` drops a `commit-msg` hook that rejects any commit whose subject does not match the Conventional Commits grammar (`<type>(<scope>)!: <description>`); `cog commit feat auth "add OAuth flow"` is the assisted-author path with prompts for body and footers; `cog log --breaking-changes` filters `git log` by Conventional Commit type / scope / breaking-change marker; `cog bump --auto` is the release verb that scans commits since the last tag, computes the next semver (major if any `!:` or `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer, minor if any `feat`, patch otherwise), regenerates `CHANGELOG.md` from a Tera template, creates the annotated/signed tag, and runs configured `pre_bump_hooks` / `post_bump_hooks` (e.g. update `Cargo.toml` version, run tests, `cargo publish`, push to remote); monorepo-aware via `[packages]` blocks that scope each package to a path and version it independently; `cog check` is the CI-side enforcement that walks a PR's commit range and fails on non-conforming commits — the canonical "PR-gates-conventional-commits, merge-creates-release" loop ships as a GitHub Action** — `cocogitto` (cocogitto, MIT, 7.0.0, binary name `cog`) is the Rust answer to the patchwork of Node tools (`commitlint` + `commitizen` + `standard-version` + `semantic-release`) that collectively implement Conventional Commits + auto-versioning; pick over the Node patchwork for the one-binary install + one TOML config + Rust startup speed, over [`git-cliff`](clis/git-cliff/) when you want the *full* enforcement-and-release loop in one tool not just the changelog (they compose — git-cliff ships a Cocogitto preset for exactly this reason; pick git-cliff alone when you only want the changelog and already have a release flow, pick cocogitto when you also want the commit-msg hook + CI gate + bump verb), over `release-please` / `semantic-release` for the lighter Rust-binary footprint without a Node runtime in CI, pairs with [`gptcommit`](clis/gptcommit/) / [`opencommit`](clis/opencommit/) (LLM-generated Conventional Commits at write time get validated by cog's hook); caveat — opinionated workflow ("Conventional Commits → semver → tag → push"); if you need calendar versioning, train-based releases, or release-please-style PR-driven versioning, cog is not the fit; the `commit-msg` hook is bypassable with `git commit --no-verify` so the `cog check` CI gate is the actual enforcement (both are needed); monorepo support exists but is less mature than the single-package path (complex per-package release workflows may still want `release-please` or a custom script); MIT-only license (vs. the dual MIT/Apache common in the Rust ecosystem) | [`cocogitto`](clis/cocogitto/) |
| Want **`curl` for gRPC services — a single Go binary that uses gRPC server reflection (or a local `.proto` / protoset file) to discover services, methods, and message shapes at call time, then invokes any unary or streaming RPC with a JSON request body and prints JSON responses, with first-class TLS / mTLS / bearer-token / custom-metadata / gzip / Unix-socket support — no codegen, no language runtime, no SDK install** — `grpcurl` (fullstorydev, MIT, v1.9.3) is the on-call answer to "this thing speaks gRPC, prove it works": `grpcurl -plaintext localhost:50051 list` enumerates services, `grpcurl -d '{"name":"world"}' host:port pkg.Service/Method` is the call, server-streaming RPCs print one JSON frame per server message, client-streaming / bidi accept newline-delimited JSON on stdin (`echo -e '{"a":1}\n{"a":2}' | grpcurl -d @ ...`), `grpc.health.v1.Health/Check` is the canonical "is it up" probe; pick over hand-generated stubs when the question is one ad-hoc call from a shell or a runbook (keep the generated client for production code paths), over [`hurl`](clis/hurl/) which is HTTP-only — they pair (use hurl for the REST half of a service, grpcurl for the gRPC half), over [`curlie`](clis/curlie/) / `curl` which cannot frame gRPC payloads, over GUI gRPC clients when you want a piped output you can `jq` (`grpcurl ... | jq '.items[] | select(.status=="ACTIVE")'`); caveat — server reflection is often disabled in production for surface-area reasons (workaround: ship a compiled `--protoset` or `--proto` directory), `-plaintext` disables TLS entirely (the most common copy-paste mistake; for self-signed staging certs prefer `-insecure` so the wire is still encrypted), JSON encoding follows the canonical protobuf-JSON mapping (int64/uint64 emitted as JSON strings, bytes as base64, well-known-types as RFC 3339 / standard JSON shapes — surprises if you diff wire bytes), bidi over stdin is line-delimited JSON not protobuf framing (you cannot replay a captured wire dump directly), and one call per invocation means it is a debug tool not a load tester — pair with [`k6`](clis/k6/)'s `k6/net/grpc` module for sustained gRPC load | [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) |
| Want **`netcat` / `curl` for WebSockets — a single Rust binary that connects to `ws://` / `wss://` endpoints from a shell, with stdin going out as WebSocket frames and incoming frames coming to stdout, plus a listening server mode (`websocat -s 8080`) so the same binary debugs a remote endpoint and stands up a local mock; URL-scheme composition (`tcp:`, `tcp-l:`, `unix:`, `unix-l:`, `cmd:`, `exec:`, `mirror:`, `broadcast:`, `autoreconnect:`) lets non-trivial topologies (TCP-to-WS reverse proxy, broadcast fan-out, per-message shell handler) ship as one-line invocations** — `websocat` (vi, MIT, v1.14.1) is the shell-native primitive for the realtime layer: interactive client is `websocat wss://api/feed`, one-shot smoke test is `echo '{"op":"sub"}' \| websocat -n1 wss://api/feed \| jq .`, sustained connection through corporate proxies wants `--ping-interval 15 --ping-timeout 5`, replay a captured session with `websocat ... < frames.ndjson`, and `websocat -s 8080` is a working WebSocket echo server in one command for browser-side dev when the backend isn't ready; pick over browser DevTools' WebSocket inspector when the work is a CI smoke test, a runbook step, or an SSH-from-bastion ad-hoc check (DevTools is read-mostly; websocat scripts), over `wscat` (Node) for the no-runtime single-binary install + URL-scheme composition + bidirectional splice that wscat does not have, over [`hurl`](clis/hurl/) / [`curlie`](clis/curlie/) / `curl` which do not speak WebSocket frames at all (use hurl / xh for the REST handshake that issues the auth token, then pipe the token into websocat for the realtime channel), over [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) which is the same shell-shaped tool for the *other* main long-lived bidirectional protocol (HTTP/2 + gRPC framing); caveat — default sends *text* frames, `--binary` switches the whole session (mixed text+binary in one connection needs `multimessage:` overlays), `--insecure` skips TLS verification (fine for self-signed staging, foot-gun in committed scripts), no automatic JSON pretty-print on receive (pipe through `jq`), `--long-help` (not `--help`) lists every URL-scheme overlay because the design is small composable primitives not one big config object, and one process == one machine — for sustained WebSocket load reach for [`k6`](clis/k6/)'s `k6/ws` module | [`websocat`](clis/websocat/) |
| Want **a scriptable load tester for HTTP / gRPC / WebSocket / browser workloads where the test is a JavaScript file (embedded `goja` runtime — no Node, no `npm install`), the load profile is declarative (constant rate, ramping, spike, scenarios with explicit closed-model VU executors *and* open-model `constant-arrival-rate` / `ramping-arrival-rate` so a slow server cannot quietly suppress your RPS the way `wrk` does), and pass / fail is a `thresholds` block (`http_req_duration: ['p(95)<500']`, `http_req_failed: ['rate<0.01']`) that makes the binary's exit code a CI gate** — `k6` (grafana, AGPL-3.0, v1.7.1) is the modern shape-of-load-test for service-level perf: write `script.js` with a `default function` that one virtual user runs in a loop, declare `options.stages` for ramp-up/hold/ramp-down or `options.scenarios` for arrival-rate, run `k6 run script.js`, watch the percentile summary, fail CI on threshold breach; protocol coverage is `k6/http` (HTTP/1.1 + HTTP/2), `k6/ws` (WebSocket), `k6/net/grpc` (gRPC unary + streaming), `k6/browser` (real Chromium per VU for end-to-end frontend perf — low density, high fidelity); metrics stream to Prometheus / InfluxDB / Grafana Cloud with `-o`, machine-readable summary with `--summary-export=summary.json`; pick over `wrk` / `ab` / `siege` when the test needs login + token + multi-step flows + assertions instead of a fixed URL hammered N times (those tools are right for "hit one URL"), over [`hyperfine`](clis/hyperfine/) when the question is "service under concurrent load" not "command N runs" (different scope — hyperfine for CLI / batch, k6 for network services), over [`hurl`](clis/hurl/) which is single-user functional contract testing (they pair — hurl on every PR for correctness, k6 nightly for performance), over [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) / [`websocat`](clis/websocat/) which are single-call debug tools (k6 is the sustained-load counterpart on the same protocols); caveat — **AGPL-3.0** is fine for using the binary against your own services from CI but has implications if you embed k6's source into a product or offer a modified k6 as a hosted service (read the licence; most teams never touch this), the JS runtime is `goja` not Node so no `npm install` / no `require('fs')` / no native modules (you get k6's stdlib + xk6 extensions), single-process VU concurrency tops out in the tens of thousands on one box (past that you need the OSS `k6 operator` for Kubernetes or hosted k6), the `k6/browser` module launches real Chromium per VU (use it for handfuls not hundreds), and the human-pretty terminal summary is *not* the machine-pretty schema — for CI dashboards always use `--summary-export=summary.json` or stream to Prometheus | [`k6`](clis/k6/) |
| Want **a multi-engine duplicate / waste-file finder in one Rust binary that goes beyond byte-exact dedup — `czkawka_cli dup` runs an XXH3-then-Blake3 hash ladder for exact duplicates with a per-tool on-disk cache (`~/.cache/czkawka/`) keyed by path+size+mtime so a daily re-scan of a 4 TB photo dir is 10-50× faster than the first pass; `czkawka_cli image` perceptually hashes via Mean / Gradient / DCT / Blockhash / VertGradient at configurable hash sizes (8–64 bits) and similarity presets (Minimal / Small / Medium / High) so a photo at 4032×3024 and the same photo at 1920×1080 land in the same group; `czkawka_cli music` does both audiotag-comparison and chromaprint-style fingerprint mode for "same song different bitrate"; same `--excluded-directories` / `--reference-directories` / `--file-to-save` / `--delete-method` flags work across `dup` / `image` / `videos` / `music` / `big` / `empty-folders` / `empty-files` / `symlinks` / `temporary` / `extension` / `broken` scanners; ships a GTK4 GUI (`czkawka_gui`) and a Slint-based GUI (`krokiet`) sharing the core engine for the click-driven review-then-delete step** — `czkawka` (qarmin, MIT for code + CC-BY-4.0 for icons, v11.0.1) is what to reach for when a 4 TB photo / project / download dir has accumulated years of duplicates and near-duplicates and the existing options are unsatisfying (`fdupes` only finds byte-exact, `findimagedupes` is Perl with a heavy install, `dupeGuru` is GUI-only Python-Qt, `Gemini` is macOS-only and proprietary); pick over `fdupes` / `rdfind` / `jdupes` for the perceptual-image + audio-fingerprint + video-frame-fingerprint modes the older byte-exact tools cannot do (czkawka matches their speed on the byte-exact path via the XXH3 ladder, then adds the fuzzy modes), over `dupeGuru` for the CLI-first scriptable workflow (then drop into the GUI for the review step against the same scan reports), over [`dust`](clis/dust/) which tells you *where* the disk is going (czkawka tells you *which specific files to delete to get it back* — common sequence: `dust ~` highlights the fattest subtree, `czkawka_cli dup -d <subtree>` quantifies how much is recoverable duplicate weight), over [`ouch`](clis/ouch/) which is orthogonal (run czkawka first to remove duplicates, then `ouch compress` the cleaner result for archival); caveat — `--delete-method aen` (all except newest) is destructive and per-group with no global "preview deletions" flag (use `--file-to-save report.txt` first then `xargs rm -i`, and `--reference-directories` to force a specific tree to be source-of-truth), perceptual image hashing is heuristic so `Small` catches resized / re-encoded photos but `High` clusters unrelated photos with similar composition (always review the report before deleting; the GUI is much faster than the CLI for this step), audio fingerprint mode needs `libchromaprint` at runtime on Linux (`apt install libchromaprint1`; the `tags` mode has no native dep), cache invalidation is path+size+mtime not content (a process that mutates content while preserving size+mtime gives stale cached hashes — `rm -rf ~/.cache/czkawka/` to force a full re-scan if you suspect this), GTK4 GUI install footprint is large on macOS (CLI is fine cross-platform; consider the lighter Slint-based `krokiet` if the GTK4 dep matters), and icons are CC-BY-4.0 not MIT (only matters if you redistribute the GUI's icon set inside another product) | [`czkawka`](clis/czkawka/) |
| Want **one terminal emulator binary configured by a Lua file (`~/.config/wezterm/wezterm.lua`) that is the *same* terminal on macOS / Linux / Windows / FreeBSD with the same keybinds, same colour scheme, same font shaping, and the same SSH-as-domain story so `wezterm ssh user@host` reuses every local Lua keybind and OSC 7 / OSC 133 cwd / prompt-marking handler on the remote pane — built-in multiplexer (panes + tabs + named workspaces + detachable mux sessions) so `tmux` / `zellij` is optional, GPU renderer (Metal / Vulkan / D3D11 / WebGPU) fast enough for high-refresh ligature shaping through Harfbuzz, image protocols (iTerm2 + Sixel + Kitty) all supported so `chafa` / `viu` / `imgcat` work everywhere, colour-emoji and CJK IME native on every OS, and a programmable event system (`wezterm.on('format-tab-title', …)`, `wezterm.on('user-var-changed', …)`, `wezterm.on('open-uri', …)`) where any keybind / tab title / status widget is a Lua callback that inspects the current pane's title / cwd / process and decides what to do** — `wezterm` (wez, MIT, 20240203-110809-5046fc22) is the only mainstream terminal that ships *one binary* running natively on macOS / Linux / Windows / FreeBSD with *one Lua config* in dotfiles, *one built-in multiplexer*, and *first-class SSH-as-a-domain*; pick over Alacritty when you want the multiplexer + Lua programmability + image protocols in the same binary (Alacritty wins on lowest install footprint and YAML-config simplicity if you don't need any of those), over Kitty when you want native Windows + macOS support and Lua over Kitty's custom DSL (Kitty wins on the `kittens` plugin ecosystem and macOS-only ligature polish), over iTerm2 / Windows Terminal when you refuse to learn three different terminals across three OSes, over `foot` / `st` when "cross-platform + multiplexer + image + IME" beats minimalist install footprint; pairs with [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) (run zellij *inside* wezterm if you live in multiplexer plugins — wezterm's built-in mux covers splits / tabs / workspaces / detach but the plugin ecosystem is far smaller than tmux's TPM or zellij's plugin system), with [`helix`](clis/helix/) and [`nushell`](clis/nushell/) (a common modern stack is `wezterm` → `nushell` → `helix`, all dotfiles, all cross-platform — wezterm's `user-var-changed` hook composes with shell prompts that emit OSC 1337 user vars for a reactive status bar); caveat — version number is a *date stamp* not semver (latest stable is `20240203-110809-5046fc22`; pin to a specific date-tag in CI, `latest` will move), install footprint is large (~80 MB macOS app bundle with bundled fonts + icons, ~30 MB Linux binary — normal for a GPU terminal with embedded Lua + multiplexer but if you want a 2 MB single-binary terminal pick `foot` or stay on Alacritty), Lua config means a typo in `wezterm.lua` falls back to defaults with a notification (develop config edits in a side window via `wezterm --config-file /tmp/test.lua` before promoting), GPU acceleration occasionally trips driver bugs on older Intel iGPUs / certain Wayland compositors (set `front_end = 'Software'` in Lua to force the CPU renderer — slower but bulletproof), and OSC 7 cwd-tracking + OSC 133 prompt-marking + jump-to-prev-prompt require a small shell-side prompt snippet from the upstream integration docs to enable (without it wezterm degrades gracefully but the killer keybinds don't light up) | [`wezterm`](clis/wezterm/) |
| Want **one Earthfile per repo declaring named build targets (`+build`, `+test`, `+lint`, `+ci`, `+docker`, `+deploy`) in Dockerfile syntax (`FROM`, `COPY`, `RUN`, `ARG`, `ENV`) plus Makefile-like target composition (`BUILD +foo`, `FROM +base`, `COPY +artifact-target/file ./`, `WAIT/END` for parallel blocks, `LOCALLY` for steps that must escape the container) executed inside hermetic BuildKit containers with explicit declared inputs / outputs (`SAVE ARTIFACT`, `SAVE IMAGE`) and content-addressable cache reuse — so `earthly +ci` runs identically on a developer laptop, in GitHub Actions, in GitLab CI, in Buildkite, and in Jenkins with no per-CI YAML rewrite; reproducing a CI failure locally is one command instead of 40 minutes of installing the right images and exporting the right env vars; multi-platform builds (`--platform=linux/amd64,linux/arm64`) share the cache where layers are identical; remote satellites (`earthly sat select prod-sat`) give CI the warm-cache benefit of a long-running shared BuildKit daemon without each developer running their own** — `earthly` (earthly, MPL-2.0, v0.8.16) collapses the four-layer status quo (`Dockerfile` for image build + `Makefile` for target deps + per-CI YAML for CI orchestration + `scripts/build.sh` for the steps that work locally but not in CI, and vice versa) into one declarative file with Dockerfile-shaped syntax for "what to run" and Makefile-shaped semantics for "how targets depend on each other," all backed by BuildKit for hermeticity and CAS-based cache; pick over plain `Dockerfile` when you also need to express target dependencies and save artifacts back to the host (Dockerfiles can't), over `Makefile` when you also want hermeticity (`make test` depends on what's installed on the host; `earthly +test` doesn't), over `bazel` / `pants` when you want Dockerfile-familiarity over a full BUILD-system adoption commitment with its own dep declaration language (bazel wins for pure code-graph-aware multi-language monorepos at FAANG scale; earthly wins for "I want CI/laptop equivalence and cache reuse without a six-month migration"), over per-CI YAML files when "the same command runs everywhere" is the load-bearing requirement; pairs with [`task`](clis/task/) / [`just`](clis/just/) — different layer (task / just orchestrate shell commands on the host with no hermeticity; earthly runs hermetic build steps; common shape is a `Taskfile.yml` recipe that calls `earthly +ci` for the heavy build with `task` as a thin wrapper for ad-hoc developer commands), with [`devbox`](clis/devbox/) / Nix (devbox / Nix give hermetic *dev environments*; earthly gives hermetic *build steps* — common pairing is devbox for the dev shell that pins `earthly` + `docker` + language toolchains, then `earthly +ci` inside it), with [`kustomize`](clis/kustomize/) (earthly *builds* the artifact, kustomize *deploys* it — typical pipeline is `earthly --push +docker` → `kustomize edit set image` → `kubectl apply -k overlays/prod`); caveat — requires Docker or Podman on the host (BuildKit runs as a container; for environments that don't already have a container runtime this is a real install dependency), MPL-2.0 file-level copyleft (using the binary in CI / dev is fine; modifying Earthly source files and redistributing requires releasing the modified files under MPL — most teams never touch this), Earthly Cloud / Satellites are a paid commercial service (the OSS binary is fully functional without them — satellites are a convenience layer for warm-cache CI; you can also self-host a remote BuildKit cache via `--remote-cache` for free), the DSL has its own keywords to learn (`SAVE ARTIFACT`, `SAVE IMAGE`, `BUILD +x`, `WAIT/END`, `LOCALLY`, `PROJECT`, `VERSION` — half-day learning curve for someone comfortable with Dockerfiles; migration of existing CI is the bigger time sink), cache is per-machine unless you adopt satellites or a remote BuildKit cache (laptop and a fresh CI runner have separate caches), and pin `VERSION 0.8` at the top of every Earthfile (the DSL contract is stable within 0.8.x; major-version bumps have historically required minor edits) | [`earthly`](clis/earthly/) |
| Want **a ranger-clone TUI file manager in one ~6 MB Rust binary — three-pane Miller-columns view (parent / current / preview), vim keymap (`hjkl` / `gg` / `G` / `dd` / `yy` / `pp` / `:` / `/`) faithful enough that ranger muscle memory works on day one, async directory loading so a `cd` into a 100k-entry tree doesn't freeze the UI, four small TOML files (`joshuto.toml`, `keymap.toml`, `mimetype.toml`, `theme.toml`) instead of ranger's `~/.config/ranger/plugins/` Python sprawl, image preview wired through whatever the host terminal supports (kitty graphics / sixel / `chafa`) via a `preview_script` shim, and a `--output-file` flag that writes the cursor path on exit so a shell wrapper can `cd` to it — drop the binary on a fresh box via `brew install joshuto` and your fingers already work, no `pip install ranger` in the bootstrap script** — `joshuto` (kamiyaa, LGPL-3.0, v0.9.9) is what to reach for when you want the ranger UX without the Python runtime; pick over `ranger` for the single-binary install + async I/O default + no plugin runtime to babysit, over [`yazi`](clis/yazi/) when you want the smaller ranger-faithful tool without yazi's Lua plugin surface (yazi wins for power and built-in image preview that "just works"; joshuto wins for "ranger but Rust"), over [`broot`](clis/broot/) when you want a *full TUI file manager* not a *fuzzy tree-finder* (broot is one collapsing tree for fast cd; joshuto is Miller columns for a working session), over [`xplr`](clis/xplr/) when you want declarative TOML keymaps over Lua-message-driven hackability (xplr lets you script the file manager itself; joshuto is a finished tool); pairs with [`zoxide`](clis/zoxide/) (`z foo` to jump, `j` to browse — the canonical "fast jump + visual confirm" loop), with [`bat`](clis/bat/) / [`fd`](clis/fd/) / [`ripgrep`](clis/ripgrep/) for previews and shell-out, and with LLM-CLI workflows that need to *visually* stage files into [`files-to-prompt`](clis/files-to-prompt/) or [`repomix`](clis/repomix/) (toggle-select a subtree, exit with the selection list, pipe into the packer); caveat — smaller feature set than ranger / yazi (no built-in tagging, no archive mounting, no per-mime command menu beyond `mimetype.toml`), image preview is *not* bundled (you wire `preview_script` to `chafa` / `viu` / kitty `icat` yourself; on a stock SSH session expect text previews only), LGPL-3.0 not MIT/Apache (binary redistribution is fine; closed-source forks of the codebase owe LGPL obligations on the modified joshuto portion), config is split across four TOML files under `~/.config/joshuto/` (the first reach for "where do I rebind a key" will guess wrong once — answer is `keymap.toml`), and project velocity is one-maintainer slow (releases land every few months, fine for a stable file manager) | [`joshuto`](clis/joshuto/) |
| Want **a Python project + dependency manager that reads / writes the same standards-track `pyproject.toml` everyone else does (PEP 517 / 518 / 621 — the `[project]` table is the source of truth, no proprietary `[tool.pdm]`-only metadata block) with a cross-platform lockfile (`pdm.lock` carries one resolution graph with per-platform PEP 508 markers, so the same lockfile works on Linux + macOS + Windows + CPython + PyPy without re-resolving per OS), a fast SAT-style backtracking resolver (`pdm config use_uv true` delegates resolution to [`uv`](clis/uv/) when the cold-cache speed gap matters), `[tool.pdm.scripts]` for named tasks with composition / pre/post hooks (one less reason to drag `task` / `just` into a pure-Python repo), and a small plugin API (`pdm-plugin-*`) that is the most extensible of the `pyproject.toml`-native managers — install via `pipx install pdm`, `pdm init` / `pdm add` / `pdm run` / `pdm build` / `pdm publish` is the full lifecycle, no `source venv/bin/activate` ceremony** — `pdm` (pdm-project, MIT, v2.26.8) is the right answer when you want `poetry`'s "one tool, one config, one lockfile" workflow without `poetry`'s proprietary `[tool.poetry]` block and history of resolver pain — adherence to PEPs over invention, so moving off pdm tomorrow leaves your manifest readable by every other PEP-621 tool; pick over [`uv`](clis/uv/) when you want the *project lifecycle* (init / add / run / build / publish + plugin API) not just the installer (uv is faster at install + resolve and now eats most of pip's mindshare; many shops use `uv` as the resolver behind `pdm`'s frontend via `use_uv true`), over `poetry` for the standards-compliant `[project]` table + cross-platform lockfile + lighter plugin API, over [`rye`](clis/rye/) when you want configurability over rye's "one way" opinionation and don't need rye's bundled CPython downloader (rye is now a thin shim over uv upstream; pdm remains independently maintained), over `pip` + `pip-tools` for the cross-platform single-lockfile + integrated task runner; caveat — resolver still slower than uv on huge graphs (a 100+ pkg monorepo locks 2-5× faster on uv; the `use_uv` flag closes it but you carry two tools), lockfile format has churned across major versions (2.x lockfiles aren't 1.x-compatible — pin pdm version in CI alongside Python), the original PEP 582 (`__pypackages__/`) flagship is now deprecated (default install creates a normal `.venv/`; do not adopt PEP 582 for new projects), plugin ecosystem is smaller than poetry's (mainstream stuff works; long-tail integrations sometimes only exist for poetry), and `pdm init` asks which build backend (`pdm-backend` / `hatchling` / `setuptools` / `flit-core`) — the answer matters for sdist layout (`pdm-backend` for self-consistent stack, `hatchling` for maximum portability) | [`pdm`](clis/pdm/) |
| Want **a Conventional-Commits Swiss-army knife in one ~5 MB Rust binary that owns the full lifecycle — `convco commit` interactively writes a fully-formed Conventional Commit (type / scope / subject / body / breaking-change / footer prompts then `git commit`), `convco check` lints messages from stdin or a git rev range (`convco check origin/main..HEAD` is the one-line PR gate; `convco check --from-stdin` is the one-line `commit-msg` hook), `convco version --bump` parses commit history since the last tag and prints the next semver (major if any `feat!` or `BREAKING CHANGE:`, minor if any `feat`, patch otherwise), `convco changelog` walks `git log` and emits a Keep-a-Changelog-shaped CHANGELOG.md grouped by type — all configured by one `.versionrc` file (JSON-shaped, deliberately mirrors `standard-version` for mechanical migration off the JS stack)** — `convco` (convco, MIT, v0.6.3) is the right pick when you want the *full* Conventional Commits lifecycle (write + lint + version + changelog) in one binary instead of the Node-native chain (`commitizen` + `commitlint` + `husky` + `standard-version` + `conventional-changelog`) and its `package.json` + lockfile + Node-version-pin tax in CI; pick over [`cocogitto`](clis/cocogitto/) when you want a calculator + linter you can drop into an existing release flow (cocogitto is more opinionated — enforces a workflow with monorepo packages model and signed tags; convco is lighter-touch with `.versionrc` mirroring `standard-version`), over [`git-cliff`](clis/git-cliff/) when you want lint + version + default-changelog in one tool (git-cliff is *only* the changelog generator and the best one — Tera template engine for arbitrary output formats; pick git-cliff when the changelog format is the deliverable, convco when the full lifecycle is), over the JS reference stack for the one-binary install + no Node runtime + faster startup; pairs with [`gptcommit`](clis/gptcommit/) / [`opencommit`](clis/opencommit/) / [`aicommits`](clis/aicommits/) (LLM authors the message from the diff, `convco check --from-stdin` validates against the grammar, human approves — clean LLM + deterministic-validator handoff);
