caveat — Conventional Commits or nothing (a repo mixing conventions makes `convco check` a noise machine; either commit fully and enforce in CI or pick a different tool), pre-1.0 with small breaking changes across 0.x minors (changelog footer rendering and breaking-change emoji have shifted — pin convco version in CI and review the diff on upgrade), no monorepo per-package SemVer out of the box (`convco version` reasons about the whole repo, not "this package vs that package within the repo" — for per-package versioning reach for cocogitto's packages mode or `release-please`), no built-in commit signing or release publishing (computes the next version + writes the changelog; the `git tag -s` and `git push --tags` are your shell loop's job), and `.versionrc` is JSON not TOML (deliberate for `standard-version` migration compatibility — minor papercut for a Rust tool) | [`convco`](clis/convco/) |
| Want **a thirty-year-old Norton-Commander-style two-pane terminal file manager that ships in every Linux distro repo (and `brew install midnight-commander` on macOS) and "just works" on a fresh server with zero config — function-key hint row across the bottom (`F3` view, `F4` `mcedit`, `F5` copy, `F6` move, `F7` mkdir, `F8` delete, `F9` menu, `F10` quit), transparent VFS so `cd` into a `.tar.gz` / `.zip` / `.iso` / `sh://user@host/path` (SFTP) / `ftp://...` URL works as a regular directory and you can copy files *out* of an archive into the other pane with one `F5`, built-in viewer + hex editor + diff, per-directory user menu (`F2`) of shell scriptlets the team triggers from one keystroke, and a real subshell living under the panels (`Ctrl+O` toggles full-screen shell, `Ctrl+Enter` pastes the highlighted filename onto the command line) — install once with the distro package manager and your fingers already work on every box you SSH into for the next decade** — `mc` (MidnightCommander, GPL-3.0-or-later, 4.8.33) is the on-ramp every shell newcomer learns first and still the right answer for triage on a fresh server where the only installed thing is the distro default toolset; pick over [`yazi`](clis/yazi/) / [`joshuto`](clis/joshuto/) / [`xplr`](clis/xplr/) / `ranger` when you want the *universally installed* tool with a thirty-year keymap that every sysadmin already knows (the modern Rust file managers win for image preview and async I/O on a personal box; mc wins for "SSH into a stranger's RHEL 7 box and it's already there"), over [`broot`](clis/broot/) when you want the full two-pane FM not the one-pane fuzzy-tree (broot for fast `cd`, mc for a working session moving files between two trees), over `nnn` for the built-in editor + viewer + VFS into archives without plugin scaffolding (nnn is more minimal and faster but punts archive browsing to plugins; mc is batteries-included), over GUI file managers when the work is on a remote host (mc is the "Total Commander on the SSH session" answer); pairs with `tmux` / [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) (mc in one pane, shell + log tails in others), with `ssh` / [`mosh`](clis/mosh/) (mc-over-mosh on a flaky link is the original "remote file management that survives a roaming laptop"), and with [`fzf`](clis/fzf/) (`Esc` `Esc` to drop into the subshell, `**<Tab>`-style fuzzy completion, then `Ctrl+Enter` to bring the picked path back into mc); caveat — keymap is from the Norton Commander era (function keys, not vim — the muscle memory is a different stack than `hjkl`-native managers; for vim-keymap pick `yazi` / `joshuto` / `xplr` instead), GPL-3.0 not MIT/Apache (binary distribution fine; closed-source forks owe GPL obligations on the modified mc portion), no built-in image preview (text + hex viewer only — for a graphical preview lane on a kitty/iTerm2 terminal pick `yazi`), the `mcedit` editor is fully featured but is *not* `vim`/`emacs` (one more keymap to learn or stay disciplined and edit in `$EDITOR` via `F4`'s "internal editor: no" config), VFS into archives is read+write but slow for very large tarballs (extract first for multi-GB archives), and the Function-Key hint row collides with terminal multiplexer prefix keys (rebind `tmux` from `Ctrl+b` away from anything mc uses, or live with `Esc-N` numeric escapes for the F-keys) | [`mc`](clis/mc/) |
| Want **a fast configuration-file-driven Markdown linter that ships ~50 rules covering heading hierarchy / list consistency / trailing whitespace + line length / code-fence style / broken or inconsistent links / HTML usage in Markdown, with `.markdownlint-cli2.jsonc` / `.yaml` / `.cjs` config so rule selection + glob includes/excludes + custom rules live next to the source, `--fix` for the deterministic-fix tail (trailing whitespace, list marker normalization, blank-line surrounds), first-class `.gitignore` honouring (`gitignore: true`) so CI runs match local dev, and a GitHub Action + `pre-commit` hook + Docker image + the same VS Code "markdownlint" extension all using *one* engine so editor diagnostics and CI gate cannot drift — install via `npm i -g markdownlint-cli2` (or `pnpm` / `bun add -g`), then `markdownlint-cli2 "**/*.md"` is the one-line repo gate** — `markdownlint-cli2` (DavidAnson, MIT, v0.22.1) is the right answer when the docs tree has grown past "obvious typos" into "needs a style gate that runs in CI and matches what the editor underlines"; pick over the older `markdownlint-cli` (now in maintenance mode — cli2 is the active project from the same author, faster, configuration-file-first instead of CLI-flag-first, and the GitHub Action / Docker image / VS Code extension all converge on cli2), over [`vale`](https://vale.sh) when the lint domain is *Markdown structure* not *English prose* (vale is the prose-style linter — passive voice, weasel words, vocab — pick *both* if both layers matter; markdownlint guards the Markdown grammar, vale guards the English), over [`prettier`](https://prettier.io) when you want *rules that fail the build* not *autoformat-on-save* (prettier reformats Markdown to one canonical shape and is the right tool for that — markdownlint enforces semantic rules prettier doesn't have opinions on like heading hierarchy depth or duplicate headings, run both: prettier formats, markdownlint lints), over `remark-lint` when you want the configuration-first single-binary CLI without standing up a `unified` plugin pipeline (remark gives you a programmable AST and is right when *you* are the linter author; markdownlint-cli2 is right when you are the linter user); pairs with [`prettier`](https://prettier.io) (different layer — formatting), with `lefthook` / `pre-commit` / `husky` (one-line hook config), and with the docs-as-code workflow where `mkdocs` / `docusaurus` / `astro` builds the site and markdownlint-cli2 gates the PRs; caveat — Node runtime required (one of the few JS-stack tools in this list — the value is the shared engine with the VS Code extension and the GitHub Action; if a no-Node policy is firm pick the older Go-port `mdl` and accept the smaller rule set), `--fix` is conservative by design (line-length wraps, complex list reflows, semantic rewrites are not auto-fixed — those still need human edits), the rule numbers (`MD001` … `MD059`) are the lingua franca but the human-readable rule names (`heading-increment`, `no-trailing-spaces`) are the readable form — use both in `.markdownlint-cli2.jsonc` config and PR comments, custom rules are JS modules loaded via `customRules:` (real flexibility but you now own a small JS dep), and config-file precedence is *nearest-ancestor wins* across a monorepo (per-package `.markdownlint-cli2.jsonc` overrides repo root — verify with `--config` if a subtree behaves unexpectedly) | [`markdownlint-cli2`](clis/markdownlint-cli2/) |
| Want **a secure modern successor to the original `dotenv` (by the same author) that makes a `.env` file safe to commit by *encrypting* the values with a public key while the matching private key lives in `.env.keys` outside git — `dotenvx run -- node index.js` (or `python app.py`, `cargo run`, `go run .`, any other process) loads + decrypts + injects envs at exec time so application code drops the `require('dotenv').config()` / `python-dotenv` import entirely, multi-environment overlays (`.env.production` + per-env encrypted overlays + explicit precedence rules: file order, `--overload` / `--no-override` semantics), `dotenvx encrypt` rotates the encryption to a fresh keypair in one command, `dotenvx decrypt` round-trips for local debugging, and a single static binary built with Bun / pkg for every major OS / arch (no Node runtime required at install or at runtime) — install via `curl -fsS https://dotenvx.sh | sh` or `brew install dotenvx/brew/dotenvx`, then `dotenvx run -f .env.production -- ./server` is the production exec line** — `dotenvx` (dotenvx, BSD-3-Clause, v1.64.0) is the right shape for a polyglot repo where one secret-handling story across Node + Python + Rust + Go + shell beats one `dotenv`-clone per language and where the deployment target (small VPS / Render / Fly.io / a Docker image) does not justify a full secret-manager round trip; pick over the original `dotenv` (the same author retiring `dotenv` toward `dotenvx` — encrypted-`.env`-in-git is the marquee feature `dotenv` does not have, plus the language-agnostic exec wrapper means `python-dotenv` / `godotenv` / `dotenv-rs` all become unnecessary), over Doppler / Infisical / 1Password Connect / AWS Secrets Manager / Vault when the project is *one repo, two or three environments, one or two operators* and the vendor SaaS dependency is overkill (the heavyweight secret managers are the right answer at team / org scale with rotation policies, audit logs, and dynamic credentials — dotenvx is the right answer at "indie / small-team / one-machine-deploy" scale), over `direnv` when the goal is *production secret loading* not *per-directory dev shell envs* (direnv is for `cd $project && nix develop`-style local shells; dotenvx is for `dotenvx run -- ./server` in production); pairs with [`sops`](https://github.com/getsops/sops) (different shape — sops encrypts arbitrary YAML/JSON with KMS / age / PGP backends, dotenvx is `.env`-shaped and uses its own ECC keypair; use sops when you need cloud-KMS-backed key custody, dotenvx when one keypair file is the right granularity), with `lefthook` / `pre-commit` (gate that no plaintext `.env` ever gets committed — `dotenvx ls` + `git diff --cached` check), and with Render / Fly / Docker (`dotenvx run` as the container `ENTRYPOINT` wrapper); caveat — encryption is only as good as `.env.keys` custody (lose the keys file and the encrypted `.env` is gibberish; leak it and the secrets are plaintext — store `.env.keys` in 1Password / a hardware key, never in the repo), pre-1.0 history had a config-format breaking change (1.x is stable — pin `dotenvx` version in CI and the install script, do not blindly `curl | sh` on every deploy), key rotation is one command but rotating *means* re-encrypting every value (fine for a few dozen secrets, friction at hundreds — that's the boundary where a real secret manager wins), the BSD-3-Clause license is permissive (binary distribution fine, attribution required), and the loaded-env precedence is the most common footgun (later `-f` flags override earlier; process env can be made to override `.env` with `--no-override` — read the precedence section once and lock the team's convention in the `package.json` / `Makefile` / `Justfile` script) | [`dotenvx`](clis/dotenvx/) |
| Want **a unit-aware arbitrary-precision command-line calculator in one Rust binary — `fend '5 GiB / 12 min to MB/s'` answers `7.456... MB/s`, `fend '0.1 + 0.2'` answers exactly `0.3` (rational, not float), `fend '5'\''10" to cm'` parses mixed imperial and answers `177.8 cm`, REPL mode supports variable bindings (`x = 1.21 GW`), lambda functions (`\x.x*2`), trig with explicit unit annotations (`sin(30 deg)`), base conversion (`0xff to binary`), and one-shot `fend 'expr'` for shell pipelines — built-in unit table covers SI / binary prefixes / imperial / time / data / angles, opt-in network-fetched currency exchange rates** — `fend` (printfn, MIT, v1.5.8) is the calculator the shell never had: `bc` is precise but unit-blind; `python -c` is unit-blind and float-by-default; `units(1)` does units but not arbitrary-precision rationals or trig; `fend` is the single binary that handles "convert this throughput", "what is 0.1 + 0.2 actually", and "how many seconds in a fortnight" without a notebook or `pint`; pick over [`numbat`](clis/numbat/) when you want a lightweight calculator with concise expressions and a built-in unit table (numbat is a full unit-aware *language* with custom unit definitions and a module system — pick numbat when you want to define your own unit system or write a small physics script; pick fend when you want `fend '5 GiB / 12 min'` to just work), over `bc` / `dc` for unit-awareness and rational-by-default arithmetic (keep `bc` for portable scripts — every Unix has it), over `python -c` / `python3 -q` for one-keystroke launch and unit-awareness without `pint`, over Qalculate's `qalc` when you prefer a clean single-binary install over a deeper unit + currency + physical-constants database; pairs with [`numbat`](clis/numbat/) (different scope — pocket calculator vs. unit-aware language), with [`jq`](clis/jq/) / [`yq`](clis/yq/) (compute a number out of a JSON field then `fend` it), with [`hyperfine`](clis/hyperfine/) (fend the throughput from a hyperfine result); caveat — currency rates are opt-in and network-fetched (off by default; safe on air-gapped boxes), not a CAS (no symbolic integration / equation solving — reach for SageMath / SymPy), REPL history persists to a state directory (mind pasted secrets), custom units require config-file edits not a CLI flag (numbat's module system is friendlier if you have many domain units), and irrational output is decimal-truncated by default (`to fraction` for exact rational, `--digits N` for wider decimals) | [`fend`](clis/fend/) |
| Want **an offline, privacy-first English grammar and style checker that runs on the local machine with zero cloud round trip — Rust core engine, plus a CLI (`harper-cli lint README.md` for one-shot, `--stdin` for pipelines, `--format json` for CI consumption) and a Language Server (`harper-ls --stdio`) that any editor with LSP support uses for inline grammar squiggles + fix actions; rule set covers spelling, grammar agreement, repeated words, capitalization, common confusables (`their`/`there`/`they're`), spacing, redundant phrases, and a growing style layer; lints code-comment prose in Rust / Python / JS / TS / Go / Markdown by parsing the source and extracting comments + string literals (so the docstring typos surface but the `let x = 42;` does not); no model download, no account, no telemetry — a single binary that lints prose** — `harper` (Automattic, Apache-2.0, v2.1.0) is the local LSP-shaped grammar checker for editors and CI when every other prose checker either calls home (Grammarly, hosted LanguageTool, GPT-based linters) or is trapped inside one editor (Word, Pages); pick over LanguageTool when you want a single static Rust binary instead of a 300 MB JVM server (LanguageTool wins on rule-set depth and non-English support; harper wins on install footprint, startup speed, and editor integration consistency), over Grammarly / Writer / GPT-based prose tools when air-gapped or privacy-sensitive (no SaaS, no API key, no network egress at all), over [`vale`](clis/vale/) when you want grammar feedback with zero config (vale ships *no* default rules and requires a `.vale.ini` + `vale sync` bootstrap; harper works the moment you install it — pick *both* when team has a documented style guide *and* wants editor squiggles), over [`typos`](clis/typos/) for grammar coverage beyond simple misspellings (typos is faster and broader for spell-pass-only; harper covers grammar + spelling — run typos in CI, harper in the editor); pairs with [`vale`](clis/vale/) (harper for grammar + editor squiggles, vale for per-project house style enforcement in CI), with [`typos`](clis/typos/) (typos for fast cross-repo spelling pass, harper for grammar nuance), with [`markdownlint-cli2`](clis/markdownlint-cli2/) (markdownlint for *Markdown structure*, harper for *prose content*); caveat — English only today (no rule sets for other languages — fall back to LanguageTool for multilingual docs), no autocorrect for nuanced grammar (fixes only for unambiguous issues like repeated words; stylistic suggestions stay as diagnostics by design — do not expect Grammarly-style rewrites), rule customization is global with a personal dictionary not per-project YAML rule packs (vale wins for per-repo style guides), code-comment parsing covers mainstream languages well but obscure DSLs may have no parser yet (file an issue or pre-extract prose to `--stdin`), and the "Automattic" repo owner sometimes confuses license scanners (the Apache-2.0 LICENSE file is canonical and unambiguous — cite the path directly if asked) | [`harper`](clis/harper/) |
| Want **a markup-aware, rule-pack-driven prose linter that enforces *your* house style across Markdown / reStructuredText / AsciiDoc / HTML / Org / XML / source-code comments via composable YAML rules — small fast Go engine + a *style pack* per project (`Packages = write-good, proselint, your-house-style` in `.vale.ini`, `vale sync` downloads them into `StylesPath`); rule types include substitution lists ("write GitHub not Github"), regex matches ("avoid passive voice"), readability scores (Flesch), occurrence counts ("don't repeat 'just' within 100 words"), and conditional checks; per-file-type parsers skip code blocks / inline code / link URLs / front-matter so you lint *prose nodes* not syntax; output is rich JSON / line-format / coloured CLI report with per-rule severity and `--minAlertLevel=error` for CI-blocking issues; first-class GitHub Action + pre-commit + Docker image** — `vale` (vale-cli, MIT, v3.14.1) is the right pick for per-project, per-team prose style enforcement in CI when "what is correct" is a team decision (terminology lists, banned words, voice and tone) rather than a universal grammar rule — rules live in your repo as YAML, reviewed in PRs like any other code; pick over [`harper`](clis/harper/) when you have a documented style guide that needs PR-level enforcement (harper ships one curated rule set for grammar + an LSP server for editor squiggles; vale ships *no* default rules and a downloadable pack ecosystem you commit per-repo with strong CI ergonomics — pick *both* when you want grammar feedback in the editor *and* style enforcement in CI), over [`markdownlint-cli2`](clis/markdownlint-cli2/) when the lint domain is *prose content* not *Markdown structure* (markdownlint enforces heading hierarchy / list spacing / link syntax; vale enforces terminology / voice / banned words — run both, they touch disjoint concerns), over [`typos`](clis/typos/) for rule-pack-driven style enforcement beyond simple misspellings (typos for the fast spell pass, vale for the style pass), over `proselint` (Python, not cataloged) which vale absorbs as a downloadable pack and runs 10–50× faster as a single Go binary with markup awareness proselint lacks; pairs with [`harper`](clis/harper/) (harper for editor grammar squiggles, vale for per-project CI gate), with [`markdownlint-cli2`](clis/markdownlint-cli2/) (markdownlint formats / structure, vale prose / content), with `pre-commit` / `lefthook` / GitHub Actions (vale's JSON output indexes by file / line / severity / rule for inline review-comment bots); caveat — no bundled rules (out of the box `vale README.md` lints nothing — must declare `Packages =` in `.vale.ini` and run `vale sync`; the reverse of harper's "works immediately" model — plan for the bootstrap step), `vale sync` requires network egress (style packs download from GitHub releases; air-gapped CI must vendor `.vale/styles/` and skip `sync`), the original repo lived at `errata-ai/vale` and now redirects to `vale-cli/vale` (old docs / Homebrew taps / Docker tags may still reference the old path — the MIT licence and v3.x line are canonical at the new location), no autocorrect (vale reports issues with the rule that fired; couple with editor LSP integrations like vale-ls if you want inline fix suggestions), rule authoring has a learning curve (`Substitution` / `Existence` / `Conditional` / `Occurrence` / `Readability` each have their own YAML schema — copy an existing pack and tweak), and a few advanced grammar packs call out to an external LanguageTool server (most packs are pure regex / lookup with no runtime dep — read the pack README before committing) | [`vale`](clis/vale/) |
| Want **the original Python TUI file manager — three-pane Miller columns (parent / current / preview), vim keymap (`hjkl` / `gg` / `G` / `yy` / `dd` / `pp` / `:` / `/`), `rifle.conf` declarative mime/ext → command opener table, `scope.sh` user-controlled preview pipeline (text via `bat`, JSON via `jq`, archives via `ouch list`, PDFs via `pdftotext`, images via `chafa` / kitty's graphics protocol), and a real Python plugin model (drop a file into `~/.config/ranger/plugins/` and it runs at startup with full access to the `fm` object — bulk rename, git status highlighting, devicons, zoxide integration); the reference implementation every "Rust ranger-clone" measures itself against** — `ranger` (ranger-fm, GPL-3.0-or-later, v1.9.4) is the right pick when you want the most complete out-of-the-box config surface and the largest plugin catalog in the keyboard-driven file-manager space and you do not mind a Python 3 runtime on every host; pick over [`joshuto`](clis/joshuto/) / [`lf`](clis/lf/) / [`yazi`](clis/yazi/) when the plugin ecosystem and `rifle.conf` opener rules matter more than a single static binary (the Rust / Go clones win for portability and cold-start latency; ranger wins for the configured workstation), over [`mc`](clis/mc/) for the vim-style keymap (mc is Norton-Commander function-keys), over [`broot`](clis/broot/) when you want a *full file manager* with paste buffer + opener rules not a *fuzzy tree finder*; pairs with [`bat`](clis/bat/) / [`fd`](clis/fd/) / [`ripgrep`](clis/ripgrep/) (`scope.sh` previews and `S` shell-out), with [`zoxide`](clis/zoxide/) (jump then visually browse), and with LLM-CLI workflows that need to *visually* stage subtrees into [`files-to-prompt`](clis/files-to-prompt/) or [`repomix`](clis/repomix/); caveat — Python 3 dependency on every host (~50 MB CPython, painful on busybox / embedded — the entire point of the Rust clones), GPL-3.0-or-later not MIT/Apache (binary redistribution fine; closed-source forks owe copyleft), image preview wiring is per-terminal (`preview_images_method` covers kitty / iTerm2 / wezterm natively but Linux X11 still needs `ueberzugpp` for non-graphics-protocol terminals), and release cadence is slow (v1.9.4 has been the stable tag for a long stretch — feature work has migrated to clones, ranger is stable not bleeding-edge) | [`ranger`](clis/ranger/) |
| Want **the Go static-binary answer to `ranger` — same Miller-columns layout, same vim keymap, same `yy` / `dd` / `pp` / `:` / `/` muscle memory, async I/O so a `cd` into a 100k-entry tree does not block the UI, but shipped as one ~3 MB binary with a 200-line shell-flavoured config DSL (`~/.config/lf/lfrc`) instead of a Python plugin tree; opener logic and previews are user-supplied shell snippets (`cmd open ${{ ... }}` for verb dispatch, `set previewer ~/.config/lf/preview` for the right pane) so anything you can write in `bash` becomes a key binding, and `lf -last-dir-path` writes the cursor directory on exit so a shell wrapper can `cd` to it** — `lf` (gokcehan, MIT, v41 / r41) is what to reach for when you want ranger ergonomics on a busybox container, an embedded Linux box, or any host where a Python runtime is the problem; pick over [`ranger`](clis/ranger/) when the Python dependency is the cost (same keymap, smaller surface — you wire `rifle.conf`-equivalent opener logic in `lfrc` shell snippets), over [`joshuto`](clis/joshuto/) when you prefer "everything is a shell snippet" extensibility over declarative TOML config (joshuto for declarative, lf for shell-out), over [`nnn`](clis/nnn/) for ranger keymap + a more configurable opener pipeline (nnn is C and smaller still but uses its own non-vim keymap and a POSIX-script plugin model), over [`yazi`](clis/yazi/) when footprint beats batteries (yazi for built-in image preview + Lua plugins; lf for "one Go binary, BYO preview"); pairs with [`bat`](clis/bat/) / [`chafa`](clis/chafa/) / [`ouch`](clis/ouch/) (the standard preview script trio), with [`zoxide`](clis/zoxide/) (jump then visually browse), and with LLM-CLI workflows that need to stage paths on a remote container before piping into [`files-to-prompt`](clis/files-to-prompt/) or [`code2prompt`](clis/code2prompt/); caveat — no built-in image preview (wire `previewer` to `chafa` / `viu` / kitty `+kitten icat`; on stock SSH expect text-only), no built-in archive support (`extract` is a `cmd`-defined `ouch decompress` snippet you write yourself), config DSL is small but quirky (`%` / `$` / `!` / `&` shell-snippet prefixes have meaningfully different semantics — sync vs interactive vs background), single-pane focus mode missing (committed to the three-column layout — for one-pane fuzzy tree pick [`broot`](clis/broot/) instead), and versioning is date-letter (`r28`, `r33`, `r41`) not semver — pin by the `r41` tag when reproducibility matters | [`lf`](clis/lf/) |
| Want **the fast, minimal Rust answer to `cloc` — walks a directory tree, classifies each file by extension, parallel-counts code / comment / blank lines per language, and prints a sorted `Language | Files | Lines | Blank | Comment | Code` table to stdout; honours `.gitignore` by default (uses the same `walkdir` + `ignore` walker as `ripgrep`, so `target/` / `node_modules/` / `dist/` / `.venv/` are skipped without a per-project `--exclude-dir` chain), `--include` / `--exclude` flags handle the rest, and the whole tool is one ~2 MB binary with no config file — typically 10-20× faster than the Perl `cloc` it imitates on large trees because it parallelises across all cores** — `loc` (cgag, dual MIT / Apache-2.0, v0.4.1) is the cheap "how big is this codebase?" probe for CI gating and one-off curiosity when `cloc`'s exhaustive language coverage is overkill; pick over [`tokei`](clis/tokei/) when you want the smallest, simplest "just give me the table" tool with no JSON / YAML / CBOR export to ignore (tokei is the more featured modern Rust counter — ~270 languages vs loc's ~150, plus `--sort` and structured output, and is the right pick for any non-trivial use; loc survives in this catalog as the minimal alternative), over [`scc`](clis/scc/) when you do *not* want COCOMO cost-estimation or complexity columns (scc adds those on top of LOC; loc is just the table), over [`cloc`](clis/cloc/) when speed beats edge-case correctness on obscure languages (cloc has the broadest coverage and most mature heredoc / embedded-language detection; loc gives the same answer in a tenth of the wall-clock time on mainstream stacks); pairs naturally with LLM-CLI workflows as the "is this repo small enough to pack into context?" pre-flight before invoking [`repomix`](clis/repomix/) / [`code2prompt`](clis/code2prompt/) / [`files-to-prompt`](clis/files-to-prompt/) so an agent does not try to pack a 4M-LOC tree into a 200k context window; caveat — smaller language coverage than tokei / cloc (~150 vs ~270 — mainstream languages covered, obscure / templating / config-as-code may be miscounted as "Unrecognized"), no structured output (stdout table only — no JSON flag for clean CI parsing, you scrape the table or pipe through `awk`; pick `tokei -o json` if you need machine-readable natively), maintenance is quiet (last point release some years old — the LOC-counting problem is largely solved, but new languages do not land; for active maintenance pick tokei), no per-file breakdown by default (`--files` lists files with counts but the per-language `--by-file` cross-tab from cloc / tokei is absent), and default skips hidden + gitignored (surprising the first time — pass `--unrestricted` to count everything including `.git/` itself, which you almost never want) | [`loc`](clis/loc/) |
| Want **a single-binary web-shell that exposes any local command — `bash`, `htop`, `tmux attach`, an interactive LLM-agent session — over WebSocket with a bundled xterm.js front-end, so a long-running CLI agent on a remote box becomes a shareable URL a teammate (or a phone) can attach to with no SSH client, no port-forward, no captive-portal hassle (read-only mode for live demos, basic-auth and TLS built in, max-clients caps for safety)** — `ttyd` (tsl0922, MIT, 1.7.7) — pick over [`tmate`](clis/tmate/) when the viewer side has only a browser (tmate needs an SSH client; ttyd renders in any tab); pairs with [`agg`](clis/agg/) (record an asciinema cast of the session, `agg cast.gif` produces the README-embeddable artifact) and [`skate`](clis/skate/) (cheap personal KV store for the wrapper script's per-host nonce / token / last-attached-URL) | [`ttyd`](clis/ttyd/) |

## Recently added (+3)

- [granted](clis/granted/) — Go-implemented cloud-credential broker for AWS / Azure / GCP that wraps SSO / IAM Identity Center / role-chaining behind one prompt: `assume <profile>` reads `~/.aws/config`, walks the SSO + role hops, caches STS creds in the OS keychain, and exports `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` / `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` / `AWS_SESSION_TOKEN` into the *current* shell only; `assume -c <profile>` opens the AWS Console in an isolated browser-container tab bound to that account, so prod and staging stay visually distinct and never share cookies (fwdcloudsec/granted, MIT, v0.39.0, 2026-03-28). Pick over plain `aws sso login` + `AWS_PROFILE` when juggling 6+ accounts daily — every shell gets its own short-lived creds in the keychain instead of files in `~/.aws/sso/cache/`, and the prompt shows which account is active and how long until expiry; pick over [`aws-vault`](clis/aws-vault/) when the auth surface is IAM Identity Center + multi-cloud (aws-vault is AWS-only and IAM-key-flavoured, granted is the modern SSO-first answer with first-class browser isolation); pick over `saml2aws` when SSO is available (saml2aws still wins on legacy SAML IdPs). Pair with [`steampipe`](clis/steampipe/) / [`doctl`](clis/doctl/) / `gcloud` for the read-side once creds are assumed, with [`infracost`](clis/infracost/) / [`opentofu`](clis/opentofu/) / [`pulumi`](clis/pulumi/) for the write-side. AI angle — agents that touch infra need *scoped, short-lived* creds and `granted` makes that the default: per-shell exports mean a sibling agent in another shell can be on `read-only-prod` while this one is on `sandbox`, STS expiry caps the blast radius of any prompt-injection to the remaining session window, and pairing granted profiles with read-only IAM Identity Center permission sets gives agents a "look but don't touch" lane separate from the operator's deploy lane; `granted console` deep links let an agent put "open this account/region in one click" links in PR comments instead of asking reviewers to navigate. Caveats — full browser-tab isolation needs the Granted browser extension (without it console opens still work but lose per-tab account scoping); the keychain dependency is awkward on a fresh CI runner with no Secret Service (use `--no-cache` or a different tool there); the `assume` shell helper has to be `source`d into the current shell to mutate env vars (covered by the standard zsh/bash/fish install snippet); granted is a credential *broker*, not a permission story — least-privilege is still IAM's job.
- [kamal](clis/kamal/) — Ruby-implemented zero-downtime deploy tool from Basecamp that ships a Dockerized app onto any set of SSH-reachable Linux hosts via one `config/deploy.yml`: `kamal deploy` builds the image (locally or on a remote builder), pushes to a registry, SSH-streams the new container to each host, and drains old containers behind its own `kamal-proxy` (Go, Let's Encrypt, websocket-aware) — no Kubernetes, no managed PaaS, just `docker` on plain VMs (basecamp/kamal, MIT, v2.11.0, 2026-03-18). Pick over Kubernetes ([`kustomize`](clis/kustomize/) / [`argocd`](clis/argocd/) / [`helm`](clis/helm/)) when 3–30 VMs is the actual scale and the operator burden of a control plane is not justified — `kamal` deploys, rolls back, and tails logs across the fleet without anything other than Docker on the hosts; pick over hosted PaaS ([`flyctl`](clis/flyctl/) / Heroku / Render / Railway) when you specifically want to *own* the hosts (cost, data residency, regulated workloads, GPU-on-bare-metal) without giving up the deploy ergonomics; pick over `docker compose` + bespoke SSH scripts when you need rolling rollouts, registry-based image distribution, secrets handling, and a proxy already wired up; pick over Capistrano / Ansible / Mina for any app you can package as an OCI image — `kamal` replaces them. Pair with [`flyctl`](clis/flyctl/) / [`doctl`](clis/doctl/) / [`hcloud`](https://github.com/hetznercloud/cli) on the host-provisioning side, with [`tilt`](clis/tilt/) / [`devspace`](clis/devspace/) on the inner-loop dev side (cluster-shaped dev, kamal for prod), with [`dagger`](clis/dagger/) / [`earthly`](clis/earthly/) on the build-pipeline side (build with one, ship with kamal). AI angle — `config/deploy.yml` is the *whole* deploy graph in one file; an agent can read it, propose a diff, explain blast radius (which hosts, which env vars, which proxy hostnames) without chasing a Helm chart spread across kustomize overlays + ArgoCD apps + CRDs; `kamal rollback` is one-command revert in seconds, so an agent that ships a bad change can recover without paging a human; `kamal app exec --reuse "rails runner …"` is a bounded shell for triage that stays in container scope (no SSH-onto-the-box blast radius); idempotent verbs (`deploy`, `redeploy`, `app boot`, `proxy reboot`) tolerate retries cleanly, which is what agents do under failure; secrets stay in the broker (1Password / Bitwarden / `op` / env at deploy time) and never have to land in a prompt or chat history. Caveats — stateful services (Postgres / Redis / Kafka / S3-equivalent) are out of scope; `kamal` assumes you point your app at managed or separately-deployed data services; the model is one app, many hosts (multi-app monorepos run `kamal` once per app — there is no top-level "deploy the whole org"); `kamal-proxy` is opinionated (HTTP/S, websockets, Let's Encrypt, sticky sessions — not a Layer-7 mesh, not Envoy; front it with a real LB if you need request-shaping); SSH is load-bearing (CI must be able to reach the hosts, or you need a deploy bastion / self-hosted runner inside the VPC); the CLI itself needs Ruby on the operator side (servers don't, only Docker — use the official `ghcr.io/basecamp/kamal` runner image in CI to skip the Ruby install).
- [bencher](clis/bencher/) — Rust-implemented continuous benchmarking suite (CLI + self-hostable server) that catches *performance* regressions in CI the same way unit tests catch correctness regressions: `bencher run --project myapp --branch main "cargo bench"` wraps your existing harness (`cargo bench` / `criterion` / `iai`, Google Benchmark, JMH, `pytest-benchmark`, `go test -bench`, `hyperfine`, raw JSON), parses the output, and POSTs results with the commit SHA + branch to the server, which stores a per-branch time series and runs statistical regression checks (t-test / IQR / percentile thresholds) on the PR (bencherdev/bencher, dual `Apache-2.0 OR MIT` for the open core with a `Bencher Plus License` carve-out for `plus/` paths, v0.6.4, 2026-05-02). Pick over hand-rolled "compare two `cargo bench` runs" scripts (every team writes one, no one keeps it green; `bencher` is the productized version with a real time-series store and PR gate); pick over `hyperfine` alone (great ad-hoc tool, no history, no PR gate — pair them with `hyperfine --export-json` → `bencher run --adapter json …`); pick over Codspeed / Datadog Continuous Profiler / NYRKIÖ when the perf data itself is sensitive or you want to own the server (the commercial alternatives are zero-ops but ship perf data off-prem); pick over `criterion.rs` / `pytest-benchmark` / `JMH` *alone* when you need a CI gate, not just numbers — `bencher` is the layer that turns local benchmark output into a PR check with history. Pair with [`grafana-k6`](clis/k6/) / [`vegeta`](clis/vegeta/) / [`bombardier`](clis/bombardier/) as load-generator inputs (their JSON adapters feed `bencher run`), with [`reviewdog`](clis/reviewdog/) to surface regressions as inline PR comments, with [`pre-commit`](clis/pre-commit/) / [`lefthook`](clis/lefthook/) for orchestration. AI angle — coding agents that "make this faster" or "refactor without regressing" need a numerical truth oracle, not a vibe; with `bencher run` on PR check, an agent that finishes a refactor sees the same red X for "you regressed throughput by 8%" that it sees for a broken test; the threshold config (per metric, per branch, t-test / percentile / static) is structured so the agent can reason about "is this regression within tolerance" and either accept with justification in the PR body or back the change out; the time-series API (per-metric history as JSON) lets an agent debugging "why did p95 jump on main last Tuesday" bisect against the actual numbers instead of re-running benches blindly; broad adapter coverage (Rust, C++, Go, Java, Python, shell) means the same gate works everywhere in a polyglot monorepo; "speed up `parse_event` by 20% on the `criterion::parse_event` bench, do not regress any other bench by more than 2%" becomes a precise, machine-checked contract. Caveats — CI noise is real (shared runners have variable thermal / neighbour behaviour; tight thresholds will flap — prefer statistical thresholds over absolute deltas, and use self-hosted runners pinned to consistent hardware for the perf job); benchmarks have to be deterministic-ish (network / wall-clock / unseeded RNG will flap the gate at random); the server is part of the deploy (hosted SaaS works out of the box; self-hosting is one Docker Compose stack but it *is* a stack you now operate); `plus/` paths are *not* Apache/MIT (read [LICENSE.md](https://github.com/bencherdev/bencher/blob/main/LICENSE.md) before vendoring or shipping a redistributable build); `bencher` tells you a metric regressed, not why — pair with `cargo flamegraph` / `samply` / `perf` / `pprof` / `tracy` for root cause.

## Previously added (+3)

- [minikube](clis/minikube/) — kubernetes/minikube's single Go binary that boots a real, conformant Kubernetes cluster on a developer laptop or CI runner via one of several drivers (Docker, Podman, HyperKit, KVM2, VirtualBox, QEMU, VMware, Parallels), exposes the standard `kubectl` API surface, and tracks upstream Kubernetes within ~1–2 weeks of GA so `--kubernetes-version=v1.34.x` can pin any of the last ~10 minor releases for compatibility testing (kubernetes/minikube, Apache-2.0, v1.38.1, 2026-02-19). Pick over [`kind`](clis/kind/) when you need hypervisor-isolated drivers (HyperKit / KVM2 / QEMU / VMware) instead of Docker-only — the right answer when the host runs no container engine, or when you want a VM boundary between the cluster and the laptop's filesystem; pick over [`k3d`](clis/k3d/) when you want upstream `kubeadm`-shaped Kubernetes (k3d wraps k3s, which is conformant but lighter and trims components — minikube is the closer mirror of a real production cluster); pick over a paid managed cluster for inner-loop dev when "boot a fresh cluster, do destructive things, throw it away in 30 seconds" is a daily workflow (`minikube delete -p <profile>` is free; `eks delete cluster` takes 15 minutes and a `terraform destroy`); the broadest addons catalog in the niche (`minikube addons enable ingress|metrics-server|registry|gvisor|volumesnapshots|csi-hostpath-driver|...`) means topology can match production without per-component Helm install. Pair with [`tilt`](clis/tilt/) / [`devspace`](clis/devspace/) / [`skaffold`](clis/skaffold/) for the inner-loop dev experience on top (cluster from minikube, file-sync + restart from tilt/devspace), with [`vcluster`](clis/vcluster/) when one minikube needs to host many isolated logical clusters for multi-tenant testing, with [`kubectx`](clis/kubectx/) / [`kubectl`](clis/kubectl/) for context switching across multiple named profiles, with [`promtool`](clis/promtool/) when validating Prometheus rules against a real cluster's metrics. AI angle — `minikube start --profile=<branch> --nodes=3 --kubernetes-version=v1.34.0` gives an agent a deterministic, throw-away cluster per branch / PR / scenario; `minikube delete -p <profile>` is the cheap reset that makes "destructive integration tests" tractable in a long-running agent loop; `minikube tunnel` exposes LoadBalancer services on the host so the agent's HTTP probes do not need NodePort gymnastics. Caveats — boot time is 30–60 s (kind is faster — keep a long-lived cluster across runs when latency dominates); HyperKit is macOS-only and has been deprecated in favor of QEMU + Docker on Apple Silicon; the Docker driver runs the cluster as containers in your host Docker (less isolation than a VM but the fastest start); addons are not all production-ready (the registry addon is fine for local image push, not for shipping; gvisor needs a compatible kernel); multi-node + HA modes consume real laptop CPU + RAM (minimum ~2 GB per node — a 3-node HA cluster is ~6 GB before workloads).
- [promtool](clis/promtool/) — the official Prometheus CLI distributed inside the same `prometheus-3.11.3.<os>-<arch>.tar.gz` as the server binary (lives at `prometheus/cmd/promtool` upstream), covering four jobs in one binary: configuration validation (`promtool check config|rules|service-discovery|web-config`), rule and alert unit tests (`promtool test rules` runs a YAML-defined suite that loads synthetic series, advances simulated time, and asserts on recording-rule values + firing alert state), PromQL debugging against a live server (`promtool query instant|range|series|labels|analyze`), and TSDB inspection (`promtool tsdb analyze|dump|create-blocks-from openmetrics`) for cardinality audits and historical backfill (prometheus/prometheus, Apache-2.0, v3.11.3, 2026-04-27). Pick over hand-rolled YAML lint + grep when the alerting story is load-bearing — `promtool test rules` is the only way to assert "this `for: 5m` actually fires the page after 5 minutes of high error rate" without deploying the rule to staging and waiting; pick over [`yamllint`](clis/yamllint/) on the orthogonal axis (yamllint catches YAML grammar; promtool catches PromQL semantics — run both); pick over the Prometheus web UI for CI gating (the UI is for humans; promtool is the JSON-emitting machine surface); pick over `amtool` (Alertmanager's CLI) for *rules* validation (amtool covers Alertmanager config + alert silencing — the two compose: promtool gates the rules, amtool gates the routing). Pair with [`vegeta`](clis/vegeta/) / [`k6`](clis/k6/) / [`oha`](clis/oha/) (load generators that produce the metrics promtool then validates queries against), with [`opencost`](clis/opencost/) (which consumes Prometheus metrics for Kubernetes cost — promtool gates the recording rules opencost's dashboards depend on), with [`reviewdog`](clis/reviewdog/) (post promtool's findings as inline PR comments), with [`pre-commit`](clis/pre-commit/) / [`lefthook`](clis/lefthook/) (run `promtool check rules` on every commit to `*.rules.yml`). AI angle — `promtool test rules` is the deterministic eval surface an agent can use to verify a generated alert rule before opening a PR (synthetic series + simulated time + asserted state — no flakes, no race conditions); `promtool query analyze` against a live server reveals the per-series cardinality cost of a generated query so an agent can refuse to ship a `count by (request_id)` that would explode the index. Caveats — pin promtool to the same minor as the in-cluster Prometheus server (rule-test semantics evolve; mixing v2 and v3 produces confusing output); `promtool query` against a live server needs network access to `:9090` (use a dedicated read-only Prometheus replica if the prod path is locked down); `promtool tsdb analyze` on a multi-TB TSDB takes minutes and significant disk I/O — schedule, don't run interactively; the JSON output is stable but verbose, plan to `jq` heavily.
- [istioctl](clis/istioctl/) — istio/istio's control-plane CLI for the Istio service mesh (CNCF graduated 2025): one Go binary that installs / upgrades / uninstalls Istio in a Kubernetes cluster, validates mesh CRDs offline, runs ~60 built-in checks against the live mesh, and inspects the Envoy sidecar of any pod (istio/istio, Apache-2.0, v1.29.2, 2026-04-13). The four jobs in concrete commands: `istioctl install --set profile=default --set revision=1-29-2` for revision-tagged canary control-plane upgrades (run two `istiod` revisions side-by-side, namespace-by-namespace migrate the data plane, retire the old revision); `istioctl validate -f <virtualservice.yaml>` catches schema *and* semantic errors (host overlap, port mismatch, conflicting policies) before they reach the cluster; `istioctl analyze --all-namespaces -o json` runs the built-in analyzer suite against live mesh state and emits machine-readable diagnostics; `istioctl proxy-status` + `istioctl proxy-config cluster|listener|route|endpoint <pod>` is the canonical "what does this Envoy actually believe" view, the irreplaceable triage tool when "the `VirtualService` says route to v2 but traffic goes to v1". Pick over [`linkerd`](clis/linkerd/) when you need the broader feature set — multi-cluster mesh, ambient mode (sidecar-less L4 + per-namespace L7 waypoints), per-route circuit breakers, advanced traffic shifting, JWT/OPA integration (linkerd is smaller surface + opinionated defaults, lower operational cost; istio is bigger surface + more knobs, higher ceiling); pick over [`cilium-cli`](clis/cilium-cli/) when the mesh and the CNI are separate concerns (cilium-cli is the eBPF-CNI-and-mesh-unified path — pick when "one network primitive owns L3 through L7" is the goal; istioctl when the CNI is already chosen and the mesh is a separable layer); pick over hand-rolled `kubectl` + `helm` for Istio install when revision-tagged upgrades matter (istioctl owns the canary-upgrade choreography that hand-rolled Helm cannot reproduce safely). Pair with [`kubeshark`](clis/kubeshark/) for L4/L7 packet-level forensics next to the mesh-config view (istioctl shows what Envoy *believes*; kubeshark shows what actually flowed), with [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) / [`grpcui`](clis/grpcui/) to verify mesh routing behaves as the `VirtualService` says, with [`minikube`](clis/minikube/) / [`kind`](clis/kind/) for local mesh experimentation, with [`cosign`](clis/cosign/) (Istio control-plane images are signed — verify before installing in regulated environments). AI angle — `istioctl analyze -o json` is a structured diagnostic stream an agent can ingest to triage mesh issues without parsing prose; `istioctl proxy-config <kind> <pod> -o json` exposes the live Envoy config as JSON the agent can diff against the declared `VirtualService` / `DestinationRule` to localize "config drift" vs "config bug"; revision-tagged upgrades give an agent a reversible upgrade primitive — install rev `1-29-2` next to `1-28-3`, migrate one namespace, verify, roll back the namespace label if `analyze` complains, all without disturbing the rest of the mesh. Caveats — pin istioctl to the same minor as in-cluster `istiod` (control-plane API surface evolves across minors; mixing istioctl 1.29 against istiod 1.27 produces confusing errors); each Istio minor has ~6 months of upstream support — the upgrade cadence is real operational work, not optional; ambient mode (the sidecar-less data plane) is GA but the surface is younger than the sidecar mode and edge cases still surface; `istioctl install` is imperative — for GitOps, prefer the `IstioOperator` CR + Helm chart route and use istioctl for triage only; the analyzer covers ~60 checks but is not exhaustive (it catches the common 80%, not the long tail).

## Previously added (+3)

- [opentofu](clis/opentofu/) — Linux Foundation-governed, MPL-2.0 fork of Terraform shipped as one Go binary `tofu` that is wire-compatible with Terraform 1.5.x at the HCL2, provider plugin protocol, and state file layers, so an existing `*.tf` tree migrates with zero rewrite and the entire Terraform Registry's providers (aws, google, azurerm, kubernetes, helm, github, datadog, cloudflare, vault, …) work without a republish (opentofu/opentofu, MPL-2.0, v1.11.6, 2026-04-08). Pick over Terraform proper when your legal team requires OSI-approved licenses for foundational tooling — BUSL-1.1 fails that gate, MPL-2.0 passes; pick over Terraform when you want **state encryption at rest** (1.7+, key from PBKDF2 / AWS KMS / GCP KMS / OpenBao / Vault) as a first-class feature rather than an external KMS shim; pick over Terraform when you want **provider-defined functions** (`provider::aws::arn_parse(...)`) to push computation into the provider rather than into module HCL gymnastics. Pair with [`tenv`](clis/tenv/) to pin `tofu` per repo via `.opentofu-version` or HCL `required_version` (the right management layer — opentofu is the engine), with [`tflint`](clis/tflint/) / [`tfsec`](clis/tfsec/) / [`checkov`](clis/checkov/) downstream (all read opentofu-flavored HCL since the language is identical), with [`infracost`](clis/infracost/) for predicted-cost gating, with [`atmos`](clis/atmos/) when the per-env stack composition story is the dominant cost. AI angle — `tofu plan -out=plan.bin && tofu show -json plan.bin` is a deterministic, machine-readable diff an agent can reason about before `apply`, and `-auto-approve` should *never* be the agent's default; the OSI license posture also means an agent's CI image can ship `tofu` without a per-release license review treadmill. Caveats — features added to Terraform proper *after* the 1.6 fork point (HCP-Terraform-specific workflows, certain newer Terraform-only modules) may not exist; check parity for your specific stack before migrating; the OpenTofu Registry mirrors the Terraform Registry on first request but obscure providers may need an explicit registry override; state-file format compatibility is one-way-forward (a state written by tofu 1.7+ with encryption is not readable by Terraform).
- [opencost](clis/opencost/) — CNCF Incubating, vendor-neutral Kubernetes cost monitoring that joins in-cluster Prometheus usage metrics (`container_cpu_usage_seconds_total`, `container_memory_working_set_bytes`, `kube_persistentvolumeclaim_*`, `node_*`) with cloud-provider pricing (AWS Cost & Usage Report / GCP Billing Export / Azure Rate Card / user-supplied JSON for on-prem and bare metal) and emits per-namespace / per-deployment / per-pod / per-label dollar cost over arbitrary time windows via REST API, Prometheus metrics, `kubectl-cost` CLI, and a UI (opencost/opencost, Apache-2.0, v1.120.1, 2026-04-28). Pick over the cloud provider's native cost explorer when the cluster is multi-tenant and "this cluster cost $14k last month" needs to break down to "the `data-eng` namespace was $4.2k of that, of which the `airflow-workers` deployment was $2.8k, of which the `etl-large` pod spec accounted for 71%"; pick over the commercial Kubecost product when you want the open core Apache-2.0 data plane without a vendor relationship (Kubecost is the SaaS layer on top — opencost is the donated CNCF project underneath); pick over a SaaS like Vantage / CloudZero / Finout when the metrics need to land in *your* Prometheus alongside the rest of your reliability data, not in a third-party dashboard. Pair with [`infracost`](clis/infracost/) on the orthogonal axis (infracost predicts cost from an [`opentofu`](clis/opentofu/) / Terraform plan *before* apply; opencost measures actual cost *after* deploy — both numbers in the same FinOps dashboard), with [`steampipe`](clis/steampipe/) for ad-hoc SQL queries across the cloud's billing API for non-Kubernetes resources, with [`popeye`](clis/popeye/) / [`kube-score`](clis/kube-score/) for the *config quality* axis next to the *cost* axis. AI angle — the predicted-cost endpoint plus per-PR resource-spec extraction lets an agent answer "this PR adds 300m CPU × 4 replicas — projected monthly cost $X" as a check, and the Prometheus metrics surface means an agent can be told "alert if any namespace's 7-day cost grows >30% week-over-week" without writing the cost-aggregation logic itself. Caveats — opencost only sees what runs as pods (VM / serverless / managed-DB workloads need the cloud's native explorer); near-real-time *estimation* against published rates, not invoice-grade reconciliation (reconcile against the authoritative bill monthly); on-prem pricing requires a hand-maintained custom JSON; first install is a Helm chart with a non-trivial Prometheus dependency.
- [yamllint](clis/yamllint/) — Python-implemented YAML linter for syntax validity *and* stylistic consistency that wraps a `PyYAML`-derived parser with a tunable rule pack covering the silent-failure classes the parser itself accepts: `truthy` (catches the unquoted `no` / `yes` / `on` / `off` / `y` / `n` that YAML 1.1 silently coerces to booleans — the Norway problem where country code `NO` parses as `false`), `key-duplicates` (catches the silent override where two same-named keys in a mapping leave only the last value standing), `indentation`, `line-length`, `trailing-spaces`, `comments-indentation`, `document-start`, `octal-values`, `quoted-strings`, ~22 rules total, all overridable per-repo via a checked-in `.yamllint` (adrienverge/yamllint, GPL-3.0-or-later, v1.38.0, 2026-01-13). Pick over "just run the YAML parser" when you ship Ansible playbooks, Helm values, Kubernetes manifests, GitHub Actions workflows, or docker-compose files and need a deterministic CI gate against the YAML-1.1 truthy + duplicate-key footguns (the most common production-incident classes for those formats); pick over schema validators ([`kubeconform`](clis/kubeconform/), [`actionlint`](clis/actionlint/), `helm lint`, `ansible-lint`) on the orthogonal axis — yamllint catches `truthy: yes`, schema validators catch `kind: Deploymnt` (typo against OpenAPI); **run both** in a serious CI. Pair with [`yamlfmt`](clis/yamlfmt/) (Google, MIT) as the formatter side — yamlfmt rewrites for style, yamllint reports for correctness; standard pipeline runs yamlfmt in pre-commit and yamllint in CI to catch the semantic violations no formatter can fix. Pair with [`reviewdog`](clis/reviewdog/) via native `-f=yamllint` parser to post findings as inline PR comments with `-filter-mode=added` so legacy violations don't block PRs that didn't introduce them. Pair with [`pre-commit`](clis/pre-commit/) / [`lefthook`](clis/lefthook/) as the orchestrator. AI angle — agents that generate YAML for Helm / Ansible / GH Actions consistently miss the truthy and duplicate-key footguns because the parser silently accepts both; running yamllint in the agent's verification loop is the cheapest mechanical catch for the class of errors that would otherwise only surface at deploy time. Caveats — GPL-3.0-or-later constrains *redistributing modified versions* (running it imposes nothing; vendoring source into a closed-source product is the constraint — invoke the published binary instead); reports only, no auto-fix (use yamlfmt for that); validates *YAML*, not "this is a valid Kubernetes Deployment" (pair with schema linters above); the `truthy` rule's default disallows `yes` / `no` / `on` / `off` *without quotes* — a deliberate bias toward explicit `true` / `false`, occasionally requires updating Ansible playbooks that relied on YAML-1.1 coercion.

## Previously added (+3)

- [turbo](clis/turbo/) — Rust-implemented incremental task runner for JS/TS monorepos that hashes each task's inputs (source files + env + lockfile slice + upstream task hashes) and either replays a cached output tarball from local disk / a shared remote store or executes and uploads the result, so `turbo run build test lint` finishes in seconds when nothing changed and a `FULL TURBO` reuse line drops a 4-minute cold build to ~12 s when only one of eleven packages changed (vercel/turborepo, MIT, v2.9.8, 2026-05-03). Pick over [`nx`](https://nx.dev/) when you want one `turbo.json` and no plugin/generator surface; pick over [`moon`](clis/moon/) when the monorepo is JS-first (moon for truly polyglot Rust+Python+Go+JS); pick over `bazel`/`pants`/`buck2` when 50-engineer scale is what you have, not 500; pick over plain `pnpm -r` once the cold build crosses 30 s. Remote-cache protocol is open (Vercel-hosted or any S3-compatible community implementation) so cache vendor lock-in is a choice not a default. AI angle — `--filter=...[origin/main]` lets agents bound their own verification scope to "tests this branch could have broken", and `turbo run --dry=json` is a machine-readable plan-then-execute surface; the cache hit on a service the agent did not touch is a cheap negative-evidence signal a reviewer can cite. Caveats — JS/TS-first; cache poisoning is real if a task reads inputs not declared under `inputs`/`env` (treat the declarations as load-bearing); shared remote caches need access controls or signed artifacts; 2.x renamed `pipeline` → `tasks` and tightened env handling vs 1.x.
- [go-jsonnet](clis/go-jsonnet/) — Pure-Go implementation of the Jsonnet config language shipping `jsonnet` / `jsonnetfmt` / `jsonnet-lint` as static binaries (google/go-jsonnet, Apache-2.0, v0.22.0, 2026-03-24): evaluates `.jsonnet` programs into JSON / YAML / a multi-file output tree (`-m out/` writes one file per top-level key — perfect for "render N Kubernetes manifests from one program"), with `local`/`function`/`import`/comprehensions giving you a real language for generating large families of YAML without going to Helm or a templating engine. Pick over [`cue`](clis/cue/) when expressiveness + functions + imports matter more than CUE's constraint/unification correctness story; pick over [`dhall`](clis/dhall/) when total-functional purity is overkill; pick over [`nickel`](clis/nickel/) when ecosystem maturity (Tanka, grafonnet, kube-libsonnet) matters more than gradual typing; pick over [`pkl`](clis/pkl/) when near-zero install footprint and lazy evaluation matter more than IDE polish; pick over `helm` / `kustomize` when you want a general-purpose language not Kubernetes-shaped templating. Pick the **Go** implementation over the C++ original when you want a static binary with no `libstdc++` runtime — the right default for distroless / Alpine CI images and for embedding via Go module. AI angle — evaluation is pure (no I/O, no time, no random) so an agent loop "edit `.jsonnet` → re-evaluate → diff generated YAML" verifies its own change without a sandbox; `jsonnet-lint` catches the unused-binding / unreachable-branch class of LLM-typical mistakes for free; `import` is hermetic via `--jpath` (no implicit network fetch). Caveats — lazy evaluation hides typos in unreferenced bindings until forced; error messages are terse; "manifests as code" demands discipline (out-of-band `kubectl edit` becomes drift you discover at next render — pair with [`flux`](clis/flux/) / [`argocd`](clis/argocd/)); Go and C++ implementations occasionally drift on edge cases — pick one per repo.
- [devspace](clis/devspace/) — Inner-loop developer CLI for Kubernetes that turns the cluster into your laptop's background runtime: `devspace dev` reads `devspace.yaml`, swaps the target deployment's container for a "dev container" with your tools and your source mounted, opens file-sync from your local working dir into `/app`, port-forwards every container port to localhost, and tails the logs — edit a file → file appears in the pod within ~1 s → in-pod hot-reloader (`nodemon` / `air` / `cargo watch` / `uvicorn --reload`) restarts → log line on your terminal, sub-3-second total round trip on most stacks (devspace-sh/devspace, Apache-2.0, v6.3.21, 2026-04-22). Pick over [`tilt`](clis/tilt/) when YAML-first + Helm-aware composition beats Tilt's UI-first Starlark `Tiltfile`; pick over [`skaffold`](clis/skaffold/) when *file sync + restart* is the dominant cost (skaffold optimises the *build* primitive); pick over `mirrord` when you want to keep your code running *in* the cluster instead of bringing the cluster's traffic / env / secrets to your laptop process (they are different inversions and not mutually exclusive); pick over the "compose for dev, manifests for staging" split when you want to stay on Kubernetes from the start so the staging-vs-laptop drift never opens. AI angle — sub-3-second feedback collapses an agent's edit-test loop from "edit → docker build → kubectl rollout → wait for ready → test" (60+ s/turn) to "edit → sync → restart → test" (~2 s/turn), directly raising edit cycles per hour and lowering token spend on "waiting" turns; `devspace.yaml` is a declarative dev contract an agent can read to know which container/ports/paths matter without inferring from the `Dockerfile`; `devspace run-pipeline <name>` exposes named flows (`dev` / `deploy` / `purge` / user-defined) so agents can be told "use `devspace run-pipeline test`" without learning the `kubectl run` / `helm test` mechanics. Caveats — the dev container is *not* the prod container (always run a separate `deploy` pipeline before declaring something works); `devspace dev` deploys workloads into whatever context `kubectl` currently points at (set per-repo kubeconfigs and prefer per-engineer namespaces or local kind/k3d); 6.x is a major from 5.x — schema for `images`/`dev`/`pipelines` reshaped between majors.

## Previously added (+3)

- [reviewdog](clis/reviewdog/) — universal lint-output → code-review-comment poster, one Go binary that reads any linter's stdout (errorformat / RDJSON / checkstyle / SARIF / 30+ named formats including `golangci-lint`, `eslint`, `hadolint`, `shellcheck`, `pyright`, `clippy`, `tflint`, `cppcheck`, `clang-tidy`, `phpstan`, `ansible-lint`) and posts the findings as inline review comments on a pull request — `github-pr-review` / `gitlab-mr-discussion` / `github-pr-check` / `gerrit` / `bitbucket-server` reporters share one `-reporter=` flag, so the same `golangci-lint run | reviewdog -f=golangci-lint -reporter=github-pr-review -filter-mode=added -fail-on-error` line works on GitHub, GitHub Enterprise, GitLab self-hosted, Gerrit, Bitbucket Cloud, and Bitbucket Server (reviewdog, MIT, v0.21.0). The differentiator is `-filter-mode=added` — only post comments on lines this PR actually touched, the only realistic path to incrementally turning on a strict lint rule on a 200 kloc legacy codebase without burying every PR in pre-existing-issue noise. Pick over hand-rolled `awk + curl` glue (every team writes their own; reviewdog handles multi-line findings, range diagnostics, Unicode filenames, and per-PR dedup); pick over [`pre-commit`](clis/pre-commit/) / [`lefthook`](clis/lefthook/) when CI-side enforcement matters more than local hooks (those run on the developer's laptop, reviewdog runs in CI for the contributors who skipped the hook); pick over [`pr-agent`](clis/pr-agent/) when you want *deterministic* lint findings as inline comments rather than LLM prose review (use both — LLM for design review, reviewdog for "trailing newline missing on line 47"); pair with the linter of your choice ([`golangci-lint`](clis/golangci-lint/), [`shellcheck`](clis/shellcheck/), [`hadolint`](clis/hadolint/), [`actionlint`](clis/actionlint/), [`yamlfmt`](clis/yamlfmt/), [`oxlint`](clis/oxlint/), [`ruff`](clis/ruff/), [`sqruff`](clis/sqruff/)) — reviewdog is the *delivery* layer, not the *finder* layer. Local mode (`reviewdog -reporter=local -diff="git diff origin/main"`) reuses the same filtering offline as a pre-push gate so contributors see exactly what CI will flag before they push. Caveats — `github-pr-review` needs `pull-requests: write`; on forked PRs `GITHUB_TOKEN` withholds that scope and you must either switch to `-reporter=github-pr-check` (works with default token) or accept the risk of `pull_request_target`; inline-comment placement requires correct line numbers, so test new linter integrations with `-reporter=local` first; not a full code-review platform — branch-protection rules + required-reviewer enforcement still come from the forge.
- [gofumpt](clis/gofumpt/) — strict superset of `gofmt` for Go that adds ~25 formatting rules nobody seriously disagrees with: collapses `var x int = 0` → `var x int`, removes empty leading/trailing lines in blocks, drops `else { return ... }` branches after a terminating `if`, normalizes octal literals to the `0o` prefix (`0755` → `0o755`), rewrites `time.Now().Sub(t)` → `time.Since(t)`, sorts struct tags, joins short composite literals onto one line, groups standard-library imports above third-party (mvdan, BSD-3-Clause, v0.9.2). The output is always a strict superset of `gofmt` — every file that passes `gofumpt -d` also passes `gofmt -d` — so migration is one-way, safe, and never regresses formatting; the `gopls` language server has a first-party `gofumpt: true` switch so VS Code / Neovim / JetBrains / Helix all format on save with the same rules CI enforces, and `golangci-lint` bundles `gofumpt` as an enableable linter so adopting it in a repo that already runs golangci-lint adds zero processes and zero CI minutes. Pick over `gofmt -s` (gofmt's built-in simplification mode does ~3 of gofumpt's rewrites — gofumpt is a strict superset of `gofmt -s` plus 20 more rules, no reason to run `gofmt -s` once gofumpt is in the pipeline); pick over style linters that *report* the same issues without *fixing* them ([`golangci-lint`](clis/golangci-lint/) with `gocritic` / `revive` / `stylecheck` finds these; gofumpt eliminates them — pair both); pair with [`reviewdog`](clis/reviewdog/) so any drift produces a single PR comment rather than a CI failure, with [`golines`](https://github.com/segmentio/golines) for the orthogonal long-line-wrapping problem (run golines first, then `gofumpt -w`); part of the same opinionated-formatter family as [`shfmt`](clis/shfmt/) (same author for shell), [`ruff`](clis/ruff/) format (Python), [`biome`](clis/biome/) (JS/TS), [`stylua`](clis/stylua/) (Lua), [`taplo`](clis/taplo/) (TOML), [`yamlfmt`](clis/yamlfmt/) (YAML). Caveats — migration is one-way (switching back to `gofmt` produces diff churn on every file); `gopls` users must set `gopls.formatting.gofumpt = true` or the editor formats with `gofmt` and CI reformats with gofumpt, producing a confusing diff loop; no configuration knobs (the project's stance is "if a rule is controversial enough to want disabled, don't adopt gofumpt for this codebase"); `-extra` mode is unstable — pin a specific version and don't run `-extra` in CI without pinning.
- [kluctl](clis/kluctl/) — templated, environment-aware deployment tool for Kubernetes that takes a tree of Kustomize bases + Jinja2-templated YAML + a typed deployment-project config and renders it into per-environment manifests, then applies them to a cluster with three-way diff (rendered manifests vs last-applied annotation vs current live spec, so the diff correctly identifies fields set by webhooks / other controllers / hand-edits and refuses to silently overwrite), dependency-ordered apply (`barrier: true` markers in `deployment.yaml` enforce "namespaces before secrets before workloads" with no wrapper script), and prune that you can trust (resources tagged with a project ID; `kluctl prune` deletes only what the project once managed and no longer does — no `kubectl delete -f old.yaml` choreography) — same `-t <target>` flag selects environment for `kluctl diff` / `deploy` / `prune` / `render` / `validate`, so one project source produces N environments with no per-env fork (kluctl, Apache-2.0, v2.27.0). The "templated multi-env Kubernetes deploy" gap between Helm (great for distributing third-party charts, awkward as a 10-environment values.yaml monster for in-house apps with no native diff/prune), plain Kustomize (deterministic and template-free, but bumping an image tag from CI requires `kustomize edit` mutation or wrapper scripts; no per-env arg system; no built-in diff), and pure GitOps controllers ([`flux`](clis/flux/) / [`argocd`](clis/argocd/) reconcile clusters *to* a rendered tree — kluctl produces that tree, and ships its own optional GitOps controller for the same project source so `kluctl deploy` from a laptop and the in-cluster `KluctlDeployment` CR have identical behavior). Pick over [`helm`](clis/helm/) for in-house apps with many environments (kluctl can still render+deploy Helm charts via its `helmChart:` deployment item); pick over [`kustomize`](clis/kustomize/) when you need templating + project model + diff + prune above the patch/overlay layer (kluctl uses Kustomize internally for that layer); pick over [`kpt`](clis/kpt/) when you want YAML-plus-Jinja2 in the middle ground rather than function pipelines; pair with [`tilt`](clis/tilt/) / [`skaffold`](clis/skaffold/) along the orthogonal axis (those for `kind dev` inner-loop hot-reload, kluctl for staging-onward outer-loop env promotion + drift detection); pair with [`reviewdog`](clis/reviewdog/) to post `kluctl diff -t prod-eu` as a PR comment so reviewers see exactly which production resources change before approval. Caveats — Jinja2-on-YAML is whitespace-sensitive (prefer Kustomize patches for structural changes, reserve Jinja2 for scalar substitution, always run `kluctl render` during template development); `.kluctl.yaml` + `deployment.yaml` + per-target args schema take a half-day to internalize (after which it removes a lot of friction; before then plain Kustomize feels simpler); prune relies on a project-ID label so migrating a cluster *into* kluctl management requires a one-time `kluctl deploy --replace-on-error` re-tag pass; cluster contexts are per-target (CI needs a deliberate pattern for materializing kubeconfig contexts from secrets — not unique to kluctl, worth planning before adoption).

## Previously added (+3)

- [slim](clis/slim/) — slimtoolkit/slim's container minifier that watches a running image with `slim build --http-probe` (or `--exec`, or `--continue-after enter`), traces every syscall + filesystem read + executed binary the workload actually touches during the probe window, and emits a new image with everything else stripped (slimtoolkit, Apache-2.0, v1.40.11): a 1.2 GB Node + Python + apt-laden image collapses to 30-50 MB with the same entrypoint and exposed ports but no `/bin/sh`, no `apt`, no `pip`, no language toolchain, and ~80-95 % fewer CVE findings on the post-strip [`trivy`](clis/trivy/) / [`grype`](clis/grype/) scan because the unused libraries that carried the CVEs are gone. The "we have a fat legacy image, the build is owned by another team, but we still have to ship something small + minimal-attack-surface this quarter" gap — the strip is *dynamic-trace driven*, the opposite axis from declarative builders ([`apko`](clis/apko/) / [`ko`](clis/ko/) / [`melange`](clis/melange/) construct small images from declared inputs; slim *reduces* an image you already have when rewriting the Dockerfile is not on the table). Pick over apko/ko/melange when the build pipeline is not yours to change; pick over hand-rolled multi-stage Dockerfiles when the dependency surface is too tangled to enumerate by hand and an exec-trace is the realistic path; pair with [`dive`](clis/dive/) (visualise the layer-by-layer bytes slim removed), [`syft`](clis/syft/) (the post-slim SBOM is the small one), [`crane`](clis/crane/) / [`skopeo`](clis/skopeo/) (push the slimmed image to the destination registry), and [`cosign`](clis/cosign/) (sign the slimmed image, not the bloated source). Caveats — the strip is only as good as the probe coverage (a code path the probes never exercise gets removed; the right test is "deploy the slimmed image to a staging environment and run the full smoke suite", not "trust the build"); HTTP probes default to common health endpoints but real apps often need `--http-probe-cmd` per route; multi-process / sidecar / cron-driven workloads need explicit `--include-path` for the lazily-loaded files; v1.40.11 is the Feb 2024 release with the project still active on GitHub but with a slower cadence than the apko/ko line — pin and re-evaluate if the upstream goes quiet for >12 months; Apache-2.0 is unrestricted including for slimming images you ship to customers.
- [chisel](clis/chisel/) — single Go binary that is both server and client of a TCP/UDP tunnel multiplexed over an authenticated SSH session wrapped in HTTP/WebSocket (jpillora, MIT, v1.11.5): `chisel server --port 80 --auth user:pass` on a public box, `chisel client --auth user:pass http://server:80 8080:internal-host:5432` on a laptop, and the laptop now reaches `internal-host:5432` through `localhost:8080` over a single outbound HTTP/WS flow that any corporate proxy / cloud LB / k8s ingress already permits — reverse tunnels (`R:0.0.0.0:8080:localhost:3000`) expose a service from inside a NAT to the internet without opening a port, SOCKS5 mode (`socks`) turns the tunnel into an authenticated proxy for browser / curl traffic, fingerprint-pinned host-key verification (`--fingerprint`) makes the SSH handshake non-MITMable without a CA, and the client auto-reconnects with exponential backoff so a flaky cafe wifi does not require operator restart. The "the network only allows HTTP/HTTPS egress and `ssh -L` is blocked" gap — pick over `ssh -L` / `ssh -R` when the wire format must look like HTTP/WebSocket to satisfy a corporate proxy or cloud LB; pick over [`bore`](clis/bore/) when the protocol surface needs reverse tunnels + SOCKS5 + fingerprint pinning, not just forward TCP (bore is intentionally minimal — pick bore when "expose one local port via one command" is the entire requirement); pick over [`cloudflared`](clis/cloudflared/) when you do not want a Cloudflare account in the trust path (cloudflared anchors at Cloudflare's edge — operationally simpler for the laptop side but adds Cloudflare to the threat model; chisel needs only a VPS you control). Pair with [`tailscale`](clis/tailscale/) / [`headscale`](clis/headscale/) along the orthogonal axis (mesh VPN for fleet-wide steady state; chisel for ad-hoc point-to-point tunnels through hostile networks); pair with [`caddy`](clis/caddy/) in front of the chisel server for ACME-managed TLS on the public side. Caveats — the auth model is shared-secret HTTP basic over the WebSocket handshake, fine for a small team but use `--keygen` + `--authfile` for per-user keys at scale; `--reverse` opens an inbound forward from the *server* side and so makes the public box an attack surface — review the `R:` forwards as carefully as any nginx vhost; chisel is intentionally not a VPN (no virtual interface, no broadcast / multicast, no mesh routing — one TCP/UDP forward per `R:`/`L:` declaration); MIT — unrestricted including for embedding into a closed-source product.
- [noti](clis/noti/) — "tell me when this long command finishes" notification primitive in one ~6 MB Go binary (variadico, MIT, v3.8.0): three forms — `noti make release` wraps a command, `make release; noti` notifies on the previous command's exit, `make release | noti` notifies on pipe EOF — and the resulting notification fires through native macOS Notification Center / Linux `notify-send` / Windows toast by default and can fan out to Slack / Pushover / Telegram / Pushbullet / Zulip / BearyChat / generic webhooks via one `NOTI_DEFAULT="banner pushover"` env var, with the exit status and runtime templated into the message so the lock-screen banner tells you whether to come back and triage. The 80 % use case is a 10-line `precmd`/`preexec` zsh hook (or `trap DEBUG` + `PROMPT_COMMAND` in bash) that auto-fires a notification for *any* command exceeding `NOTI_THRESHOLD=30` seconds, reclaiming the wall-clock minutes otherwise lost between "command finished" and "human noticed" — long agent runs (`noti opencode run "..."`), eval batches (`noti python -m my_eval`), model downloads (`noti ollama pull <large-model>`), and CI watching (`gh pr checks --watch | noti`) all become walk-away tasks. The "agent / eval / build is going to take 10+ minutes and I want my attention back" gap none of the catalog's other primitives cover at this granularity. Pick over [`pueue`](clis/pueue/) when you want notification not queueing (pueue is a job queue with history + dependencies + parallelism caps; the two compose — `pueue add -- noti long-job` enqueues *and* pings on completion); pick over [`gum`](clis/gum/) `gum spin` when the command is walk-away not interactive (gum is a foreground spinner; noti is a fire-and-forget post-completion ping); pick over native `terminal-notifier` (macOS-only) / `notify-send` (Linux-only) when you want one binary across both platforms plus optional remote backends. Caveats — remote backend tokens (`NOTI_PUSHOVER_TOKEN` / `NOTI_SLACK_TOKEN` / `NOTI_TELEGRAM_TOKEN`) are bearer credentials, store them in a shell-secret manager not a checked-in dotfiles repo; Linux desktop backend depends on `notify-send` from `libnotify` (headless servers must use a remote backend); macOS Notification Center silently drops banners if terminal app notification permission is denied; notifications are fire-and-forget — `noti` does not retry on network failure (use a backend with server-side queueing like Pushover for best-effort retries); v3.8.0 is March 2025 with the repo still active on GitHub through May 2026 — slow cadence reflects feature completeness, pin and re-evaluate annually; MIT — unrestricted including for embedding into team `rcfile` repos, CI runner images, or proprietary developer toolkits.

## Previously added (+3)

- [flox](clis/flox/) — Rust + Nix developer-environment manager that wraps the full ~120k-package nixpkgs catalog behind a `flox install ripgrep node@22 postgresql@16` / `flox activate` workflow with a checked-in `manifest.toml` + `manifest.lock` (flox, GPL-2.0, v1.11.4): the same env that `flox activate` drops you into is shareable as a *catalog* publish (`flox push` → FloxHub or self-hosted catalog → `flox pull` on a teammate's laptop / CI runner / container layer), and `flox containerize -o myapp:latest` exports the same env as a minimal OCI image so the dev shell, CI runner, and deployed container all materialise from one manifest + lock pair. The "Nix is the right answer to reproducible dev environments but nobody on the team will write Nix" gap — pick over [`mise`](clis/mise/) / [`asdf`](clis/asdf/) when the toolchain spans *system libraries* (postgres, ffmpeg, imagemagick, openssl with pinned ABI) not just language runtimes; pick over [`devbox`](clis/devbox/) when GPL-2.0 + the more polished CLI surface is acceptable (devbox is Apache-2.0 with a fully-DIY catalog model — both wrap Nix, the choice is mostly catalog hosting + licensing posture); pick over plain `nix` when the team will not write Nix expressions; pair with [`direnv`](clis/direnv/) (one-line `.envrc` `use flox` auto-activates on `cd`). Caveats — GPL-2.0 on the CLI is fine for *use* but constrains redistributing a modified binary inside a closed-source product; FloxHub is a commercial hosted service with a free tier (self-host the catalog server is supported but is real ops surface; public nixpkgs always works without FloxHub); first install on a fresh laptop pulls a Nix store layer (hundreds of MB to a few GB — pin or pre-warm); macOS + Linux first-class, Windows is WSL2-only.
