- [nixpacks](clis/nixpacks/) — Buildpacks-style build orchestrator that takes an app source directory with no `Dockerfile` / `Procfile` / buildpack declaration and produces a runnable OCI image by detecting the language + framework, resolving runtime + system packages from a pinned `nixpkgs` revision, and emitting a multi-stage `Dockerfile` BuildKit then turns into the final image (railwayapp, MIT, v1.41.0): two key commands — `nixpacks build ./app -t myapp:latest` (one-shot to a local Docker image) and `nixpacks plan ./app` (print the JSON build plan, useful for inspection / CI gating). Detection covers ~25 ecosystems out of the box (Node with framework hints for Next/Remix/Astro/SvelteKit, Python with Django/Flask/FastAPI hints, Go, Rust, Ruby/Rails, PHP, Java, Deno, Bun, Elixir, Crystal, Haskell, Zig, Swift, Dart, Clojure, .NET, more) with three escape hatches: `nixpacks.toml`, `NIXPACKS_*` env vars, or `--build-cmd` / `--start-cmd` / `--pkgs` flags. The "the agent generated a working app, now containerise it" gap — pick over a hand-rolled Dockerfile for the *first* containerisation pass (rarely the smallest image, ~150-500 MB, but correct on day one); pick over [`pack`](clis/pack/) when one binary with no buildpack registry / builder image is the win and Nix-pinned system packages matter; pick over [`ko`](clis/ko/) / [`apko`](clis/apko/) for *full polyglot apps* needing a build phase + system packages (ko/apko are sharper for "Go binary into distroless" / "declarative apk image"); pair with [`dive`](clis/dive/) to inspect the result, with [`cosign`](clis/cosign/) for signing. Caveats — image size is not optimised for minimum bytes (read the generated Dockerfile when size matters); BuildKit (Docker 18.09+ with `DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1` or `docker buildx`) is required at build time; auto-detect occasionally guesses wrong on monorepos with multiple apps in subdirs (pass explicit subpath, use `nixpacks.toml`); pin nixpacks itself in CI so the embedded nixpkgs revision stays reproducible across builds; Linux containers only.
- [uutils-coreutils](clis/uutils-coreutils/) — from-scratch Rust rewrite of GNU coreutils (`ls`, `cp`, `mv`, `rm`, `cat`, `cut`, `sort`, `uniq`, `tr`, `wc`, `head`, `tail`, `dd`, `du`, `df`, `stat`, `printf`, `seq`, `tac`, `nl`, `paste`, `comm`, `join`, `split`, `od`, `hexdump`, ~100 utilities total) shipped as one multicall `coreutils` binary plus per-utility binaries (uutils, MIT, 0.8.0): the project is the **default coreutils on Ubuntu 25.10 and the planned default for Ubuntu 26.04 LTS**, validated against the GNU project's own test suite (>90% pass rate across most utilities), with bug-for-bug parity on flag semantics + exit codes the wire-level goal so existing `Makefile` / shell-script / ansible invocations port over with zero edits in the common case. The "Rust memory safety in the TCB of every shell pipeline" gap with three concrete payoffs — statically-linkable `cargo install --target x86_64-unknown-linux-musl coreutils` produces one ~5 MB blob suitable as the entire userland of a `FROM scratch` container; cross-platform Linux/macOS/Windows/WASI in a way GNU's Cygwin port has never quite achieved; embeddable as a Rust library crate so a build tool can call `uucore::ls::uumain(...)` directly. Pick over GNU coreutils when the deploy target is `FROM scratch` / minimal embedded (glibc + GNU coreutils is more bytes than the app), the target is Windows or macOS and you want one set of flags, the security posture demands "no C in the init-script TCB", or the distro is Ubuntu 25.10+ (don't fight it); pick over [BusyBox](https://busybox.net) / Toybox when memory safety + GNU-flag-compat matter more than absolute minimum size (BusyBox is ~1 MB with a non-GNU dialect for size; uutils is ~5-15 MB and matches GNU); pair with [`apko`](clis/apko/) for the `FROM scratch` userland-in-one-binary container build, with [`syft`](clis/syft/) / [`grype`](clis/grype/) for the resulting tiny SBOM. Caveats — compatibility is very high but not 100% (known gaps in some `dd` `conv=` modes, locale-aware `sort` collation under non-trivial `LC_COLLATE`, `cp --reflink=always` semantics, some SELinux/xattr/ACL flags — read the per-utility status table before flipping a production-critical script); switching the default `/usr/bin/ls` etc. on a non-Ubuntu-25.10 distro is real operational risk (install side-by-side as `uu-*` first).

## Previously added (+3)

- [aqua](clis/aqua/) — declarative CLI version manager whose source of truth is an `aqua.yaml` checked into the repo (aquaproj, MIT, v2.57.2): each entry pins a tool to an exact upstream release (`kubernetes/kubectl@v1.31.2`), the resolver fetches the binary into a content-addressed cache, verifies SHA-256 + optional Cosign signature + optional SLSA provenance, and exec's it through a per-tool *shim* binary on `PATH` so `cd ~/repos/foo && kubectl version` runs the version foo's `aqua.yaml` declares with no shell-hook PATH-mutation tax. The "polyglot CLI tool sprawl needs the same per-repo pinning + supply-chain verification language runtimes already get" gap — the community **standard registry** ships ~1,800 curated package definitions with checksum URLs and version-overrides so adding a tool is `aqua g -i kubernetes/kubectl` not hand-writing a manifest, and the registry-as-curated-allowlist model is the contribution `mise` and `asdf` notably do not have. Pick over [`mise`](clis/mise/) for the ~1,800 binary CLIs (mise for *language runtimes* — node / python / go / rust toolchains; aqua for everything sitting on top); pick over [`pkgx`](clis/pkgx/) when the manifest *is* the source of truth (pkgx is invocation-scoped with no committed file); pick over [`asdf`](clis/asdf/) when the plugin sprawl + shell-hook PATH switching has become the cost (aqua replaces a plugin per tool with one registry + shim binaries). Pair with [`cosign`](clis/cosign/) and [`slsa-verifier`](clis/slsa-verifier/) (aqua verifies signatures and provenance natively); pair with [`mise`](clis/mise/) along the orthogonal axis. Caveats — the shim-binary model means `aqua i` must run before the first invocation in a fresh checkout (or `--install` policy in CI); the policy file (`aqua policy init`) is the right gate for "trust the upstream signing key" but needs a one-time review; per-package signature verification is opt-in per registry entry and not yet uniform across all ~1,800 packages.
- [cargo-llvm-cov](clis/cargo-llvm-cov/) — source-based code coverage for Rust via LLVM's instrumentation profile run as a `cargo` subcommand (taiki-e, MIT/Apache-2.0, v0.8.5): builds with `-C instrument-coverage`, runs `cargo test` (or `cargo nextest`, or any `--` passthrough binary), collects the resulting `.profraw` files, calls the `llvm-profdata` and `llvm-cov` binaries that ship with the *exact* `rustc` toolchain in use (resolved via `rustup component add llvm-tools-preview`, guaranteeing a profile-data-format match the way a system `llvm-cov` does not), and writes HTML / LCOV / Cobertura / JSON / text reports. The "agent-generated Rust code under-tests error paths and a line-coverage gate is the cheapest mechanical check" gap — `--fail-under-lines 80` is a one-flag CI assertion that needs no Rust-specific reviewer expertise to enforce, and `cargo llvm-cov nextest` is the strict upgrade for any project running [`cargo-nextest`](clis/cargo-nextest/) (faster runner + per-test process isolation while still collecting profile data). Pick over `cargo-tarpaulin` (`ptrace`-based, drifts on macOS / Windows; `cargo-llvm-cov` is platform-uniform because it uses the same LLVM instrumentation Clang/Swift use) and over `grcov` (the older Mozilla-derived path now mostly subsumed; cargo-llvm-cov is what Rust-project maintainers themselves recommend for new setups). Pair with [`cargo-mutants`](clis/cargo-mutants/) on the orthogonal axis (line coverage tells you whether the line *ran*; mutation testing tells you whether running the line *mattered* to any assertion) — a serious Rust CI runs nextest as the runner, cargo-llvm-cov as the coverage gate on every PR, cargo-mutants as the deeper nightly job. Caveats — `llvm-tools-preview` component must be installed once per toolchain; `--branch` and `--mcdc` need recent rustc nightly; very large workspaces benefit from the split `--no-report` + `cargo llvm-cov report` for caching the test step separately from the report render.
- [hugo](clis/hugo/) — fast Go static-site generator (gohugoio, Apache-2.0, v0.161.1): one binary parses front-matter (YAML / TOML / JSON) on Markdown content, applies Go `html/template` templates organised by section + kind + type through a documented lookup chain, and writes a fully-baked static site to `public/` in a single aggressively-parallel pass; a 10,000-page documentation site routinely builds in under 10 seconds on a laptop, the property that distinguishes hugo from Jekyll (Ruby, slow), Gatsby (Node + React SSR, slow + flaky), and Eleventy (Node, faster but still single-threaded). Built-in features the comparable tools either lack or treat as plugins — full-text search via JSON output, taxonomies with auto-generated listing pages and per-facet RSS, multilingual sites with translation linkage, image processing with on-disk caching, asset bundling + minification + fingerprinting + SRI, Sass/SCSS via embedded `dart-sass`, shortcodes, sub-millisecond live-reload `hugo server`. The "agent regenerates 200 pages overnight and the publishing step has to stay sub-10s or the inner loop breaks" gap — Node-based generators with deep plugin chains break under transitive yanks and the agent cannot tell whether its content edit or `npm`'s tree shake broke the build; hugo's deploy artefact is one statically-linked Go binary's output, served by any HTTP server / CDN / object store with no Node runtime and no `node_modules`. Pick over [`mdbook`](clis/mdbook/) for *site*-shaped collections with sections + tags + taxonomies (mdbook for *book*-shaped docs with one linear `SUMMARY.md` ordering — narrow + deep; hugo for broad + shallow; many orgs run both); pick over [`zola`](clis/zola/) when the wider plugin/theme ecosystem matters more than "smaller and opinionated"; pair with [`marker`](clis/marker/) (marker → Markdown ingestion of legacy PDFs → hugo build to publish) and complementary to [`pandoc`](clis/pandoc/) (pandoc transforms one document; hugo builds an entire site of cross-linked documents). Caveats — Go template syntax has a learning curve next to Liquid / Jinja; the "extended" variant is required for Sass / WebP processing (Homebrew installs it by default; the `go install` path needs `-tags extended`); theme + module versioning lives in the Hugo Modules system on top of `go.mod` semantics, which is one extra concept to learn.

## Recently added (+3)

- [vault](clis/vault/) — HashiCorp's self-hosted secrets-management server + CLI in one Go binary (hashicorp, BUSL-1.1, v2.0.0): an HA daemon (Raft-integrated storage by default since v1.4) that stores secrets encrypted at rest, gates every read through a per-token policy engine, and emits a tamper-evident audit log; the differentiator vs an "encrypted KV" is *dynamic secrets* — `vault read database/creds/app-readonly` mints a fresh Postgres user with a 1h lease on demand instead of handing back a pre-stored password, the `pki` engine mints short-lived TLS leaf certs from an internal CA, the `aws`/`gcp`/`azure` engines mint short-lived cloud STS sessions, the `transit` engine does encryption-as-a-service for application data, and the `ssh` engine signs short-lived SSH client certs against a Vault-rooted SSH CA. Auth is pluggable too — `kubernetes` (the pod's service-account JWT becomes the Vault identity), `approle` (canonical machine auth), `oidc` (operator SSO), `aws`/`gcp`/`azure` IAM, `userpass`, `cert`, `ldap`, `jwt` are all built-in. Pick over [`sops`](clis/sops/) + [`age`](clis/age/) when secrets are *many, short-lived, machine-generated, and the audit log is a compliance requirement* (sops wins when secrets are *few, slow-moving, and a checked-in encrypted file plus IAM on the KMS key is the threat model*); pick over [`teller`](clis/teller/) at the layer where secrets are *generated and stored*, not where they are *aggregated and injected* (teller is the developer-laptop / CI front-end on top of a Vault backend); pair with [`step-cli`](clis/step-cli/) when PKI is the only thing you need (step's smaller surface beats Vault's full plugin stack for that single use). Caveats — license is **BUSL-1.1**, not OSI-approved (each release flips to MPL-2.0 four years later; if your legal team requires OSI, use [OpenBao](https://github.com/openbao/openbao), the LF MPL-2.0 fork from the last pre-BUSL release); unseal is real operational surface (use auto-unseal via cloud KMS in production, Shamir manual unseal is for dev clusters and DR only); tokens are bearer credentials so use short TTLs and prefer AppRole/workload-identity for machines; audit log is mandatory for compliance and Vault refuses requests if all configured audit devices are failing — configure ≥2; storage backend choice is permanent in practice, pick Raft for new deployments. BUSL-1.1 — the Go *client SDK* (`github.com/hashicorp/vault/api`) remains MPL-2.0; only the server and CLI are BUSL.
- [nebula](clis/nebula/) — peer-to-peer overlay mesh network from Slack built on the Noise Protocol Framework (slackhq, MIT, v1.10.3): every host runs the `nebula` Go binary which opens a single UDP socket, exchanges Noise IK handshakes with peers, and exposes a virtual `nebula1` interface with a fixed private IP from a CIDR you control; once two hosts have handshaken, traffic flows *directly* between them — no central relay sits in the data path. Identity is a `host.crt` issued offline by `nebula-cert` against a `ca.crt` you generated once; each cert binds a hostname, a virtual IP, and a list of *groups* (`db`, `web`, `prod`, `laptop`, …) that the host firewall config (rules look like `allow tcp 5432 from group:web to group:db`) keys off — so a compromised laptop cert cannot reach `group:db` even if the network would otherwise route the packet. The control plane is 2–4 `lighthouse` nodes whose only job is to answer "what is host X's current public ip:port?" so two NATed peers can hole-punch; the lighthouse never sees traffic content. Pick over [`tailscale`](clis/tailscale/) / [`headscale`](clis/headscale/) when you want a *fully self-hosted* control plane out of the box and per-host firewall as the *primary* policy surface (tailscale wins on UX, IdP-SSO onboarding, and MagicDNS — pick that for small teams; nebula wins on full self-hosting and explicit group-based identity at fleet scale); pick over [`cloudflared`](clis/cloudflared/) / [`frp`](clis/frp/) / [`bore`](clis/bore/) when you want a *mesh* not a tunnel (every node both client and server). Pair with [`step-cli`](clis/step-cli/) when you want a more flexible CA than `nebula-cert` for issuing host certs from CI; pair with [`vault`](clis/vault/) to seal-store the offline CA private key. Caveats — the CA private key is the entire trust root (offline storage in a hardware token or sealed Vault blob is the bar); cert lifetime is your renewal cadence (30–90d short-lived certs + automated renewal beat year-long certs without a CRL); NAT traversal works but is not magic (symmetric NAT on both ends falls back to the v1.7+ relay or fails — plan ≥1 relay-capable peer per region); no MagicDNS / no service discovery (you get stable internal IPs, not names — layer your own DNS on top); firewall is *deny by default* once you write any rule (allow icmp during bring-up). MIT — safe to bundle into commercial products and customer-side appliances.
- [mage](clis/mage/) — "Make for Go projects, written in Go" task runner (magefile, Apache-2.0, v1.17.2): instead of a `Makefile` whose recipes are shell snippets glued together with tabs, you write `magefile.go` — a normal Go source file in package `main` with the `//go:build mage` build tag — and every exported function becomes a callable target (`mage build` runs `func Build()`, `mage release v1.2.3` runs `func Release(version string)` with positional args type-coerced from the CLI). Targets can take `context.Context` (so `-t 30s` cancellation propagates), return `error` (mage prints and exits non-zero), declare deps via `mg.Deps(Build, Lint)` / `mg.SerialDeps` (memoized per process — diamond graphs run each node once), and group into namespaces by hanging methods off a `mg.Namespace`-typed struct (`mage docker:build`). The `sh` helper package gives you `sh.Run("go", "build", "./...")` / `sh.RunV` / `sh.Output` / `sh.RunWith(env, …)` so you don't wrap every shell call in `os/exec` boilerplate; the `target` package gives you Make-style `target.Path(out, deps...)` and `target.Glob(out, "src/**/*.go")` staleness checks; `mage -compile ./bin/build` produces a self-contained build binary you ship to CI runners that don't have mage or Go installed. Pick over [`task`](clis/task/) / [`just`](clis/just/) when the project is *Go-native* and you want type-checked, IDE-debuggable build code (task wins for polyglot repos with declarative YAML config; just wins for small to medium projects in any language with a more readable shell-flavored DSL); pair with [`goreleaser`](clis/goreleaser/) inside `func Release()` for the actual cross-compile + tarball + GitHub-release step (mage invokes goreleaser; you don't reimplement it), with [`gotestsum`](clis/gotestsum/) inside `func Test()` for nicer test output, and with [`golangci-lint`](clis/golangci-lint/) inside `func Lint()`; reach for [`earthly`](clis/earthly/) or [`dagger`](clis/dagger/) when you need containerized hermetic builds (mage runs on the host). Caveats — magefiles need the `//go:build mage` build tag (forgetting it is the #1 first-time foot-gun — the file ends up compiled into your application binary or breaks `go test`); compilation cache lives in `$MAGEFILE_CACHE` (default `~/.magefile`) so ephemeral CI runners pay the ~1–3s compile cost on every run unless you cache that directory or use `mage -compile`; `mg.Deps` runs in parallel by default (use `mg.SerialDeps` for targets that mutate the working tree); errors from `sh.Run` swallow command stderr unless you use `sh.RunV` or capture with `sh.Output`; many teams put magefiles in a separate `magefiles/` subdir with its own `go.mod` so build deps don't leak into the production module graph. Apache-2.0 — safe to vendor into commercial projects and to invoke from CI.

## Recently added (+3)

- [tenv](clis/tenv/) — single-binary version manager that owns the *whole* HashiCorp / OpenTofu IaC toolchain (tofuutils, Apache-2.0, v4.10.0): one Go binary covers OpenTofu / Terraform / Terragrunt / Terramate / Atmos with one shim layer, one config schema (`.tool-versions`, `.terraform-version`, `.opentofu-version`, or HCL `required_version` parsed straight out of the root module), and one upgrade path; signature verification is on by default end-to-end (HashiCorp PGP, OpenTofu / Terragrunt / Terramate cosign keyless attestations) and tenv refuses unsigned binaries unless you explicitly `--skip-signature`. The "Terraform → OpenTofu fork has split shops into mixed-binary repos overnight, and the four-tool world of `tfenv` / `tofuenv` / `tgenv` / a hand-rolled wrapper is now the wrong default" gap — none of the existing catalog entries solve IaC version management as a first-class problem with HCL `required_version` resolution baked in; [`asdf`](clis/asdf/) and [`mise`](clis/mise/) cover hundreds of plugins broadly but do not parse the module's `required_version` block, do not signature-verify by default, and add a plugin shim layer that has historically misbehaved on Terraform / OpenTofu specifically. Pick over `asdf` / `mise` when the repo is IaC-only (lighter, signature-verified, HCL-aware); pair with [`tflint`](clis/tflint/), [`tfsec`](clis/tfsec/), and [`infracost`](clis/infracost/) downstream of the version-pin step; pair with [`pulumi`](clis/pulumi/) only as comparison (different IaC engine entirely). Caveats — set `TENV_AUTO_INSTALL=true` so first invocation in a fresh checkout fetches the pinned version instead of erroring; the shim layer in `~/.tenv/bin` must come before any leftover `/usr/local/bin/terraform` on PATH; signature verification needs upstream PGP / cosign attestation reachability (mirror keys for air-gapped CI); HCL `required_version` resolution is strict by design and will error rather than silently pick something close. Apache-2.0 — safe to redistribute inside internal developer-platform images and customer-shipped IaC bundles.
- [mirrord](clis/mirrord/) — "run your laptop process *as if it were* a pod in your remote Kubernetes cluster" tool (metalbear-co, MIT, 3.155.0): a small dynamic library injected via `LD_PRELOAD` / `DYLD_INSERT_LIBRARIES` intercepts every `connect` / `bind` / `accept` / `read` / `write` / `open` / `getaddrinfo` / env-var lookup in the local process and routes it over a gRPC tunnel to a `mirrord-agent` ephemeral container scheduled into the target's namespace, which performs the syscall *from inside the cluster network namespace, with the target pod's service account, and against the target pod's mounted secrets*; net effect is your local debugger-attached binary sees in-cluster DNS, the same env vars and secret files as the real pod, the same internal Kafka / Redis / Postgres the pod can hit, and (optionally) receives mirrored copies of every request the real pod is currently serving (default — does not disrupt the real pod) or steals the traffic outright with header / path filtering. The "the cluster's network and secrets *are* the test environment, but the build → push → deploy → wait → curl → repeat loop is destroying the day" gap — the closest comparison is [`telepresence`](clis/telepresence/) (CNCF Sandbox, VPN-style traffic agent + intercepts model, host-level DNS rewriting, long-running sessions); mirrord is newer, ships per-process via `LD_PRELOAD` so two services on the same laptop can target two different clusters with no host-level state, needs no DNS / route changes, and has finer-grained controls over what is mirrored vs stolen vs read locally. Pick over [`tilt`](clis/tilt/) / [`skaffold`](clis/skaffold/) for the *one* service you are actively editing (those optimize the deploy step; mirrord eliminates it for that one service); pick over [`kubefwd`](clis/kubefwd/) / `kubectl port-forward` when one TCP forward is not enough and you need full cluster network + DNS + env + secrets + inbound traffic in scope. Caveats — needs RBAC for ephemeral containers (or pod create+delete) in the target namespace; `--steal` against a production-adjacent target *will* send live requests to your laptop (always pair with `--steal-filter`); `LD_PRELOAD` injection has known compatibility edges (statically linked Go without CGO, musl-static Rust, macOS SIP-protected binaries — `--copy-target` mode runs your binary inside the agent instead of locally); local file writes default to local-only by design. MIT — safe to redistribute the CLI; the hosted "mirrord for Teams" RBAC operator is a separate commercial product but the OSS CLI is fully usable standalone.
- [figlet](clis/figlet/) — the canonical 30-year-old C banner-text generator that turns a string into multi-line ASCII-art block letters by composing per-character glyph blocks from a `.flf` font file (cmatsuoka mirror, BSD-3-Clause, 2.2.5): single C source file, no external deps, one binary, ~430 community fonts in the [figlet font DB](http://www.figlet.org/fontdb.cgi), output is plain printable ASCII so it survives copy-paste, log aggregators, plaintext email, screenshot OCR, and every terminal in existence going back to a VT100. The "make this short string visually unmistakable in a terminal scrollback / CI log / MOTD / release notes header" gap — the modern terminal-styling tools cover *different* problems: [`gum`](clis/gum/) `gum style` gives you a polished modern terminal card with Unicode borders and ANSI color sized for one line, [`boxes`](clis/boxes/) wraps arbitrary text in an ASCII border, [`glow`](clis/glow/) renders Markdown, [`vhs`](clis/vhs/) records terminal sessions; figlet is the specific job of "make the *text itself* huge in plain ASCII" so a CI step printing `figlet "DEPLOY PROD"` before the prod stage cannot be missed in scrollback, an SSH MOTD greets every session with the host's role in 4-line block letters, and a release-notes file opens with `figlet "v2.10.0"` as a clear visual anchor. Pick over `gum style` when the consumer is plaintext (logs, email, OCR'd screenshots) not a Unicode+truecolor terminal; pair with [`boxes`](clis/boxes/) (`figlet 'RELEASE' | boxes -d stone`) and with `lolcat` for color; reach for `toilet` (the GPL-2.0 descendant by the `cacaview` author, ships in most distros) when you want Unicode color glyphs and `.flf`-compatible fonts. Caveats — 2.2.5 is the last release of the canonical reference codebase (2012, mature not abandoned, format and CLI flags stable for 30 years); default output is monochrome ASCII (no Unicode, no ANSI color); pin `-w` explicitly in scripted use because terminal width assumptions vary between log viewers; do not rely on figlet output as a structured-log marker (one banner becomes 6 lines or one giant line depending on the parser); font path is install-dependent (`figlet -I2` prints the directory it actually searches). BSD-3-Clause — safe to bundle into release scripts, Docker base images, and CI runner images; some `.flf` font files have their own headers' licenses if you redistribute fonts.

## Recently added (+3)

- [zarf](clis/zarf/) — declarative packager + installer for Kubernetes workloads built around the air-gapped / disconnected / DMZ deployment case (zarf-dev, Apache-2.0, v0.75.1): one Go binary, two-phase model — on a connected build host `zarf package create` reads a `zarf.yaml` declaring `components` (Helm charts + raw manifests + container images + Git repos + arbitrary OCI artifacts + shell `actions:`) and emits a single signed content-addressed `zarf-package-*.tar.zst` tarball that bundles every image and chart and repo it transitively references; on a disconnected target cluster `zarf init` installs an in-cluster registry + Gitea + a mutating webhook that rewrites image refs, then `zarf package deploy ...` pushes every bundled image into the in-cluster registry and applies every manifest with zero internet egress. The "this cluster does not have unrestricted egress to docker.io / quay.io / ghcr.io / GitHub" gap none of the catalog's reconcilers fill — [`flux`](clis/flux/) and [`argocd`](clis/argocd/) assume connectivity; Zarf is the *bootstrap and supply* layer beneath them, so once Zarf installs Gitea in-cluster you can point Flux/Argo at that in-cluster Gitea and reconcile fully offline. Pick over plain [`helm`](clis/helm/) + [`helmfile`](clis/helmfile/) the moment the cluster cannot reach the public registries (Helm + Helmfile is enough until the network changes); pick over [`skopeo`](clis/skopeo/) `sync` when you need *cluster-shaped collections* (images + manifests + charts + CRDs + seed Git repos + install hooks from one tarball), not just image-mirroring; the same package, byte-for-byte, deploys to a laptop k3d cluster and a literally-airgapped lab. Caveats — installs an opinionated in-cluster support stack (registry + Gitea + admission webhook; point at an existing private registry with `--registry-url` to skip), package size grows quickly (a platform bundle is routinely 5-15 GB; zstd-compressed inside the tarball — see [`zstd`](clis/zstd/)), image refs in deployed manifests are *rewritten* by the webhook to point at the in-cluster registry (this is the magic that makes packages portable but it surprises operators staring at `kubectl describe pod`), the project moved from `defenseunicorns/zarf` to the vendor-neutral `zarf-dev/zarf` org when it joined the CNCF Sandbox in 2024 — pin the new import path. CNCF Sandbox project as of 2024.
- [gowall](clis/gowall/) — single-binary CLI that re-colors images to match a named color palette and runs the adjacent ricer-image chores ImageMagick was overkill for (Achno, MIT, v0.2.4): one ~5 MB Go binary — `gowall convert wallpaper.jpg -t catppuccin` maps every pixel to the nearest color in the Catppuccin palette using a configurable algorithm (`--algorithm nearest|riemersma|floyd-steinberg`, the last two being true error-diffusion ditherers that preserve perceptual gradients on photographic input where naive nearest-color quantization shows visible banding), with about 30 ricer-grade palettes built-in (Catppuccin / Gruvbox / Nord / Tokyo Night / Dracula / Solarized / Rosé Pine / Everforest / Monokai / Material Ocean) plus user-defined palettes in `~/.config/gowall/config.yml`; the same binary covers `gowall extract image.jpg` (k-means in CIELAB pulls a representative palette out of an arbitrary image, output as hex + a swatch preview), `gowall pipeline image.jpg --pipeline grayscale,blur=10,brighten=20` (resize / crop / rotate / flip / blur / sharpen / brighten / contrast / invert / sepia / pixelate / border / shadow / Real-ESRGAN upscale chained into one read/write pass), `gowall mosaic *.jpg --rows 3 --cols 4` (contact sheet), `gowall qrcode "..."` (palette-colored QR), and parallel-batch with `-d ./out`. The "I want every wallpaper / screenshot / theme asset to read in the same palette as my shell + editor + window manager" gap — none of the catalog's image tools cover palette-mapping as a one-liner ([`chafa`](clis/chafa/) renders, [`gifsicle`](clis/gifsicle/) is GIF-only, [`silicon`](clis/silicon/) does code-to-PNG); gowall is the focused recolor + reshape primitive a ricing setup standardizes on. Pick over reaching for `magick` whenever the operation is "match this image to this palette" or "extract a palette from this image" — gowall is one flag, ImageMagick is a script. Caveats — recolor quality depends heavily on `--algorithm` (default nearest is poster-style and bad for photos; use `floyd-steinberg` or `riemersma` for any photographic input), the Real-ESRGAN upscale step is *not* bundled (shells out to `realesrgan-ncnn-vulkan`), no HEIC / AVIF input as of v0.2.x (convert to PNG first), built-in palette names are case-sensitive and rotate as upstream theme projects rename flavors (pin both gowall version and palette name in dotfiles).
- [xcaddy](clis/xcaddy/) — official custom-build tool for the Caddy web server, the seam that makes [`caddy`](clis/caddy/) actually deployable in production where stock modules are not enough (caddyserver, Apache-2.0, v0.4.5): one static Go binary — `xcaddy build v2.10.0 --with github.com/caddy-dns/cloudflare --with github.com/mholt/caddy-ratelimit --with example.com/me/mymodule` reads the requested Caddy version, fetches each `--with` module (any Go module path works, with optional `@version` pin and optional `=local/path` override for module development), generates a tiny `main.go` that imports them all, runs `go build`, and drops a single ~50 MB static `caddy` binary in cwd that has those modules baked in (full Caddyfile / admin API / `caddy reload` parity with a stock download, just with the extra modules linked in); the second mode `xcaddy run --config Caddyfile` is the dev loop for writing your *own* Caddy module — from inside the module's source tree it builds a temporary Caddy that imports the local package via a Go module replace, runs it under your Caddyfile, and re-builds + restarts on file change. The "stock Caddy ships a fixed module set, but every production deploy needs at least one external module" gap — almost any deploy that does ACME-DNS for wildcard certs needs a DNS-provider module that lives outside core (Cloudflare, Route53, DigitalOcean, Hetzner, Gandi, ~70 more), and any non-default storage backend (Redis / S3 / Postgres / Consul / etcd) or auth / rate-limit / transform module is the same story; xcaddy is the only supported way to get those without forking Caddy. Pick over a hand-rolled `go build` of a vendored `cmd/caddy/main.go` when you want the official path with reproducible module pins and zero `go.mod` surgery. Pair with [`apko`](clis/apko/) / [`ko`](clis/ko/) to wrap the resulting `caddy` binary in a minimal container image, and with [`mise`](clis/mise/) to pin the Go toolchain version that performs the build. Caveats — the build is *only* as reproducible as your `go.sum` and `GOPROXY` (pin every `--with` module to an exact version, set `GOFLAGS=-trimpath`, build inside a fixed Go toolchain), requires Go 1.22+ on the build host (not a binary-only solution; the build step is real), `xcaddy run` injects its replace into *your* module's go.mod not Caddy's (for Caddy-core hacking, build Caddy from source directly), and each `--with` module ships under its own license — verify before linking proprietary plugins.

## Previously added (+3)

- [mutagen](clis/mutagen/) — high-performance two-way real-time file synchronization daemon for the "edit locally, run remotely" loop (mutagen-io, MIT, v0.18.1): a Go binary plus auto-deployed `mutagen-agent` that pairs over SSH / Docker / local sockets and keeps two file trees identical in milliseconds via native FS events (FSEvents / inotify / ReadDirectoryChangesW), shipping only deltas, normalizing file modes / ownership / symlinks across mismatched OSes, and surfacing true conflicts via `mutagen sync list` rather than silently overwriting; a sibling `mutagen forward` subsystem multiplexes TCP / Unix-socket tunnels on the *same* SSH transport so a `mutagen.yml` declares both syncs and forwards and `mutagen project start` brings them up together. The "edit on my laptop, run on the GPU box / dev container / staging VM" gap that none of `rsync` (one-shot, one-way), `sshfs` (every editor read is a network round-trip), `git push` (ceremony), or [`syncthing`](clis/syncthing/) (peer-to-peer mesh for personal devices, not point-to-point dev loop) actually fills well; pick over [`rclone`](clis/rclone/) when the question is "keep two local trees in sync" not "ship a file to a bucket"; pick over `unison` for the modern Go reimagining with native watchers, container support, and project config. Pairs with LLM-CLI workflows where the agent runs on a remote GPU and the human reviews on a laptop — bidirectional sync means agent edits land in the local editor / LSP / git client immediately, and human fixups land back on the remote box before the next agent step. Caveats — daemon is per-user and sessions outlive shells (stale sync sessions *will* keep running in the background; `mutagen project terminate` is the cleanup), initial scan of a million-file tree is expensive (set `--ignore` for `node_modules` / `.venv` / `target` *before* the first sync), conflicts surface as a session-level alert not per-file prompts, default release builds bundle SSPL components opt-out via the `--sspl` build flag, MIT for the core ([LICENSE](https://github.com/mutagen-io/mutagen/blob/master/LICENSE)).
- [zstd](clis/zstd/) — Zstandard, the modern general-purpose lossless compressor from Meta with gzip-class ratios at multi-GB/s decompression (facebook, BSD-2-Clause, v1.5.7): the reference C implementation that ships the `zstd` CLI, `libzstd`, and the `pzstd` parallel front-end, with a level dial from `-1` (fast) to `-22 --ultra` (archival), a `--long` window up to 2 GiB for large-file ratio, dictionary mode (`zstd --train logs/*.json -o log.dict && zstd -D log.dict`) that lifts ratios on tiny-message corpora (JSON events, gRPC payloads, log lines) by 3-5×, and a unique `--adapt` mode that retunes the level in real time to match downstream pipe throughput (`zfs send | zstd --adapt | nc dr-host 9000` never stalls on a slow link). The "modern default for general-purpose compression" gap — the format Linux kernel images, btrfs / ZFS, RPM, deb, Arch packages, the Rust crate registry, and a growing share of cloud storage have already standardized on; faster *and* smaller than gzip across the board, faster to decompress than xz by a wide margin. Pick over [`crabz`](clis/crabz/) when you want zstd's full surface (dictionary training, ultra mode, `--adapt`) not just a `pigz` drop-in that happens to support zstd; pick over `gzip` / `pigz` for any new pipeline (use `pigz` only for legacy gzip-only consumers); pick over `xz` / `lzma` for everything except "ratio is everything and decompression speed doesn't matter". Pairs with LLM-CLI workflows as the "ship the context" piece (`tar --zstd -cf ctx.tar.zst src/`) — compression times in single-digit seconds, download bounded by network not decompression. Caveats — flag stability across major versions is good but not perfect (pin `--version` and the flag string for reproducible artifacts), `--long=N` requires the *decompressor* to allocate `2^N` bytes (a 1.5 GiB archive at `--long=31` needs ~2 GiB RAM to decompress; default 27 = 128 MiB is safe everywhere), dictionaries must be present at decompress time (ship alongside data), `-T0` parallelism is per-frame so tiny inputs see no speedup. BSD-2-Clause + GPL-2.0 dual license ([LICENSE](https://github.com/facebook/zstd/blob/dev/LICENSE), [COPYING](https://github.com/facebook/zstd/blob/dev/COPYING)).
- [streamlink](clis/streamlink/) — CLI that takes a *web page URL* of a live video stream and pipes the underlying media stream to a player of your choice (streamlink, BSD-2-Clause, 8.3.0): pure-Python tool with ~150 site-specific plugins in-tree (Twitch, YouTube live, Kick, BBC iPlayer, every major IPTV portal) — `streamlink https://twitch.tv/somechannel best` resolves the page to its underlying HLS / DASH manifest, picks the highest-quality variant, and pipes it into `mpv` / `vlc` / `ffplay`, with `--stdout` for further `ffmpeg` recording / restreaming / vision-pipeline ingestion. The "browserless live stream" gap none of the catalog's downloaders cover — where [`yt-dlp`](clis/yt-dlp/) excels at *video downloads* and [`gallery-dl`](clis/gallery-dl/) excels at *image galleries*, `streamlink` excels at *live playback piped into your player of choice*; the per-site plugin model means when a site changes its manifest format one plugin breaks while the rest keep working. Pick over `yt-dlp` when the source is a continuously-broadcasting live stream rather than a finished VOD (the overlap is "VOD URLs"; the disjoint parts are "live broadcasts of arbitrary duration" → streamlink only, and "post-processed file with chapters and metadata" → yt-dlp only); pick over invoking `ffmpeg` directly on a manifest URL when you don't already *have* the manifest URL (per-site logic that finds the manifest behind a page, handles auth, signs URLs, picks CDN, and routes ad-bypass is exactly what the plugins do). Pairs with LLM-CLI workflows as the "point a model at a live video signal" piece — `streamlink --stdout <url> 480p | ffmpeg -i - -vf fps=1 -f image2 frame_%d.jpg` gives a 1-FPS frame stream from any supported live source for vision-model event detection or `whisper`-paired transcript bootstrapping. Caveats — plugin breakage is a fact of life (pin to the major and `pipx upgrade streamlink` regularly), DRM-protected streams (Widevine / FairPlay / PlayReady) cannot be played by streamlink or any other CLI, Python startup is 1-3 s cold-start to first frame so don't spawn one per second in tight loops, `--twitch-disable-ads` works at the manifest level (ethically a choice). BSD-2-Clause ([LICENSE](https://github.com/streamlink/streamlink/blob/master/LICENSE)).

## Previously added (+3)

- [jujutsu](clis/jujutsu/) — git-compatible next-generation VCS where the working copy *is* a commit and history rewriting is the default operation, not a recovery tool (jj-vcs, Apache-2.0, v0.40.0): one Rust binary that speaks the on-disk git format as a backend (so `jj git clone` / `jj git push` interop with any git remote — GitHub / Gitea / plain SSH bare repos / your existing CI), but the user model has no staging area (every save is auto-amended into `@`, `jj new` opens a fresh empty child for a boundary), every operation is recorded in `jj op log` and rollback-able with `jj op restore <op-id>` (atomic across working copy + bookmark pointers — `git reflog` only shows commit moves and never restored branch refs atomically), and conflicts become first-class commit content (a `jj rebase` over a conflict *succeeds* — the resulting "conflicted commit" can be rebased / split / abandoned without resolving first), with stable change IDs separate from rewriteable commit IDs so middle-of-stack edits don't break references. The "git's commit model is the actual problem, but I cannot abandon git as the wire format" gap — every "post-git" VCS (Mercurial / Pijul / Fossil / Sapling) failed at adoption because rewriting the wire format means rewriting GitHub; `jj` keeps git on the wire and replaces only the user model, so you drop it into any git repo today and your colleagues never know. Pick over plain `git` when stacked-PR workflows ([Phabricator](https://www.phacility.com/) / Graphite / Sapling-style) are your day job and you want native primitives instead of `git rebase --interactive --autosquash` rituals; over [`gitu`](clis/gitu/) / [`lazygit`](clis/lazygit/) when you want to *change* the model not skin git's CLI; over [`sapling`](clis/sapling/) when git-wire-compatibility matters more than Meta-monorepo-scale features. Caveats — the git backend is the only mature one (the hand-written backend is experimental), the colocated `.jj/` next to `.git/` adds disk + needs a teammate culture that tolerates it, monthly release cadence with breakages still possible (pin a version in CI).
- [typioca](clis/typioca/) — cosy Bubble Tea / Lipgloss terminal typing-test TUI with time / words / sentences / custom-corpus modes and persistent local SQLite stats (bloznelis, MIT, v3.1.0): one ~7 MB Go binary, no runtime deps, runs over SSH on 80×24 — pick a mode (time 15/30/60/120 s, words 10/25/50/100, sentences with full capitalisation + punctuation, or custom: feed it any `.txt` file as the corpus), pick a wordlist (English common-1k / common-3k / 10k ship in-binary, plus punctuation + numbers toggles), and a live status row updates WPM / accuracy / time-remaining each keystroke; on finish a `go-echarts` ASCII line-plot of WPM-over-time + errors-over-time, and a "Show stats" view walks back through every prior run from `~/.local/share/typioca/` so "did punctuation practice actually move my accuracy" is a real query not a guess. The "terminal typing test that practices the *shape* of typing not just word streams" gap — `tt` and `thokr` are word-stream-only so you never train sentence-start capitalisation / period-space-capital / comma cadence / parentheticals; `typioca`'s sentence mode + custom-corpus hook means you can paste a chapter of your own draft and practice the prose you'll write tomorrow. Pick over [`tt`](https://github.com/lemnos/tt) when sentence-shape practice + history DB matter (tt for the fastest single-screen WPM benchmark); pick over `thokr` when active maintenance matters (thokr inactive since 2022); pick over `smassh` when one Go binary beats Python + Textual. Caveats — no code-mode (typing real source with brackets / indentation enforced — none of the terminal typing tools do this well; `monkeytype` web is still the answer there), no cross-device sync (intentionally local-first), the SQLite path is hardcoded under `XDG_DATA_HOME`.
- [boxes](clis/boxes/) — pipe-friendly filter that draws ~60 ASCII / Unicode art borders around stdin text, *and* removes them too so it round-trips inside an editor (ascii-boxes, GPL-3.0-or-later, v2.3.1): one C utility (Thomas Jensen, 1999, still actively maintained — 2.3.1 cut October 2025) reads text on stdin, wraps it in a configurable border via a declarative design DSL (each design ~30 lines of `boxes-config`, no recompile, shipped library covers C/C++ block comments, shell `#` banners, HTML / SQL / Lisp comment markers, ASCII boxes, Unicode single / double / heavy / rounded, plus joke designs — cat / dog / ice cube / stone tablet) and writes it to stdout; `boxes -r` is the *inverse* operation that strips the border back out, so binding `,mc` in vim to `boxes -d c-cmt2` and `,md` to `boxes -r` gives a fully reversible commenting workflow inside any editor that pipes through filters. The "decorate text but stay reversible and editor-friendly" gap — `figlet` / `toilet` make large *letter* art with no wrapping and no inverse, `cowsay` is one fixed cartoon template with no design DSL, `gum style` makes pretty rounded boxes but has no remove operation and no comment-marker designs; `boxes` is the only entry that treats the border as a reversible markup transform on real paragraph content, which is why it's been the canonical "wrap this banner comment" tool in vim / emacs / Sublime keybindings since the late 90s. Pick over [`gum`](clis/gum/) when banner comments inside source files matter (gum for modern TUI chrome inside a Bubble Tea app); pick over `figlet` / `toilet` when you want the text wrapped not the text rendered as 8-line letters (often pipe them together — `figlet hello | boxes -d stone`). Caveats — the design library is ASCII-art aesthetic by default (set up your own Unicode-rounded design via `boxes-config` if you want modern chrome), GPL-3.0-or-later constrains closed-source bundling, ANSI-color support exists but the design must declare colour shapes (most shipped designs are monochrome).

## Previously added (+3)

- [porsmo](clis/porsmo/) — pomodoro / timer / stopwatch TUI in Rust (ColorCookie-dev, MIT, v0.3.0): one ~2 MB binary with three subcommands (`porsmo pomodoro`, `porsmo timer 45m`, `porsmo stopwatch`), a unified `1h25m3s` duration syntax, big-block ASCII digits readable from across a desk, single-key pause/resume/skip (`Space`, `S`), and a system-bell beep on completion so a long-running job can yell from a tmux pane you're not looking at; v0.3.0 dropped the legacy colon-prefixed time syntax (`1:` for 1m) and the implicit 25-minute default in favour of the conventional form. The "I want all three of pomodoro / countdown / stopwatch with the same keymap and the same install" gap — most pomodoro CLIs are single-mode (`tomato` / `gone` / `pomodoro` / `pomo`) and switching modes means switching binaries and key muscle memory; `porsmo` is the rare unified surface where the same `Space` pauses every mode and the same time string parses everywhere. Pick over [`peaclock`](clis/peaclock/) when you want pomodoro logic and a one-shot timer in the same tool (peaclock is the prettier display-clock with a stopwatch; porsmo is the productivity-loop tool); pick over a desktop notification daemon when you want a foreground TUI you actually look at, not a tray icon you forget. Caveats — no task list / statistics / sync (intentionally — for that reach for `pomotroid` / `flow` / a full GTD app), the bell uses the terminal's audible bell (silent if your terminal disables it), no Windows release artifact (`cargo install porsmo` works on Windows but `paru -S porsmo` does not).
- [humanlog](clis/humanlog/) — stdin filter that turns JSON / logfmt structured logs into human-readable colorized lines (humanlogio, Apache-2.0, v0.7.8): one Go binary that auto-detects JSON vs logfmt vs zap-development format per-line, parses every common timestamp dialect (RFC3339, Unix seconds, Unix milliseconds, `2006-01-02 15:04:05`), reformats the line as `<relative-ts> <COLOR-LEVEL> <msg>  key=value …` in `bunyan` style, and falls through any non-structured line unchanged so a partial migration (some services structured, some not) survives one pipe — `kubectl logs -f deploy/api | humanlog` is the single command that fixes the daily papercut of reading Loki-shaped JSON with your eyes during local debugging. The "producer emits JSON for the aggregator, I want to read it as a human, without changing the producer" gap — the producer-side fix ("just log pretty in dev") loses the structure the aggregator needs; the aggregator-side fix ("only read logs in Loki / Datadog") is overkill for a two-minute `kubectl logs -f`; `humanlog` is the consumer-side seam that lets the same JSON line feed Loki *and* read like `bunyan` did in 2014. Pick over [`lnav`](clis/lnav/) when the workload is the *live tail* of a structured stream rather than navigation of files at rest (lnav is the navigator, humanlog is the prettifier — they pair well); pick over a `jq` one-liner when the format detection / timestamp parsing / colour layer is worth the binary install. Caveats — only formats, doesn't filter (chain with `jq` / `grep` upstream for filtering), `--keep` / `--skip` shape the output but the producer's key names are still the API, no Windows-native colour fallback on legacy `cmd.exe` (Windows Terminal / PowerShell 7 are fine).
- [nh](clis/nh/) — "Yet Another Nix Helper" — Rust CLI wrapping `nixos-rebuild`, `home-manager`, and `darwin-rebuild` with a unified UX (nix-community, EUPL-1.2, v4.3.2): one binary, three subcommands that mirror the three rebuild surfaces (`nh os` / `nh home` / `nh darwin`) each with `switch` / `boot` / `test` / `build`, default behaviour of "find the flake in `$FLAKE` or `~/.config/nix-config`, build it, show me an `nvd` diff of the upcoming generation (added 47, removed 3, upgraded `linux 6.11.0 → 6.12.1`), then ask before activating" — the pre-activation diff is the loop plain `nixos-rebuild` never had. Bonus: `nh search` is a fast TUI front-end to the `search.nixos.org` backend (no browser tab), `nh clean` wraps `nix-collect-garbage -d` with the right user / root / boot-loader flag split. The "three rebuild commands with three flag sets and zero feedback about what changes" gap — vanilla `nixos-rebuild` / `home-manager switch` / `darwin-rebuild` are three different muscle memories and you only learn what the new generation did by reading the journal after activation; `nh` collapses them into one CLI surface and adds the diff that makes "is this rebuild safe to switch into right now?" answerable in two seconds. Pick over plain `nixos-rebuild` once you rebuild more than weekly (the diff alone pays for itself the first time it catches an unintended kernel bump); pairs with `nvd` (which `nh` shells out to for the diff render) and any flake-based config repo. Caveats — EUPL-1.2 is uncommon (compatible with GPL but legally novel for some shops — review before vendoring), `$FLAKE` discovery defaults are opinionated (set the env var or use `--flake .` explicitly in CI), `nh clean all` touches root and user GC roots — sanity-check the `--keep` / `--keep-since` window in scripts.

## Previously added (+3)

- [mpv](clis/mpv/) — scriptable media player with JSON-IPC and a Lua/JS plugin runtime (mpv-player, GPL-2.0-or-later default / LGPL-2.1-or-later with `-Dgpl=false`, v0.41.0): one binary that decodes nearly every container/codec FFmpeg knows (`mp4`/`mkv`/`webm`/`flac`/`opus`/HLS/DASH/RTSP/network URLs), exposes a Unix-socket JSON IPC (`{"command":["seek",30]}`) so any shell or editor can drive playback, runs headless via `--vo=tct` (24-bit terminal blocks) or `--vo=caca` (ASCII over SSH), and accepts in-process Lua / JavaScript scripts under `~/.config/mpv/scripts/` for things like MPRIS bridging (`mpv-mpris`), auto-queue (`autoload.lua`), and YouTube sponsor-skip (`sponsorblock.lua`). The "I want a player I can control from outside its own process" gap — GUI players (VLC / IINA / QuickTime) have weak automation and no scripting model, raw `ffplay` is a debug viewer not a configurable player; mpv is the seam where the same binary that drives a colour-managed Vulkan pipeline on a workstation also runs as a headless daemon a tmux keybind controls over a socket. Pick over [`vlc`](clis/vlc/) when scriptability + IPC matter (use VLC for the GUI, mpv for the automation surface); pairs with `yt-dlp` (transparent `ytdl://...` invocation), `socat` / `jq` (IPC scripting), `playerctl` (MPRIS2 desktop integration). Caveats — default config is intentionally minimal (most users copy `~/.config/mpv/mpv.conf` from a curated dotfiles repo), the GPL-2.0-or-later default build links GPL FFmpeg (use `-Dgpl=false` for an LGPL binary if embedding in a proprietary product), the IPC socket is unauthenticated (anyone with filesystem access to it can control playback — keep it in `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, not shared `/tmp`).
- [wl-clipboard](clis/wl-clipboard/) — Wayland-native clipboard CLI, the `xclip` / `xsel` analogue (bugaevc, GPL-3.0-or-later, v2.3.0): two C binaries (`wl-copy`, `wl-paste`) that talk the `wl_data_device` and `wlr-data-control` protocols directly, with first-class MIME handling (`wl-copy --type image/png`, `wl-paste --list-types`), primary-selection support (`-p` for the middle-click buffer), and a watch mode (`wl-paste --watch <cmd>`) that fires on every selection change. The "Wayland broke my `echo … | xclip -sel clip` muscle memory" gap — Wayland's selection protocol requires the process that called `set_selection` to *stay alive* to actually serve the buffer when a paste arrives, so the X11-era one-liner pattern silently empties the clipboard the moment the shell exits; `wl-clipboard`'s fork-and-detach helper is the small infrastructure piece that makes Wayland clipboard usable from a shell at all, and the canonical primitive everything downstream is built on (clipboard-history daemons like [`clipse`](clis/clipse/) / `cliphist` / `clipman` all subscribe via `wl-paste --watch`, screenshot tools `grim` / `slurp` pipe images into `wl-copy --type image/png`, password managers do `echo $secret | wl-copy && sleep 30 && wl-copy --clear`). Pick over an X11-shim when you want the real protocol surface (MIME types, primary selection, watch mode), not just a `xclip` rename. Caveats — Wayland-only (no Windows, no macOS, no plain X11 session — keep an `xclip` / `pbcopy` fallback in shell functions), GPL-3.0-or-later (fine for normal use, awkward for closed-source bundling), watch-mode helper relies on the compositor honouring `wlr-data-control` (modern GNOME / KDE / sway / Hyprland are fine; very old GNOME sessions need the `xdg-desktop-portal` fallback path).
- [ncmpcpp](clis/ncmpcpp/) — featureful ncurses TUI client for MPD (Music Player Daemon) (ncmpcpp, GPL-2.0-or-later, 0.10.1): a vim-keymapped front-end (`j`/`k`/`/`/`Enter`/`a`/`s`) speaking MPD's plain-text TCP protocol on `localhost:6600`, with a Playlist editor, filesystem Browse view, live Search Engine, three-pane Media Library (artist → album → track), in-place Tag Editor (ID3v2 / Vorbis / FLAC), Lyrics fetcher, real-time spectrum / wave / ellipse Visualiser, and an Outputs screen that toggles ALSA / PulseAudio / Pipewire sinks the daemon exposes — all rebindable via `~/.config/ncmpcpp/bindings`. The "audio session that survives my SSH reconnect / laptop sleep / terminal crash" gap — every modern audio app bundles decoder + UI + library + sink into one process that dies together; the MPD daemon owns playback state and library indexing for years at a time, and `ncmpcpp` is one of many thin clients (`mpc`, `rmpc`, `cantata`, MALP) that attach read/write and disappear without disturbing playback. Pick over [`mpc`](https://www.musicpd.org/clients/mpc/) when you want the interactive library / tag-editor / visualiser surface (use both — `mpc` for shell scripting, `ncmpcpp` for the day-long reader); pick over `rmpc` when feature breadth + 15 years of accumulated community configs matter more than modern Rust + image previews (rmpc for the modern surface, ncmpcpp for the kitchen-sink default). Pairs with `mpd` (mandatory backend — must write `mpd.conf` and point `music_directory` at your library before launch), `mpc` (shell scripting), `mpDris2` / `mpd-mpris` (MPRIS2 bridge so `playerctl` works), [`beets`](clis/beets/) (library tagging pipeline that writes the same tree mpd indexes), and a hotkey daemon for global media keys. Caveats — hard prereq is a running MPD daemon (`brew install mpd` is not enough on its own), visualiser needs MPD's `audio_output { type "fifo" … }` configured and a matching `visualizer_data_source` in the client's config or the screen stays blank, tag-editor writes are immediate and in-place (back up or pair with [`beets`](clis/beets/) which tracks history), no GitHub Releases (installs come from distro packages or `git checkout 0.10.1`).

## Previously added (+3)

- [noseyparker](clis/noseyparker/) — Hyperscan-accelerated secret detector for full git histories, GitHub orgs, S3 buckets, JIRA tickets, and Slack exports (praetorian-inc, Apache-2.0, v0.24.0): a Rust binary that walks every blob ever committed (including dangling / force-pushed-away ones) through ~170 multi-pattern regex rules in roughly the time `ripgrep` does one, and stores findings in a content-addressed SQLite "datastore" so a daily rescan of a 50 GB monorepo with 200k commits is incremental and seconds-fast. The "what's already leaked across all 4,000 repos in our org and every fork of them?" gap — [`gitleaks`](clis/gitleaks/) / [`trufflehog`](clis/trufflehog/) / [`ripsecrets`](clis/ripsecrets/) / [`talisman`](clis/talisman/) / [`ggshield`](clis/ggshield/) all model the problem as "scan a working tree, optionally walk recent commits"; `noseyparker` is designed around the assumption that the secret survives only in a dangling blob in someone's fork, and that you want to rescan the whole world weekly without paying full price. Pick over the developer-boundary tools when the question is fleet-level inventory not pre-commit hook (use both — `gitleaks` / `talisman` on the developer boundary, `noseyparker` for the security team's weekly sweep). Caveats — needs Hyperscan / vectorscan to build from source (binary releases avoid this), Slack / JIRA / S3 adapters need API tokens with read scope, finding a key is step one and revoking it is step two (this tool does not rotate credentials).
- [reptyr](clis/reptyr/) — reparent a running process to a new terminal via ptrace (nelhage, MIT, v0.10.0): a tiny C utility that detaches an already-running program from its current controlling TTY and reattaches it to the TTY of the calling shell, so the 12-hour `make` you forgot to start under `tmux` can be rescued without killing it — walks `/proc/PID/fd` to find descriptors pointing at the old PTY, attaches via `ptrace`, swaps them for descriptors pointing at *your current* terminal, fixes up the process group + session leader. The "I started a long-running job in the wrong place and I'd like it back" gap — terminal multiplexers ([`tmux`](clis/tmux/) / [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) / `screen` / `dtach` / [`tmate`](clis/tmate/)) exist so you never need `reptyr` in the first place, but `reptyr` is the rescue tool for "I forgot to start under a multiplexer and I cannot kill this job". Does not overlap with [`mosh`](clis/mosh/) (which rescues the *interactive* session, not a running batch job) or `nohup` / `disown` / `setsid` (which preserve a process across logout but *disconnect* the TTY rather than reattach it). Caveats — Linux only (the macOS SIP / Mach task-port model makes this trick effectively impossible), needs `kernel.yama.ptrace_scope=0` (or capability + same-uid), processes that opened `/dev/tty` directly and stashed the result can confuse the reattach, SELinux / AppArmor profiles can deny the `ptrace`. Worth installing on every long-lived Linux box you SSH into, exactly once, against the day you need it.
- [patat](clis/patat/) — Pandoc-driven terminal Markdown presenter with live `eval` blocks through arbitrary interpreters (jaspervdj, GPL-2.0-only, v0.15.2.0): one Haskell binary that renders a Markdown file as a slide deck (slides split on `---` / `# Heading`) and pipes fenced code blocks tagged with a class through *any* external command at presentation time, output captured inline — `ghci` / `python` / `psql` / `bash` snippets become live demos, not screenshots, and `replace` / `fragment` modes let you walk a 20-line program one line at a time. The "presenter that runs real code" gap — [`presenterm`](clis/presenterm/) is the modern Rust default with great defaults and image rendering but is opinionated about which interpreters to bundle, [`slides`](clis/slides/) is Charm's minimalist visual-polish take with a smaller eval surface; `patat` takes the opposite trade — rendering is plainer but anything shell-callable can be a live block, and Pandoc-as-parser means the same `.md` doubles as a paper / blog post / README without a second toolchain. Pick over [`presenterm`](clis/presenterm/) when REPL-output-as-slides is the whole point and you want the full Pandoc Markdown dialect (footnotes, citations, tables, smart quotes); pick `presenterm` instead for media-heavy decks on a graphical terminal. Caveats — no animation or transitions (this is a prose / code presenter, not a visual one), GPL-2.0-only constrains closed-source embedding, image rendering needs a kitty- / iterm2- / w3m-graphics-capable terminal, image-protocol fall-throughs to text on plain xterm.

## Previously added (+3)

- [s-tui](clis/s-tui/) — terminal CPU monitor + built-in stress test (amanusk, GPL-2.0, v1.4.0): a `urwid` TUI that draws four scrolling graphs (frequency / utilization / temperature / power) plus the active cpufreq governor, with a one-key (`s`) integrated stresser that pegs every logical core so you can watch thermal throttling happen in real time and a `Throttling` banner light up the moment the SoC hits its thermal limit. The "is this laptop / SBC actually thermally healthy?" gap — `htop` / `bottom` / `btop` show CPU usage but not the *frequency-vs-temperature-vs-power* triangle that tells you whether boost clocks are sustainable; running `stress-ng` separately and tailing `sensors -u` in another pane gets you the same info but never aligned in time. Pick over [`bottom`](clis/bottom/) / [`htop`](clis/htop/) when the question is specifically "does this machine throttle under sustained load?" rather than "what is using my CPU right now" (use both — they answer different questions). Caveats — Linux is the first-class target (per-core temperature on macOS via `psutil` is mostly empty; use `macmon` / `asitop` on Apple Silicon), `--csv` is the only persistence (ship into Prometheus / Grafana for retention), governor switching needs root.
- [tut](clis/tut/) — Mastodon TUI client (RasmusLindroth, MIT, 2.0.1): a Go `tcell` three-pane TUI that talks to any Mastodon-compatible instance (Mastodon / Pleroma / Akkoma / GoToSocial) over its REST + streaming API — `j`/`k` to scroll the timeline, `Enter` to open a thread + media, `c` compose, `b` boost, `f` favourite, `T` switch timeline (home / local / federated / notifications / lists / tags / bookmarks), `g <n>` switch accounts; configuration is one TOML file with key-rebind tables and per-instance `[[account]]` blocks. The "federated social inside `tmux`" gap — Mastodon's web UI is JS-heavy and modal; in a terminal-resident workflow, `tut` is the analogue to `circumflex` for HN: read your timeline in a pane next to your editor without context-switching to a browser, and run multiple accounts (work / personal / alt) from one config file. Pick over [`toot`](clis/toot/) when the long-lived TUI matters more than one-shot scriptability (toot is a Python CLI + lighter TUI — pick `toot` for `toot post "..."` in shell scripts, pick `tut` for the day-long reader). Caveats — Mastodon-API only (no Bluesky / Twitter / Threads), no background notifications (foreground-only — pair with email / web push for "background" alerts), poll *creation* is limited (compose polls in the web UI, engage in `tut`).
- [qalc](clis/qalc/) — powerful CLI calculator with units, currencies, symbolic math, and live exchange rates (Qalculate, GPL-2.0-or-later, v5.10.0): the readline CLI front-end of the `libqalculate` engine — first-class units (SI + imperial + obscure: `furlong`, `slug`, `parsec`), ~170 currencies with daily-refreshed rates from ECB / Mycurrency.net (`qalc -e` to update), symbolic math (`solve()`, `diff()`, `integrate()`, `factor()`, `simplify()`), arbitrary-precision arithmetic, complex numbers, vectors / matrices, interval arithmetic for uncertainty propagation, and a parser that DWIMs natural input (`5 ft 3 in to cm`, `(25 USD + 10 EUR) to JPY`, `30C to F`, `now + 90 days`, `speed of light * 1 ns to m`). The "calculator that actually understands what I typed" gap — `bc` / `dc` are POSIX-correct but unit-blind, `python -c` is a programming language not a calculator, spreadsheets are mouse-driven; `qalc` is the same C++ brain that powers the Qalculate GUI in 200 KB of binary that runs over SSH. Pick over [`kalker`](clis/kalker/) when units + currency + CAS-lite matter (kalker is faster startup and plain numerics; pick kalker for hot loops, qalc for the one-shot "wait, is 25 USD + 10 EUR really ¥…" question). For an LLM-CLI workflow, `qalc -t` (terse mode) makes `result=$(qalc -t '5 mi to km')` a clean shell primitive an agent can use to convert units without rolling its own conversion table. Caveats — start-up + parser overhead means it is not for million-row arithmetic (use `awk` / `bc` for that), exchange-rate refresh hits the network (set `auto-update-exchange-rates 0` for fully offline use), CAS depth is far below SymPy / Maxima / Mathematica.

## Previously added (+3)

- [diffr](clis/diffr/) — word-level diff highlighter that wraps any line-level diff (mookid, MIT, v0.1.5): a small Rust filter that reads unified-diff output on stdin and re-emits it with intra-line additions/deletions colored, so you see *which characters* changed inside a `-`/`+` line pair, not just that the whole line changed. Composes with `less -R`, `delta`, code review tools — anything that already understands ANSI-colored unified diffs (`git config --global core.pager 'diffr | less -R'` is the one-line install). The "this `-`/`+` pair differs by three characters but the default red/green paints the entire line" gap — for long lines (SQL, JSON, prose, generated code), line-level coloring buries the actual edit; `diffr` keeps the unified-diff format your tools already understand and just adds the missing intra-line layer. Pick over [`bat`](clis/bat/) when the artifact is a *diff* not a *file at rest* (complementary, not competing); pick over a full diff replacement when you want a small filter you stack on top of whatever pager you already use rather than a new pager with its own theme system. Caveats — token diff is heuristic (very long lines or near-total rewrites can still highlight most of the line), needs a TTY-aware downstream (`less -R`, not plain `less`), unified diff only (`diff -c` / `diff -y` not supported — convert to `-u` first).
- [e1s](clis/e1s/) — k9s-style terminal UI for AWS ECS (keidarcy, MIT, v1.0.53): a single Go binary that gives you a keyboard-driven TUI over ECS clusters, services, tasks, containers, and task definitions — list, describe, exec into a running container via ECS Exec (`e`), tail CloudWatch logs (`l`), force a new deployment (`U`), scale (`s`), edit task definitions in `$EDITOR` and have it register a new revision + update the service on save (`t`), all without ever opening the AWS console. Uses ambient AWS credentials (env vars, `~/.aws/credentials`, SSO, IMDS), supports `--readonly` for production. The "I need to debug this ECS task right now and the AWS console is twelve clicks deep per action" gap — ECS has no first-party TUI; the console is slow and modal-heavy, the `aws ecs ...` CLI is correct but verbose and stateless. Pick over [`k9s`](clis/k9s/) when the workload is on ECS / Fargate not EKS (same shape, same muscle memory, different control plane — many shops run both); pick over the `aws` CLI when the work is interactive incident response not scripted automation (use both — `aws` for CI, `e1s` for "what's going on right now"). Caveats — ECS Exec must be enabled on the service for the `e` key to work (one-time service update), permissions only as broad as the caller's IAM (`--readonly` is belt-and-braces on top), one region at a time (re-launch with `AWS_REGION=…` for multi-region operators), edits ECS resources directly (if your task definitions are managed by IaC, in-TUI edits drift until reconciled).
- [pipes-sh](clis/pipes-sh/) — animated pipes terminal screensaver in pure Bash (pipeseroni, MIT, v1.3.0): the classic Windows 95 3D-pipes screensaver as a ~300-line shell script that draws colored pipe segments using nothing but ANSI escapes and box-drawing characters, configurable pipe count (`-p`), char set (`-t 0..3` including ASCII-only), frame rate (`-f`), and reset interval (`-r`). The "I want a zero-dependency, instantly-recognizable terminal animation that runs over SSH and proves the link is alive" gap — most terminal toys need a runtime (Python, Node, Go) or curses; `pipes.sh` is one Bash script with no dependencies beyond `tput`, so it runs on every Linux box, every BSD, every macOS install, inside every container image, over every SSH hop. Genuinely useful as a liveness canary on a noisy SSH session — pipes that stop moving = the link is wedged, easier to eyeball than `top`. Pick over [`cmatrix`](clis/cmatrix/) when you want zero-dependency Bash instead of compiled C+ncurses (same screensaver vibe, different aesthetic and dependency footprint). Caveats — needs Bash 4+ (default macOS `/bin/bash` is 3.2; install via Homebrew or use the bundled wrapper), Unicode box drawing assumes UTF-8 locale (`-t 2` for ASCII-only fallback), busy-loop animation will spin a core if you crank `-f`, not a real screensaver (does not lock the terminal — pair with `vlock` / `physlock` if you need that).

## Previously added (+3)

- [circumflex](clis/circumflex/) — Hacker News in the terminal with Markdown-rendered comments (bensadeh, MIT, v4.0): a Go TUI (`clx`) that hits the official HN API, paginates the front page / new / show / ask, and opens comment threads as an indented, syntax-highlighted reader via `glamour`. The "I want HN comments without leaving the terminal *and* I want them readable" gap — the HN web UI is plain text, `lynx`/`w3m` mangles thread indentation and code blocks, and generic RSS readers throw the comments at you as a flat list. `circumflex` is HN-shaped: indentation matches reply depth, code fences render correctly, `h` collapses a subtree so a 600-comment thread is skimmable in two minutes. Pick over [`newsboat`](clis/newsboat/) when the use-case is specifically HN comment threads (newsboat is the generic RSS workhorse with state and OPML; pair them — `newsboat` for everything else, `circumflex` for HN); pick over a browser tab when you want to stay inside `tmux`. Read-only by design (no upvoting, no posting), needs a 256-colour UTF-8 terminal for Glamour to look right, and hits the API on every launch (no offline mode).
- [cotp](clis/cotp/) — encrypted terminal TOTP / HOTP authenticator (replydev, GPL-3.0, v1.9.9): a Rust binary that stores 2FA seeds in a single Argon2id-derived AES-GCM-encrypted vault under `$XDG_DATA_HOME/cotp/` and prints rolling 6-digit codes either one-shot (`cotp -s github`) or in a live-updating TUI list with progress bars per entry. The "my 2FA seeds should not live on a phone I can lose, and they should not live in a SaaS vault someone else can subpoena" gap — for terminal-resident developers, `cotp -s github | pbcopy` is one keystroke vs unlocking a phone, and the encrypted vault is one file you back up like any other secret. Imports Aegis JSON, andOTP, FreeOTP+, Authy, Google Authenticator QR exports, and 2FAS, so migrating off a phone authenticator is one `cotp import` away. Pick over a phone authenticator when you're frequently SSH'd into a box and need a code right now; pair with `pass` / `gopass` / a clipboard helper (`xclip` / `wl-copy` / `pbcopy`). Caveats — master password is a single point of failure (no recovery if forgotten), local vault means *you* own the backup discipline (no Authy-style cloud sync), GPL-3.0 limits closed-source embedding, and TOTP/HOTP only (no Duo-style push approval — that's a proprietary protocol).
- [tuifeed](clis/tuifeed/) — stateless three-pane RSS / Atom reader (veeso, MIT, v0.4.2): a Rust `tui-realm` reader configured by one TOML file (`[sources]` is a string-to-URL map) — left pane lists sources, middle pane lists posts, right pane renders the article body via `html2text`; `o` opens the original URL in `$BROWSER`, everything else is keyboard. The "I want RSS without a daemon, a database, or a sync service" gap — self-hosted readers (Tiny Tiny RSS, FreshRSS, Miniflux) want a server + DB + maintenance; SaaS readers want an account; `newsboat` keeps SQLite + OPML state to migrate. `tuifeed` is the smallest possible slice: dotfile-friendly TOML, fetch on launch, render in three panes, done. Pick over [`newsboat`](clis/newsboat/) when you want a *throwaway* reader on a fresh box with no state to migrate (pick `newsboat` instead for a *durable* workflow with hundreds of feeds, real read/unread state, podcasts, query feeds, OPML import/export). Caveats — stateless within a session (every launch starts every post as "unseen", painful past ~30 feeds), HTML-to-text only (no images/JS, press `o` for rich rendering), Atom + RSS only (no JSON Feed, no podcast enclosures), single-source `r` is fine but `R` (refresh-all) is sequential and waits on the slowest feed.

## Previously added (+3)

- [huh](clis/huh/) — interactive forms and prompts in the terminal, as both a Go library and a standalone CLI (charmbracelet, MIT, v2.0.3): a Bubble Tea–powered widget set (Input, Select, MultiSelect, Confirm, Note, Text) that turns "ask the user a few structured questions from a shell script" into one composable command — `name=$(huh input --title "Your name?")` for a polished prompt with one bash line, or `huh.NewForm(huh.NewGroup(...))` in Go for a grouped, validated, single-screen TUI with arrow navigation, `/`-search inside long lists, inline error reporting, and theme-able styling. The "structured prompt for shell + Go" gap — bash has only `read` / `select` (no validation, no multi-select, no rendering); Python has `inquirer` / `questionary`, JS has `prompts` / `enquirer`, Go and shell were both underserved. Pick over [`gum`](clis/gum/) when the workflow is *forms* specifically (richer field types, grouped multi-field layout, validation as a first-class field) rather than gum's broader shell-glue toolkit (spinners, styled output, file pickers, *plus* prompts) — they coexist; Charm itself uses both. Pick over `dialog` / `whiptail` for any prompt UX past 1990; pick over inline `read` once you need validation, multi-select, or "ask three related things and let the user go back and edit the first one". For an LLM-CLI workflow, `huh` is the human-in-the-loop input layer: an agent that needs "which of these 5 candidate patches do you want me to apply?" gets a real multi-select with search instead of "type 1, 2, or 3 (comma-separated)". Caveats — modal/blocking forms only (use Bubble Tea directly for non-modal TUIs); requires `/dev/tty` (piping input does not work — use `--default` flags for CI); v2 reshaped the Group/Field API from v1 (pin to v2.0.3 and follow the current README).
- [pop](clis/pop/) — send emails from the terminal via SMTP (charmbracelet, MIT, v0.2.1): a small Charm Go CLI that wraps SMTP into a single command — set four env vars (`SMTP_HOST` / `SMTP_PORT` / `SMTP_USERNAME` / `SMTP_PASSWORD`), then `echo "build green" | pop -t alerts@example.com -s "CI green"` delivers via your provider's relay, with `-a file` for attachments, `-c` / `-b` for Cc / Bcc, and a Bubble Tea TUI when run with no flags for interactive composition. The "send one email, no MUA setup" gap — `mail` / `mailx` need a working local MTA (modern systems lack one); `curl --mail-from` works but the syntax is brutal; Python's `smtplib` needs a wrapper script every time. `pop` is the *single, memorable command* that wraps SMTP-over-TLS auth and MIME assembly with the exact UX a script author wants: subject as a flag, body as stdin, attachments as `-a`, exit 0 on success. Pick over [`himalaya`](clis/himalaya/) when you only ever need outbound from scripts (himalaya is a full IMAP / read+send client with folders, threading, search, multiple accounts); pick over [`aerc`](clis/aerc/) when you need non-interactive outbound only (aerc is an interactive TUI mail client). Pick over `curl --mail-rcpt` to stop relooking up the option names every six months. Pick over a provider SDK (Mailgun / Postmark / SES) for low-volume "ping me when X happens" mail that doesn't deserve a service account — use the SDKs when you actually need templating, suppression lists, and webhooks. For an LLM-CLI workflow, `pop` is the "page a human" output sink — when an agent finishes an overnight task and needs to push a digest somewhere the user actually checks (their inbox) without standing up a webhook or chat app. Caveats — outbound only (no IMAP / POP3 — the name is Charm's, not the protocol's); credentials live in env (use `chmod 600` files or `pass` / `gopass` / 1Password CLI to inject); provider quirks bite (Gmail needs an app password; Office 365 often rejects SMTP basic auth — verify before wiring into a critical alert path); no retry / queue (a transient failure loses the message — wrap calls in your own retry loop or relay through a local Postfix in submission mode); plain text by default (HTML works but you assemble the markup yourself).
- [ghostty](clis/ghostty/) — fast, native, GPU-accelerated terminal emulator (ghostty-org, MIT, v1.3.1): a Zig-built terminal split into `libghostty` (embeddable parser + renderer + state machine) and a standalone app (AppKit on macOS, GTK 4 on Linux) that targets "feels native everywhere, runs every TUI correctly, stays out of your way" — full xterm conformance, Kitty / iTerm2 protocol compatibility (graphics, keyboard, OSC 52 clipboard), single hot-reloaded `~/.config/ghostty/config` file, no Electron, no JavaScript anywhere in the render path. The "actually-native, actually-fast, actually-correct" terminal gap — Electron-based emulators are slow with huge RAM footprints, web-tech wrappers misrender edge cases, and 1990s X11 emulators are perfectly fast but hostile UX (no ligatures, no true colour). `ghostty` aims for the empty fourth corner: native windowing toolkits, GPU rendering, full protocol breadth, zero web stack. Pick over [`kitty`](clis/kitty/) when you want strict xterm conformance and a leaner, scripting-free design (kitty is older, Python-extensible, with novel protocol extensions and Windows support — pick kitty for its plugin ecosystem); pick over [`wezterm`](clis/wezterm/) when scripting the terminal itself would be a *liability* (wezterm is Rust + Lua, cross-platform including Windows — pick wezterm when you want a Lua scripting layer); pick over [`tabby`](clis/tabby/) when minimal beats app-store experience (tabby is Electron with built-in SSH client and plugin marketplace — pick tabby for the GUI-settings UX). For an LLM-CLI workflow, `ghostty` is the host terminal under any agent that opens a TUI (a picker, a diff viewer, a long streaming response) — strict xterm conformance means agent-driven TUIs render the same on every machine. Pair with `tmux` / `zellij` *inside* it for session persistence (ghostty is a terminal, not a multiplexer). Caveats — no Windows binary yet (planned via WinUI; macOS + Linux/GTK only today); GTK 4 on Linux means modern distros only (Ubuntu 22.04+, Fedora 38+, Arch current); Zig toolchain churn for source builds (pin to the Zig version listed in the release's `flake.nix` / `build.zig`); `libghostty` ships only the cell grid — tabs / splits are the application layer (so embedders get the terminal, not the chrome).

## Previously added (+3)
