- [tini](clis/tini/) — tiny but valid `init` for containers (krallin, MIT, v0.19.0): a ~1k-LOC C program designed to run as PID 1 inside a container, doing exactly two jobs — reap zombie processes that get re-parented to PID 1 (so the global process table never leaks), and forward signals (`SIGTERM`/`SIGINT`) from the container engine to the wrapped application so apps shut down cleanly within the 10-second grace window before `SIGKILL`. The "PID 1 problem" answer — most application runtimes (python, node, ruby, java, shell) were never designed to run as PID 1 and silently get zombie-reaping or signal-handling wrong; `tini` is the smallest possible drop-in that does both correctly via `ENTRYPOINT ["/sbin/tini", "--"]` so the app never has to know it's PID 1. Single static binary (~700 KB), bake-able into `FROM scratch`, forwards exit codes faithfully (no naive 0/1 collapse that breaks CI gating), supports subreaper mode (`-s` / `TINI_SUBREAPER=1` / `PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER`) for entrypoint scripts that themselves fork helpers. The upstream tool Docker eventually adopted as `--init` (literally the same mechanism). Pick over `docker run --init` when you need to support runtimes that don't have the flag (older containerd, some k8s clusters, podman with old defaults, OCI-compliant runners outside Docker — bake `tini` into the image and the question goes away). Pick over writing signal handlers in the app when you'd otherwise re-do the work in every language runtime (Python's GIL, Java's signal masks, Node's libuv handlers — all subtly different; `tini` solves it once at the kernel-PID-1 layer). Pair with [`overmind`](clis/overmind/) only if you also want a multi-process supervisor inside the container (different layer — `tini` runs *one* command, `overmind` runs *many*); for an LLM-CLI workflow `tini` is the container-init under any agent that runs inside Docker / k8s (model runner, sandboxed code-exec container, per-session Jupyter kernel) so "container exited 0 = task succeeded" is trustable. Caveats — not a service manager (use `s6-overlay` / `runit` for multiple long-running procs in one container; `tini` won't restart on crash), subreaper mode requires Linux ≥ 3.4, `exec` form `ENTRYPOINT` is mandatory (shell form puts `/bin/sh -c` as PID 1 and defeats the point), v0.19.0 (2021) is feature-complete and security-quiet by design — you do not want surprise behaviour from PID 1.
- [stow](clis/stow/) — symlink farm manager for dotfiles and source-built software (GNU, GPL-3.0-or-later, v2.4.1): reads a *package directory* (e.g. `~/dotfiles/zsh/`) and mirrors its tree into a *target directory* (e.g. `~`) by creating symlinks; folding-aware so two packages can transparently share a subdir (`~/.config/nvim` is one dir-link if only `nvim` writes there, automatically *unfolds* into a real dir of file-links the moment a sibling package needs to write into the same subdir). The "version-controlled dotfiles + zero magic" gap — naive `ln -sf` shell installers grow into 200-line bash scripts with `if [ -L ~/.zshrc ]` ladders and silent failures, while heavier tools require a system install before you can install your dotfiles (chicken-and-egg on a fresh machine); `stow` solves it with one Perl script that has been around since 1996. Conflict detection is up-front (`stow -nv pkg` reports every would-be collision before any change, idempotent and safe to run twice); no state file (the answer to "what is installed" is recoverable purely from `find ~ -lname '*/dotfiles/*'`, so there is no `~/.stowdb` to lose); also doubles as an install-into-`/usr/local`-without-a-package-manager primitive (`./configure --prefix=/usr/local/stow/foo-1.2 && make install && stow foo-1.2`). Pick over [`chezmoi`](clis/chezmoi/) when you want the install plan to be reviewable symlinks and zero templating (chezmoi is a much larger Go binary with templating, secret encryption, machine-specific data — pick it when files differ per-machine or contain secrets); pick over [`yadm`](clis/yadm/) when you want the source dir to be a normal git repo whose `git status` doesn't include all of `$HOME` (yadm makes `$HOME` itself a working tree — different model). Pick over a hand-rolled `ln -sf` script when you have more than ten files or any shared subdirectory. For an LLM-CLI workflow, `stow` is the dotfiles bootstrap primitive — `git clone ~/dotfiles && cd ~/dotfiles && stow */` and a fresh sandbox is wired up. Caveats — symlinks only (some old SSH versions complain about symlinked `~/.ssh/config` perms; setuid binaries refuse to follow symlinks for security); target defaults to *parent of cwd* (always pass `-t ~` explicitly or run from a dir whose parent is the target — most first-time bug reports trace here); no templating / per-host logic (use `.stow-local-ignore` for coarse skip-lists or graduate to chezmoi); Perl runtime required (tiny in practice but a real detail for stripped container images).
- [dotbot](clis/dotbot/) — declarative dotfile bootstrapper driven by a YAML / JSON plan file (anishathalye, MIT, v1.24.0): a ~1k-line Python script vendored into your dotfiles repo as a git submodule that reads `install.conf.yaml` and executes its directives in order — `link:` (create symlinks with `force` / `relink` / `create` / glob / `if`-shell-guard options), `create:` (`mkdir -p`), `shell:` (run arbitrary commands with `stdout` / `stderr` / `stdin` / `quiet` / `description` controls), `clean:` (remove dead symlinks pointing into the repo). The "declarative dotfiles bootstrap, no global install" gap — `git clone --recursive ~/dotfiles && cd ~/dotfiles && ./install` is the entire onboarding on a fresh machine, with the only system requirement being Python 3 + git (both already present everywhere); the YAML is one screen, top-to-bottom = execution order so there is no DAG to learn, and `if:` guards are plain shell expressions (`if: '[ "$(uname)" = Darwin ]'`) — no DSL, no Jinja, no per-tool template language. Pick over [`chezmoi`](clis/chezmoi/) when you want the install plan reviewable as YAML and the runtime to be "stock Python" (chezmoi is heavier — single Go binary with templating + secret encryption + machine-specific data + state directory; pick it when you genuinely need per-host templating or secret management); pick over [`yadm`](clis/yadm/) when you want a separate dotfiles repo *projected* into `$HOME` via symlinks (yadm makes `$HOME` itself a git working tree — no symlinks); pick over [`stow`](clis/stow/) when you also need post-link shell hooks (install tmux plugin manager, `chsh -s zsh`, generate ssh keys) — stow is *only* the symlink farm with no shell / `mkdir` / `if` story; pick over ansible / chef / saltstack when "it's just my laptop, leave me alone" — those assume a control-plane and inventory. For an LLM-CLI workflow, `dotbot` is the per-host config materialiser — an agent provisioning a new shell sandbox can `git clone --recursive` a curated dotfiles repo and `./install` to land a known-good environment in one command, with a YAML diff a reviewer can read instead of a bash script they can't. Caveats — Python 3 is a runtime dep (already there 99 % of the time, but not on stripped Alpine — `apk add python3` first); no rollback (failed `shell:` directive halfway through leaves prior `link:` operations applied — keep destructive steps near the end and rely on idempotent re-runs); no per-host data files (one config per invocation — convention is one `install.<host>.conf.yaml` plus a tiny `./install` wrapper that picks the right one); submodule UX = standard Git submodule UX (forgetting `--recursive` on `git clone` is the classic confusing failure); slow release cadence is the point — v1.x format frozen since 2014, old configs keep working.

## Previously added (+3)

- [yank](clis/yank/) — keyboard-only terminal-output → clipboard picker (mptre, MIT, v1.3.0): a tiny C utility that reads stdin, tokenizes the stream by a configurable regex (default whitespace + punctuation), shows the underlying text with the current token highlighted, and lets you walk tokens with `h` / `l` / arrows and copy the chosen one to the system clipboard with Enter. The "I see the value on screen, I want it in my paste buffer, keyboard-only" gap — over SSH, in `tmux`, on a remote tty, or any terminal where mouse selection is broken or unavailable, `command | yank` is one keystroke per token vs the multi-step tmux copy-mode dance (`prefix [` → navigate → mark → yank → exit → `prefix ]`). Tokenizer is a single regex flag so IPv6 / hashes / quoted strings are one-shot; clipboard backend is just a shell command in `YANK_CLIPBOARD_CMD`, so it works identically locally (`pbcopy` / `xclip` / `xsel`), over SSH (route through OSC-52 with a one-line wrapper), or into a tmux paste buffer (`tmux load-buffer -`). Pick over [`fzf`](clis/fzf/) when you want a *token* from inside one stream of text rather than a *line* from a list (pair them: `fzf` to pick a line, `yank` to pick a token from that line); pick over [`xclip`](clis/xclip/) / `pbcopy` when you want one chosen field rather than all of stdin (`cmd | xclip` copies everything, `cmd | yank` copies one token interactively); pick over tmux copy-mode when you want a unix filter that works *outside* tmux too. For an LLM-CLI workflow, `yank` is the human-in-the-loop copy step: agent prints "the failing pod is `api-7d8f-x9k2`", human runs `kubectl get pods | yank`, picks, pastes — inverse of `pbpaste | llm`. Caveats — single token only (no multi-select / range / join), regex tokenizer is bytes not structure (use `jq` + `fzf` for JSON), needs a clipboard helper to exist on the host (set `YANK_CLIPBOARD_CMD=cat` on a headless box to fall back to stdout), v1.3.0 (2022) is feature-complete and bug-fix-only.
- [q-text-as-data](clis/q-text-as-data/) — SQL directly against CSV / TSV / SQLite from the shell (harelba, GPL-3.0, v3.1.6): a Python program that exposes any delimited text file as a SQLite table on the fly and runs your SQL query against it (`q "SELECT c1, COUNT(*) FROM ./access.tsv GROUP BY c1"`). Auto-detects delimiter and types, supports header rows (`-H`), `JOIN` across multiple files (and across mixed CSV + `.sqlite` files in the same query), full SQLite SQL (window functions, CTEs, `LIKE` / `REGEXP`), and an opt-in `.qsql` cache (`-C readwrite`) that turns the parsed table into a portable SQLite database next to the input — the same artifact is openable with `sqlite3` directly. Fills the "I have a CSV and one SQL question, not a project" gap between `awk` / `cut` / `sort | uniq -c` (great for one-column work, no `JOIN` or `GROUP BY` shape) and `pandas` / a real DB (overkill setup for one ad-hoc question). Pick over [`xsv`](clis/xsv/) when you want expression-level SQL (`CASE WHEN`, window functions, sub-queries, multi-file `JOIN ... ON`) rather than row-and-column transformations (`xsv select` / `xsv join` are faster and Rust-fast for shape work); pick over [`miller`](clis/miller/) when you want SQL semantics rather than miller's DSL — miller wins for true streaming and nested JSON, q wins when the query is "JOIN three files on a shared key with a window function over the result"; pick over [`dasel`](clis/dasel/) / [`jq`](clis/jq/) for tabular data (those are tree-data tools); pick over `sqlite3 -csv` when you want one CLI invocation rather than the modal SQLite REPL with `.import` per session. For an LLM-CLI workflow, `q` is the structured-question step on top of unstructured CSV exports: agent generates SQL against `./report.csv`, you run via `q`, the small result is quotable for the next prompt — agent never has to load the file into memory. Skip for >1 GB files (use DuckDB for true columnar / out-of-core analytics) and for embedding into a redistributed product (GPL-3.0; use xsv / DuckDB instead). Caveats — Python startup (~150 ms per invocation, so don't put `q` in a tight loop), heuristic type detection (a `007` column becomes `7` — quote / cast in SQL when leading zeros matter), latest tag is 2021.
- [timewarrior](clis/timewarrior/) — command-line time tracking with plain-text storage (GothenburgBitFactory, MIT, v1.9.1): records intervals as start-timestamp + optional end + free-form tags (`timew start project-x review pr-1234` / `timew stop`), backfill with `timew track 09:00 - 10:30 project-x deep-work`, retroactive tagging with `timew tag @1 billable`, and built-in reports (`timew day` / `week` / `month` / `summary` / `gaps`) plus shell-script extensions that read the interval set on stdin and emit any custom report shape (CSV invoice, gantt, totals). The "time tracking that lives where the work happens" gap — for developers already in the terminal, `timew` is a local plain-text file-backed timer (data under `~/.timewarrior/data/` is `git`-able, `grep`-able, hand-editable) instead of a SaaS calendar app with its own auth / sync / "did the timer actually start" failure modes. Pick over [`taskwarrior`](clis/taskwarrior/) when the question is "what was done" not "what to do" (sibling project, same maintainer; most users run both via the on-modify hook so starting / stopping a `task` auto-starts / stops the matching `timew` interval with the task's tags); pick over [`watson`](clis/watson/) when active maintenance + extension hooks matter (timew released 2025-08, watson is in maintenance mode; watson stores in single JSON, timew in monthly text files better for very long histories); pick over [`hledger`](clis/hledger/) timeclock when you want first-class start/stop, retroactive tagging, gap detection, and chart-style `day` / `week` views rather than ledger register output; pick over Toggl / Harvest / Clockify when local-first + scriptability + no monthly fee for a Pro-tier feature beat mobile + team billing dashboards. For an LLM-CLI workflow, `timew` is the session-accounting substrate: agent reads `timew export :week --json` to summarize the week, draft an invoice, or surface "you logged 0 hours on project-x but 3 PRs were merged — backfill?" — all from local plain-text data, no OAuth dance. Caveats — no mobile / web UI (timer doesn't follow you to phone), no auto-detection (will record an 8-hour interval you actually spent in unrelated meetings unless you `stop`), tag space is flat free-form (typo `bilable` and your invoice misses an hour — wrap `timew start` in a validating shell function), reports are text/Unicode bar charts (export JSON for graphics), single-user only.

## Previously added (+3)

- [gcalcli](clis/gcalcli/) — Google Calendar from the shell (insanum, MIT, v4.5.1): a Python CLI that talks Google Calendar v3 API directly with subcommands for `agenda` / `add` / `quick` / `search` / `edit` / `delete` / `import` / `remind`, output as colored TTY tables, plain text, TSV, or JSON, and an OAuth refresh token cached in `~/.gcalcli_oauth` so subsequent calls are one HTTPS round-trip. The "calendar as a Unix datasource" tool — `gcalcli --nocolor agenda today tomorrow | head -3` is fast enough to run from a tmux `status-right` every minute, `gcalcli quick "design review thursday 3pm 45min"` lets the server-side natural-language parser do event creation, `gcalcli import ./conference.ics` ingests bulk schedules in one command, and `gcalcli remind 5 'notify-send "calendar" "%s"'` wires events into any local action (mute Slack, dim lights, start a recording) that Calendar's built-in reminders cannot do. Pick over [`khal`](clis/khal/) + `vdirsyncer` when the source-of-truth is Google directly and a local CalDAV sync layer is the cost; pick `khal` instead for offline-first, multi-provider, or non-Google accounts. Pick over raw Calendar v3 API + `curl` when you want OAuth, pagination, RFC 5545 datetime, and rate-limit handling done for you. Skip for pure todo lists ([`taskwarrior`](clis/taskwarrior/) is the right tool — many people run both). Caveats — one-time Google Cloud OAuth client setup (~10 min); token at `~/.gcalcli_oauth` in plaintext (treat like an SSH key); `quick` parser is en-US-biased (use `add --when` for unambiguous machine input).
- [kmon](clis/kmon/) — Linux kernel module manager + activity monitor in one TUI (orhun, GPL-3.0, v1.7.1): a Rust+`tui-rs` dashboard that wraps `lsmod` / `modinfo` / `modprobe` / `insmod` / `rmmod` / `depmod` / `dmesg` into a single keyboard-driven screen — left pane is the live filterable module list, right pane is `modinfo` (author, license, parameters, signature, dependencies) for the selected module, bottom pane streams the kernel ring buffer so a `load` or `unload` action's kernel-log output is visible without switching terminals. Common actions are one keystroke: `l` load, `u` unload, `b` blacklist (writes `/etc/modprobe.d/blacklist-kmon.conf` with the right syntax the first time), `r` dependency / used-by tree, `/` filter, `e` edit module parameters, `?` help. The discoverable surface for the Linux module subsystem — collapses `lsmod | grep && modinfo && dmesg | tail` into one screen and shows the dependency tree when an `rmmod` would fail "module is in use" so you actually know *why*. Pick over [`bottom`](clis/bottom/) / [`zenith`](clis/zenith/) / [`btop`](clis/btop/) when the question is "is this kernel module loaded with the right parameters and what does dmesg say about it" rather than "is the machine healthy" (those are general system monitors, orthogonal to module-level focus). Pair with [`procs`](clis/procs/) for process-level introspection (same Rust-TUI-wrapping-a-Unix-tool family, ships together comfortably). Skip on macOS / FreeBSD / WSL1 (Linux only) and skip for headless automation (call `lsmod` / `modprobe` directly). Caveats — root needed for write actions; `b` overwrites `blacklist-kmon.conf` (do not hand-edit); GPL-3.0 matters for closed-source appliance embedding (invoke as separate process is fine, linking crates is not).
- [wuzz](clis/wuzz/) — interactive `curl` as a Go TUI (asciimoo, AGPL-3.0, v0.5.0): the "REPL for HTTP" gap between `curl` and a full GUI client (Postman / Insomnia / Bruno). Multi-pane terminal layout (URL, method, params, headers, body, response headers, response body), tab between panes to edit any of them inline, `Ctrl-R` sends the request, response renders in place with content-type-aware syntax highlighting (JSON / HTML / XML) and a working in-pane search (`/`). Per-request "history" is JSON files under `~/.wuzz/`, snapshot-able with `Ctrl-F` and replayable from the shell with `wuzz --request-file …`, which makes it a clean handoff format between an LLM agent (drafts the request) and a human (inspects in `wuzz`, approves the agent's plan to make the same call programmatically). Pick over [`oha`](clis/oha/) / [`plow`](clis/plow/) / [`ali`](clis/ali/) when the goal is *single-request exploration* not load testing (use `wuzz` to figure out what request to make, then `oha` to load-test it); pick over [`xh`](clis/xh/) / [`httpie`](clis/httpie/) when you want to try eight variants of a header and watch the response change live (those are better one-line single-request clients); pick over [`posting`](clis/posting/) when you want one tiny Go binary instead of a Postman-style multi-collection workflow (use `posting` for the richer collection model); pair with [`mitmproxy`](clis/mitmproxy/) (originate from `wuzz`, watch the wire in mitmproxy). Skip for SaaS embedding (AGPL-3.0 network clause; prefer `xh` / `httpie` / `posting` for hosted UIs) and skip when the request flow is locked in and belongs in version control (use [`hurl`](clis/hurl/)). Caveats — v0.5.0 (2021) is the latest tag, dependency `gocui` is archived, history has no folder/environment abstraction, no cross-session cookie jar, TLS UI is `--tls-skip-verify` only.

## Previously added (+3)

- [kanidm](clis/kanidm/) — modern identity management platform with single-binary CLI (kanidm, MPL-2.0, v1.10.0): a Rust IDM server (`kanidmd`) plus thick client CLI (`kanidm`) that together replace the FreeIPA / 389-DS / OpenLDAP / Keycloak stack with one binary, one config file, and a CLI-first administration model. The server speaks **OAuth2 / OIDC** (full RP flow, PKCE, refresh tokens), **LDAP** read-only compatibility for legacy clients, **SSH public key distribution** (`AuthorizedKeysCommand` integration), **Unix accounts** via PAM/NSS (`kanidm_unixd`), **WebAuthn / passkeys** as a first-class credential (not a bolt-on second factor), and a **RADIUS** subset for Wi-Fi / VPN. Every web-UI operation maps to a `kanidm person create` / `kanidm group add-members` / `kanidm system oauth2 create` invocation, so provisioning is scriptable, GitOps-shaped, and reviewable. Pick over Keycloak when its 12-container footprint and SPI plugin surface are the cost (kanidm is one binary + one SQLite-derived store); pick Keycloak when enterprise-scale federation, custom auth flows, and thousands of OIDC clients matter. Pick over FreeIPA for sovereign / homelab / small-team deployments where "owns the host" is the FreeIPA tax. Pair with `kanidm_unixd` for PAM/NSS (replaces SSSD against this realm — do not run both). Skip when Active Directory semantics or LDAP *write* are required (kanidm's LDAP face is read-only by design) and when hosted IDM (Authentik / Auth0 / Okta / Entra ID) is acceptable. Caveats — passwords are second-class by default (passkeys preferred — plan the credential rollout); always read release notes before bumping a minor version on a production server.
- [hledger](clis/hledger/) — plain-text double-entry accounting in a single Haskell binary (simonmichael, GPL-3.0, 1.52.1): reads a human-editable journal file (one transaction per block, balanced debits/credits in indented postings) and answers `balance` / `register` / `incomestatement` / `balancesheet` / `cashflow` queries from the shell, plus a `hledger-ui` TUI and `hledger-web` browser UI over the same data. The journal format is the one `ledger` invented in 2003, so the same file is readable by `hledger`, `ledger`, and (via small converter) `beancount` — survives every SaaS shutdown for the next thirty years in a `git`-tracked text file. Daily workflow: download bank CSVs, `hledger import` (CSV-to-journal rules in `*.csv.rules` files classify each line into accounts), commit to git, then `hledger balance ^expenses:food --monthly --tree` or `hledger register assets:bank:checking -p 2026-04` answers questions. Multi-currency, commodity prices, historical-cost vs market-value reporting, budgets (`--budget`), forecasting (periodic transactions), and investment tracking are all in core. Pick over [`ledger`](https://www.ledger-cli.org/) when speed on large journals, friendlier error messages, and the sibling TUI / web UI matter (same file format — migration is a no-op). Pick over [`beancount`](https://github.com/beancount/beancount) when the workflow is shell-pipe-shaped, not Python-API-shaped (beancount + Fava is the Python-native sibling with a richer plugin ecosystem). Pick over SaaS (YNAB, Monarch, Lunch Money) when reviewable diffs on financial data and 30-year longevity beat polished mobile apps with bank integrations. Skip for pure cash-flow tracking with no double-entry mental model (a spreadsheet is lighter). Caveats — CSV import rules take an evening to learn (start from the docs' example rules); `hledger` is read-mostly from the CLI (new transactions edit the journal in `$EDITOR`; pair with `hledger-ui` / `hledger-web` / `hledger-iadd` for interactive add).
- [gallery-dl](clis/gallery-dl/) — command-line image-and-gallery downloader for ~300 sites (mikf, GPL-2.0, v1.32.0): a Python CLI (`gallery-dl <url>`) that walks paginated galleries, user feeds, tag searches, and entire account histories on image-hosting + social sites and writes the originals to disk with structured filenames, per-site metadata sidecars, and resume-on-interrupt state. Is to image hosts what [`yt-dlp`](https://github.com/yt-dlp/yt-dlp) is to video hosts — a per-site extractor framework that knows the pagination, login, rate-limit, and metadata quirks of each target so a single `gallery-dl <url>` does the right thing for Reddit (subreddits, users, saved), Twitter / X (timelines, likes, bookmarks), Pixiv, DeviantArt, Flickr, Tumblr, Imgur, Instagram, Mastodon / Pleroma, ArtStation, Behance, Danbooru / Gelbooru / e621 and the booru family, Kemono / Coomer, FurAffinity, Newgrounds, plus image hosts (Catbox, Pomf clones), wallpaper sites, manga / doujinshi readers, and dozens of niche communities. Output paths are template-driven (`{category}/{user}/{id}_{filename}`), `-o write-metadata=true` drops a JSON sidecar with author / tags / timestamp / source URL / EXIF next to every file, and `--download-archive` keeps a SQLite-or-text record of fetched IDs so re-runs only pull *new* items — the entire workflow is idempotent and incremental, suitable for cron. Authenticated sources work via `--cookies cookies.txt` exported from the browser (or per-site OAuth flows where supported). Pair with [`yt-dlp`](https://github.com/yt-dlp/yt-dlp) for a complete "archive any URL on the public web" toolbox (yt-dlp for video, gallery-dl for image galleries) and with Hydrus Network downstream (Hydrus consumes gallery-dl output). Pick over `wget -r` / [`httrack`](https://www.httrack.com/) when the target is in the supported-sites list — a generic recursive download misses pagination, login, and metadata. Skip for video sites (yt-dlp's job) and one-off single-image saves (right-click is faster). Caveats — supported-sites list churns (extractors break when sites change layouts; `pip install --upgrade gallery-dl` regularly); rate-limit / TOS compliance is the user's responsibility (configure `--sleep` / `--sleep-request` to be polite); active development is migrating to Codeberg (`https://codeberg.org/mikf/gallery-dl`) — GitHub remains the release-tag home for now but watch announcements if pinning the repo URL in CI.

## Previously added (+3)

- [khal](clis/khal/) — terminal CalDAV calendar (pimutils, MIT, v0.11.3): a Python CLI + `urwid` TUI (`ikhal`) that reads and writes plain `.ics` files in a local Maildir-style tree synced to any CalDAV server (Nextcloud, Radicale, Baikal, Sogo, Fastmail, Posteo, mailbox.org, iCloud with app-specific password) by `vdirsyncer`. `khal new tomorrow 14:00 1h "sync with N"` adds an event without leaving the shell, `khal calendar` paints a month grid, `ikhal` opens day/week/month views with edit-in-`$EDITOR` for full iCalendar fields. One-`.ics`-per-event storage means backups, `git`, `grep`, and `rsync` all work without a special export step. Multi-calendar with per-calendar colour, default, read-only flag, and conflict-priority. Pick over [`gcalcli`](https://github.com/insanum/gcalcli) when the source-of-truth is a CalDAV server you control or a hosted account with a CalDAV bridge (gcalcli talks Google API directly, no `vdirsyncer` round-trip — pick gcalcli when the only account is Google). Pairs with [`vdirsyncer`](https://github.com/pimutils/vdirsyncer) (sync engine, same `pimutils` org), [`khard`](https://github.com/lucc/khard) (CardDAV address book sibling), and [`mutt`](https://gitlab.com/muttmua/mutt) / [`neomutt`](https://github.com/neomutt/neomutt) / [`aerc`](clis/aerc/) for a fully terminal-native PIM stack. Skip when a polished GUI calendar with drag-to-resize matters more (Thunderbird + Lightning, Apple Calendar, Nextcloud web UI).
- [mbsync](clis/mbsync/) — bidirectional IMAP↔Maildir sync (isync project, ossi, GPL-2.0-or-later + OpenSSL exception, v1.5.1): the `mbsync` binary opens IMAP connections to one or more accounts and reconciles them against a local Maildir tree, tracking per-message UIDs and per-flag changes in `.uidvalidity` / `.mbsyncstate` sidecar files so a flag toggle / move / delete on either side propagates correctly to the other on the next run. Drives a Maildir under `~/.mail/<account>/<box>/` that [`mutt`](https://gitlab.com/muttmua/mutt) / [`neomutt`](https://github.com/neomutt/neomutt) / [`aerc`](clis/aerc/) / [`notmuch`](https://notmuchmail.org/) read directly with full two-way semantics. Per-channel `~/.mbsyncrc` rules (`Patterns "INBOX" "Archive*" "!Junk"`) and conflict policy (`Sync All` / `Create Both` / `Expunge Both`) make the same engine a one-way archive mirror, two-way sync, or download-only newsfeed depending on directives. Pick over [`getmail`](https://github.com/getmail6/getmail6) / [`fetchmail`](https://www.fetchmail.info/) when bidirectional sync (flag changes propagate back) is required (those are one-way download). Pair with [`goimapnotify`](https://gitlab.com/shackra/goimapnotify) for IDLE push notifications (mbsync itself is run-on-demand, not a daemon) and with [`mailctl`](https://github.com/pdobsan/mailctl) / `oauth2ms` for Gmail OAuth2 (mbsync accepts the token via `PassCmd` but does not perform the OAuth dance). Passwords go via `PassCmd "..."` from [`pass`](https://www.passwordstore.org/) / [`gopass`](https://github.com/gopasspw/gopass) / Keychain / libsecret — never plaintext in `mbsyncrc`. Skip when the remote is Exchange/Graph-only without IMAP (use the vendor's bridge or [`davmail`](https://davmail.sourceforge.net/)) and when the client is a GUI mail app (Thunderbird, Apple Mail speak IMAP directly, a separate sync engine adds nothing).
- [nickel](clis/nickel/) — gradually-typed configuration language with contracts and merging (nickel-lang, MIT, v1.16.0): a single Rust binary (`nickel`) plus an LSP (`nls`) that evaluates `.ncl` files into JSON / YAML / TOML and validates them against record contracts (lightweight structural types checked at evaluation time, with precise blame on failure). The pitch is "YAML / JSON / TOML ran out — I have ten Kubernetes manifests sharing 80 % of a base, three Terraform tfvars that need `if env == "prod"`, a Helm-values tree that wants real merging." `.ncl` is a Nix-flavoured purely-functional record language with lazy evaluation, deep recursive merging (`base & override`), type annotations (`name | String`) and contracts (`port | std.number.PortNumber`), list comprehensions, pattern matching, an `import` system, and a `std` library. `nickel export -f yaml config.ncl` emits the rendered YAML; `nickel eval --field deploy.image` prints one value; `nls` provides go-to-definition / hover types / inline diagnostics in any LSP-aware editor. Heritage is Nix — explicitly designed as "Nix the config language without the package manager." Pick over [`cue`](clis/cue/) when you want lazy evaluation + Nix-flavoured merge (cue uses unification + stronger types, pick cue when you want the constraint-solving model); pick over [`dhall`](clis/dhall/) when you want a less-strict, more-pragmatic language (dhall is total + proven termination, pick dhall when those guarantees matter); pick over [`jsonnet`](https://jsonnet.org/) when you want types/contracts (jsonnet is untyped, pick jsonnet for the K8s/Bazel ecosystem). All four occupy the same niche — the best one is the one your team will maintain. Skip when the config is genuinely flat and small (plain YAML/TOML + JSON Schema is lighter) and when you also need package management (that is Nix's job, not Nickel's).
- [duplicacy](clis/duplicacy/) — lock-free deduplicating cloud backup tool (gilbertchen, source-available custom license — free for CLI use, v3.2.4): one Go binary that backs up to local disk / SFTP / S3 / B2 / Wasabi / R2 / Storj / Google Drive / OneDrive / Dropbox / WebDAV / GCS, with HMAC-SHA-256 content-addressed chunks that let *multiple machines push into the same repository concurrently* without any server-side lock — N laptops + a NAS + a server can all back up to the same B2 bucket in parallel and dedupe against each other's chunks, which is the killer differentiator vs restic. Encryption is RSA-OAEP per-repo, optional Reed-Solomon erasure coding protects against silent storage corruption, and `prune` uses fossil collection (chunks marked unreferenced survive one more cycle before deletion) so concurrent backups never race the GC. Pick over [`restic`](clis/restic/) when multiple writers share one repo or when erasure coding matters; pick `restic` instead for the BSD-2 license, broader plugin ecosystem (`autorestic`, `backrest`), and single-writer simplicity. Pick over [`borgbackup`](clis/borgbackup/) when the target is cloud object storage (borg requires SSH); pick over [`rclone`](clis/rclone/) `+ rsync` for *versioned* backups (rclone is sync, not snapshots). Skip when single-machine + single-target + no encryption is enough — `rsync --link-dest` is simpler.
- [gocryptfs](clis/gocryptfs/) — encrypted FUSE overlay filesystem in Go (rfjakob, MIT, v2.5.7): mounts a plaintext view at one path that transparently encrypts to / decrypts from a ciphertext directory at another path, per-file AES-256-GCM (or `-xchacha` XChaCha20-Poly1305) chunked into 4 KiB authenticated blocks so random-access reads work without decrypting whole files and one-byte edits to a 1 GB file only re-encrypt a few KB (essential for the cloud-sync use case — Dropbox / iCloud / rclone-mounted S3 only re-uploads changed blocks). Filenames encrypted with EME (deterministic per directory so cloud dedup still works, but reveal nothing across directories). The ciphertext side is just a regular directory tree of regular files — no block device, no loop mount — so it backs up, syncs, snapshots, and restores with any tool that handles ordinary files. Deliberate evolution of EncFS with the chosen-ciphertext / file-size-oracle / no-MAC holes closed (Defuse Security 2017 audit is the historical baseline). Pick over [`age`](clis/age/) / [`sops`](clis/sops/) when access pattern is "edit files normally with normal tools" (age/sops are file-at-a-time round-trips). Pick over EncFS in every new deployment (EncFS is in maintenance, has known crypto holes). Pick over CryFS when per-file ciphertext mapping matters for cloud-sync diffs (CryFS hides metadata better but breaks per-file dedup). Pick over LUKS / dm-crypt for per-user encrypted folders on shared hosts where root is not available. Pairs with [`restic`](clis/restic/) / [`duplicacy`](clis/duplicacy/) (which dedupe the ciphertext tree — mostly metadata hiding rather than dedup gains since encrypted data is incompressible). Caveats — needs FUSE (kernel module on Linux, macFUSE on macOS, WinFsp on Windows); encrypted filenames ~1.4× plaintext length (Base64); reveals file sizes rounded to 4 KiB blocks (use CryFS if hiding sizes matters more).
- [dsq](clis/dsq/) — SQL queries against any structured file (multiprocessio, Apache-2.0, v0.23.0): one Go binary embedding SQLite that reads JSON / NDJSON / CSV / TSV / Parquet / ORC / Avro / Excel / YAML / logfmt / Apache + nginx access logs, exposes each input as `{}` (single-file shorthand) or `{N}` (positional) inside a SQL query, and returns JSON (or CSV with `-c`, or pretty TTY tables with `--pretty`). The pitch is "I have a 200 MB JSON dump from a vendor API and I want to JOIN it against a CSV from the analytics team and a Parquet snapshot from the data lake — and I do not want to write a Python script." Nested JSON flattens into dotted column names; full SQL (CTEs, window functions, subqueries, `JSON_EXTRACT`); `--cache` persists the converted SQLite under `~/.dsq-cache/` so repeated queries against a 1 GB Parquet skip re-parsing on subsequent runs. Pick over [`jq`](clis/jq/) / [`miller`](clis/miller/) / [`gron`](clis/gron/) when the operation is SQL-shaped (joins, group-by, window functions); pick those instead for path-extraction or per-record streaming transforms. Pick over [`q`](https://harelba.github.io/q/) when input might be Parquet / ORC / Avro / Excel / YAML (q is CSV/TSV-only). Pick over [`textql`](https://github.com/dinedal/textql) — textql is CSV-only and unmaintained since 2020. Pick over **DuckDB** when the workflow is "a one-liner from the shell against whatever file is in the directory"; pick DuckDB instead when the dataset is large enough that columnar execution matters (>1 GB Parquet, complex aggregations) or when persistent tables / indexes / extensions matter. Pairs with [`xsv`](clis/xsv/) / [`qsv`](clis/qsv/) (preprocess CSV first), [`visidata`](clis/visidata/) (interactive exploration after dsq narrows the data). Caveats — parent project (multiprocessio / datastation) wound down active development around 2023; loads source files into temp SQLite (10 GB JSON needs ~10 GB temp disk — use `--cache`); JSON root assumed to be array of objects (use `-s '<jsonpath>'` otherwise); needs CGO for embedded SQLite.

## Previously added (+3)

- [ccache](clis/ccache/) — drop-in compiler cache for `gcc` / `clang` / `nvcc` / MSVC (ccache, GPL-3.0-or-later, v4.13.4): sits between the build system (`make` / `ninja` / `cmake` / `bazel` / `meson` / `xcodebuild`) and the real compiler, hashes source + headers + flags (direct mode) or preprocessor output (preprocessor mode), and on a hit copies (or hardlinks) the previously-cached object file out of `~/.cache/ccache/` instead of running `cc1` — typical clean-rebuild speedups on a warm cache are 10×–50× for any C / C++ codebase with deep template instantiation (LLVM, Chromium, kernel modules, large game engines). `ccache --show-stats` is the dashboard (direct vs preprocessor hits, miss reasons, on-disk size); `secondary_storage` / `remote_storage` extends the cache across CI runners over HTTP / Redis / S3-compatible. Pick over [`sccache`](clis/sccache/) when the toolchain is purely native C/C++ on a single host and you want the smallest binary footprint and broadest distro coverage; pick `sccache` when you need Rust support or first-class cloud storage backends. Orthogonal to [`bazel`](clis/bazel/) / [`buck2`](clis/buck2/) (those have their own remote caches; ccache is for projects *not* on a hermetic build system). Skip when builds are already < 30 s (overhead exceeds savings) or the language is Go / Rust / JVM (those have native build caches).
- [overmind](clis/overmind/) — `Procfile`-based dev process manager that runs each declared process inside its own `tmux` window (DarthSim, MIT, v2.5.1): unlike `foreman` (which forks children directly and multiplexes their stdout into one terminal, losing TTY semantics and breaking interactive debuggers), overmind starts a detached tmux session and gives each Procfile entry its own window — which means `binding.pry` / `ipdb.set_trace()` / `delve` / `node --inspect-brk` actually work, `overmind connect web` attaches to a single process's tmux window with full scrollback, `overmind restart worker` cycles only that window without touching the web server / asset pipeline / database clients, and SIGTERM propagation correctly signals each child with a configurable graceful-shutdown timeout (matches what Kubernetes and systemd expect). `OVERMIND_FORMATION=web=2,worker=3` scales replicas; `.overmind.env` overlays per-developer environment overrides; `Procfile.dev` keeps the dev stack file separate from production. Pick over [`hivemind`](https://github.com/DarthSim/hivemind) (same author, no tmux dep) when per-process restart / attach / debugger PTYs matter — that is the entire reason overmind exists. Pick over [`mprocs`](clis/mprocs/) when you want detachable per-process tmux windows; pick mprocs when you want a single-screen TUI dashboard. Skip in production (overmind is explicitly a dev tool — production uses systemd / Kubernetes / Nomad) and skip when the stack is already a Docker Compose file.
- [slsa-verifier](clis/slsa-verifier/) — reference CLI for verifying SLSA provenance attestations (slsa-framework, Apache-2.0, v2.7.1): given a downloaded artifact (binary, OCI image, npm tarball) plus its `*.intoto.jsonl` provenance file or sigstore bundle, `slsa-verifier verify-artifact <bin> --provenance-path <prov> --source-uri github.com/org/repo --source-tag v1.2.3` checks all four claims of the SLSA chain — Sigstore Rekor inclusion proof, Fulcio-issued cert subject matches an allow-listed reusable builder workflow identity, source repo + ref match what you specified, and the builder is at a SLSA-L3-recognised version pin. Exits non-zero on any mismatch, which makes it the canonical CI-gate / admission-controller endpoint of the SLSA chain. Sibling subcommands `verify-image` (OCI registry-fetched provenance) and `verify-npm-package` cover the two other major artifact shapes; `verify-github-attestation` (added in v2.7.1) handles GitHub-native attestations produced by `actions/attest-build-provenance`. Pick over plain [`cosign verify`](clis/cosign/) / [`cosign verify-blob`](clis/cosign/) when the requirement is not just "is the signature valid" but "does the signed claim match the source repo + tag I expect, built by a builder workflow I trust at SLSA L3". Pair with [`syft`](clis/syft/) + [`grype`](clis/grype/) for SBOM + CVE scanning (orthogonal — what's *in* the artifact vs how it was *built*); pair with `witness` (in-toto) for the *creation* side of the same attestation chain. Skip when the threat model excludes build-pipeline tampering (for internal-only artifacts behind an authenticated registry, plain cosign against a KMS key is enough).

## Previously added (+3)

- [doctl](clis/doctl/) — DigitalOcean's official Go-implemented CLI (digitalocean, Apache-2.0, v1.155.0): one binary wraps every public DigitalOcean API surface — Droplets, Kubernetes (DOKS), App Platform, Spaces (S3-compatible), Volumes, managed Databases (Postgres / MySQL / Redis / Kafka / MongoDB), Load Balancers, Reserved IPs, Firewalls, VPCs, Container Registry, Functions (serverless), Monitoring, GenAI Platform — with a uniform verb-noun grammar (`doctl compute droplet create`, `doctl kubernetes cluster kubeconfig save`, `doctl apps create --spec app.yaml`), `--format ID,Name,PublicIPv4 --no-header` for shell-pipe output everywhere, and `--wait` for async actions (Droplet create / snapshot / resize block until status flips). Pick when the deployment target is *DigitalOcean specifically* — orthogonal to every other vendor CLI in the catalog ([`flyctl`](clis/flyctl/), [`heroku`](clis/heroku/), [`render-cli`](clis/render-cli/), [`railway`](clis/railway/), `aws`, `gcloud`, `az`) because it talks to a different provider's API. For App Platform, the deployment unit is a committed `app.yaml` (`doctl apps update <id> --spec app.yaml`) so GitOps-shaped pipelines work without a third-party orchestrator. Skip when the workload lives on AWS / GCP / Azure / OCI / Linode / Vultr — the right CLI is the vendor's own.
- [headscale](clis/headscale/) — open-source, self-hosted reimplementation of the Tailscale control plane (juanfont, BSD-3-Clause, v0.28.0): one Go binary (`headscale serve`) replaces hosted `controlplane.tailscale.com` while leaving every other piece of the Tailscale ecosystem untouched — official Tailscale clients on macOS / iOS / Android / Windows / Linux register against your control server with `tailscale up --login-server=https://headscale.example.com`, get `100.x.y.z` addresses, negotiate WireGuard tunnels peer-to-peer (control plane never sees packet payloads), and use DERP relays for NAT traversal. Feature parity for ACLs (HuJSON policy file with `groups`, `tagOwners`, `acls`, `ssh`, `autogroup:*`), tags-as-identity (v0.28 made tagged devices first-class — server identity, not user-owned), pre-auth keys (now bcrypt-hashed), MagicDNS, subnet routers, exit nodes, OIDC SSO; storage is one SQLite file (or PostgreSQL above ~1000 nodes) plus a config YAML. Pick over hosted [`tailscale`](clis/tailscale/) SaaS when per-user pricing at fleet scale is the problem, when the threat model demands no third party sees the metadata, when the deployment is air-gapped / sovereign-cloud, or when "I run my own thing" is the goal; pair with [`tailscale`](clis/tailscale/) (the client side), [`derper`](https://pkg.go.dev/tailscale.com/cmd/derper) (self-hosted DERP relay), and [`caddy`](clis/caddy/) / [`traefik`](clis/traefik/) for HTTPS termination. Skip when fewer than ~5 devices — Tailscale's free tier covers 100 devices / 3 users.
- [octodns](clis/octodns/) — Python-implemented DNS-as-code framework + `octodns-sync` CLI (octodns, MIT, v1.16.0): per-zone YAML files (`zones/example.com.yaml`, one record per key, typed records `A` / `AAAA` / `ALIAS` / `CAA` / `CNAME` / `MX` / `NS` / `SRV` / `TXT` / `dynamic` for weighted+geo) drive a planner + applier that synchronises desired state to *N* providers in parallel — Route 53, Cloudflare, Google Cloud DNS, Azure DNS, NS1, DNSimple, DigitalOcean, Hetzner, PowerDNS, BIND zonefiles, and ~30 more via separate `octodns-<provider>` PyPI plugins (engine and providers ship as separate packages so install footprint scales to actual use). `octodns-sync` prints a record-level diff per zone per target provider (`+ A example.com 300 198.51.100.1` style), `--doit` applies, `--force` overrides safety; the killer surface is **multi-provider per zone** — commit the same zone to Route 53 *and* Cloudflare, NS-record round-robin between them, and a single-provider outage stops mattering (the design that originally ran `github.com`). Pick over [`dnscontrol`](clis/dnscontrol/) when the team is Python-shaped or when multi-provider lockstep matters; pick `dnscontrol` instead for a JS/DSL config in one Go binary. Pick over generic [`terraform`](clis/terraform/) DNS-provider blocks when a DNS-shaped plan an on-call engineer can read at 2 a.m. matters more than a unified resource graph. Pair with [`dig`](clis/dig/) / [`drill`](clis/drill/) / [`dog`](clis/dog/) for post-apply verification. Skip for one small zone on one provider — the provider console is lighter; octodns earns its keep when zones × providers × records crosses a few dozen.

## Previously added (+3)

- [pre-commit](clis/pre-commit/) — Python-implemented multi-language git-hook framework (pre-commit, MIT, v4.4.0): one `.pre-commit-config.yaml` (each entry pinning a hook repo by `rev:` tag or SHA) is the single source of truth for every check that runs on `git commit` / `git push`, hook executables fetched from upstream repos and run in per-hook isolated environments (Python venv / Node / Ruby / Rust / Go / Docker / system) gated against the staged-file subset, and `pre-commit autoupdate` walks every entry to its latest tag in one command. The original cross-language hook orchestrator and the one most third-party linters / formatters publish a `.pre-commit-hooks.yaml` for, so adopting it gives the widest out-of-the-box hook catalog of any framework in this niche (`ruff`, `black`, `isort`, `mypy`, `prettier`, `eslint`, `shellcheck`, `shfmt`, `golangci-lint`, `terraform_fmt`, `detect-secrets`, `gitleaks`, `markdownlint`, `yamllint`, `hadolint`, `actionlint`, plus hundreds more). Pick over [`lefthook`](clis/lefthook/) when the project is Python-shaped or when the team wants the broadest catalog of pre-authored hooks; pick `lefthook` instead when the team wants to avoid a Python runtime dependency and wants parallel hook execution by default. Pick over [`talisman`](clis/talisman/) when the goal is a *general* hook framework — talisman is a single-purpose secret-scan hook, pre-commit hosts secret scanners (`detect-secrets`, `gitleaks`) as one of many concerns. Skip when the repo already standardised on `husky` + `lint-staged` for a Node-only codebase, or when CI alone is the gate.
- [bats](clis/bats/) — bats-core TAP-emitting test framework for Bash (bats-core, MIT, v1.11.0): each test case is a `@test "description" { ... }` block evaluated under a fresh subshell so `set -e` failures, `cd`, and `export` do not leak between cases; the `run cmd args...` helper captures stdout / stderr / exit code into `$output` / `$status` / `$lines` for assertions; companion libraries `bats-assert` / `bats-support` / `bats-file` / `bats-mock` add the matcher vocabulary (`assert_output --partial 'foo'`, `assert_file_exists`, `assert_failure 2`); and `--formatter tap` / `--formatter junit` slot directly into the same CI runners that parse `pytest --junitxml`. What to reach for when the thing under test is *itself* shell — a `Makefile`, a `justfile` recipe, a release script, a provisioning bootstrap, a `git` hook, the very wrapper functions in `~/.zshrc` that ship a team's local toolbelt. Pick over [`shellcheck`](clis/shellcheck/) / [`shfmt`](clis/shfmt/) when the question is *behavioral* not *static* (typically all three run side-by-side in the same CI job); pick over hand-rolled `assert.sh` shims when the project crosses ~5 test cases; pick over rewriting the script in Python / Go just to get a real test framework when the script is genuinely Unix glue. Skip when the codebase has *no* shell (use the language's native test framework) or when the only "shell" is a 5-line wrapper that calls one binary.
- [fdupes](clis/fdupes/) — classic Adrián López duplicate-file finder (adrianlopezroche, MIT, v2.3.0): walks one or more directory trees, groups files by size, short-circuits non-matches with a partial-content hash, and confirms duplicates with a full byte-for-byte comparison so the "these are the same file" answer is exact; output is plain stdout (one duplicate set per blank-line-separated block, ready to pipe into `xargs rm`), with `-r` recursive / `-S` show sizes / `-n` skip empty / `-A` exclude hidden / `-d` interactive delete with per-group keep-which prompt / `-N` (with `-d`) preserve first match and delete rest non-interactively / `-L` hardlink dupes together (reclaim disk without losing any path) / `-s` follow symlinks. The byte-by-byte final compare means a hash collision cannot delete the wrong file — paranoia-grade safety for the "de-dupe my photo backup" workflow. Pick over [`fclones`](clis/fclones/) when the workload is small-to-medium (a few hundred GB, a few hundred thousand files) and the goal is the simplest, most-shipped-by-distros tool (fclones wins on million-file trees with parallel walk + Rust core + reflink-aware dedup; fdupes wins on "already installed on every Linux box"); pick over [`czkawka`](clis/czkawka/) when the workflow is shell-pipe-shaped not GUI-shaped; pick over `rdfind` / `jdupes` when familiarity matters more than micro-optimisations. Skip when the dedup target is *block-level* on a CoW filesystem (use `duperemove` on btrfs).

## Previously added (+3)

- [elvish](clis/elvish/) — typed-pipeline shell with first-class data structures (elves, BSD-2-Clause, v0.21.0): a single Go binary that replaces `bash`/`zsh` with a language whose pipelines carry real values (lists, maps, closures, channels) rather than `IFS`-split byte streams, so `from-json | each {|r| put $r[name] }` does the work `jq | awk` would do in a POSIX shell, `try { … } catch e { … } finally { … }` replaces the `set -e` / `set -o pipefail` / `trap ERR` minefield with structured exceptions, and history search / directory jump / autosuggest / syntax highlighting / module system (`use github.com/zzamboni/elvish-modules/alias`) are in the core binary with no `oh-my-elvish` plugin manager to maintain. Pick over [`fish-shell`](clis/fish-shell/) when scripting ergonomics matter more than interactive flourishes (fish keeps the stringly-typed POSIX pipeline and invests in autosuggest/highlight; elvish keeps fewer interactive flourishes and invests in typed values + exceptions); pick over [`nushell`](clis/nushell/) when you want a Lispy general-purpose language with lists/maps as core types instead of a tabular SQL-flavoured DSL (nushell wins for table wrangling, elvish wins for small programs that happen to spawn processes); pick over [`oils`](clis/oils/) when you do *not* need POSIX compatibility for an existing bash codebase. Skip as the CI shell (CI scripts assume bash) and on shared servers where coworkers will edit your scripts.
- [cloudflared](clis/cloudflared/) — Cloudflare Tunnel client that exposes localhost over outbound-only TLS to a public URL (cloudflare, Apache-2.0, v2026.3.0): a single Go binary opens a persistent QUIC/TLS connection from your machine *out* to Cloudflare's edge and reverse-proxies inbound HTTPS back to a chosen loopback port, so a Mac on coffee-shop Wi-Fi or a Pi behind CGNAT becomes reachable at a stable hostname with no firewall changes, no static IP, no `certbot`, and no abuse-handling burden (DDoS protection and WAF run at the edge). Two modes: **quick tunnels** (`cloudflared tunnel --url http://localhost:8080`, no signup, random `*.trycloudflare.com` subdomain, dies with the process — the lowest-friction "demo this branch to a coworker right now" in the catalog) and **named tunnels** (Cloudflare account + a domain in a Cloudflare zone, persistent hostname, `cloudflared service install` runs it under launchd / systemd). Pick over [`bore`](clis/bore/) when you want edge TLS + DDoS protection + a free hosted broker (bore wins when you specifically want to *own* the broker on your own VPS); orthogonal to [`tailscale`](clis/tailscale/) (Tailscale builds a *private* mesh between your devices; cloudflared builds *public* ingress to one of them — many setups run both); orthogonal to [`sshuttle`](clis/sshuttle/) (sshuttle routes your laptop's egress through a remote SSH host; cloudflared routes inbound public traffic to your laptop). For LLM workflows, `cloudflared tunnel --url http://localhost:11434` exposes a local `ollama` / `mlx_lm.server` to a remote agent runner with one command and HTTPS by default. Skip when the workload demands no third-party plaintext exposure (TLS terminates at the edge by default — use `origin-cert` or pick a self-hosted tunneller like bore).
- [fswatch](clis/fswatch/) — cross-platform filesystem-event monitor (emcrisostomo, GPL-3.0, v1.20.1): a single C++ binary that abstracts over macOS FSEvents, Linux inotify, BSD kqueue, Solaris file-event-notification, and a polling fallback, so the same `fswatch -o . | xargs -n1 -I{} make` pipeline reacts to filesystem changes identically on every Unix without per-OS detection; output is plain stdout (one path per line, or event-flag-tagged with `-x`, or custom-format with `-f`) so it composes with `xargs` / `while read` / `awk` / `parallel` rather than forcing a built-in DSL or `-c "shell command"` flag. Pick over [`entr`](clis/entr/) when you want recursive directory watching with a fixed argv (entr reads the watch list from stdin, so re-listing with `find … | entr` is needed to pick up new files; fswatch watches whole trees recursively); pick over [`watchexec`](clis/watchexec/) when you want raw event stream to feed something more complex than "re-run my command" (watchexec bundles the command runner, signal handling, and `SIGTERM`-previous-run on new event — the right pick for the common case; fswatch is the lower-level Unix-pipeline primitive); pick over [`watchman`](clis/watchman/) when you do *not* need a long-running daemon with query history (watchman wins for >100k-file monorepos with many concurrent consumers; fswatch wins for scripts and small/medium projects). For LLM workflows, `fswatch -o <project> | head -1` is a synchronous "wait for any change in this tree, then return" primitive that gates an agent's next tool call on a build/test/format step having actually happened. Caveats — GPL-3.0 binary (use `libfswatch` for permissive embedding), Linux watch limits (`max_user_watches`) still apply on big trees.

## Previously added (+3)

- [tmuxp](clis/tmuxp/) — declarative tmux session manager (tmux-python, MIT, v1.67.0): a `~/.config/tmuxp/<name>.yaml` (or per-repo `.tmuxp.yaml`) names a session and lists windows with arrays of panes, each pane carrying a `start_directory` and `shell_command` list; `tmuxp load .` reconstructs the whole multi-window, multi-pane environment in one command (attaching if it already exists), `tmuxp freeze <session>` round-trips a hand-built session back to YAML so "I tweaked it interactively" is one keystroke from "committed to the repo," and `tmuxp convert` translates [`tmuxinator`](https://github.com/tmuxinator/tmuxinator) (Ruby) and [`teamocil`](https://github.com/remi/teamocil) (Ruby) workspace files for mixed-language teams. Underneath sits `libtmux`, the same project's pure-Python wrapper around tmux control mode, so the file is also addressable from automation that wants to introspect a live session without shelling out. Pick when the team is committed to `tmux` and wants reproducible dev environments as a committed artifact instead of a "open 4 panes, in pane 1 run X" README; pair with [`zellij`](clis/zellij/) only as the "or instead pick a modern multiplexer" branch (zellij replaces tmux; tmuxp manages it). Skip for single-process dashboards (use [`mprocs`](clis/mprocs/)) and for background task queues (use [`pueue`](clis/pueue/)).
- [mitmproxy](clis/mitmproxy/) — interactive HTTPS-intercepting proxy with a Python addon API (mitmproxy, MIT, v12.2.2): TUI (`mitmproxy`), web UI (`mitmweb`), and recorder (`mitmdump`) all driven by the same engine; on first launch it generates a local CA at `~/.mitmproxy/mitmproxy-ca-cert.pem`, and once you trust that cert in your browser / OS / language runtime, every TLS handshake to every host through `HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:8080` is silently re-terminated and rendered as an inspectable flow (request line, headers, decoded body, response, timing). The killer surface is the addon API — a 5-line `def request(flow): ...` Python file mutates, blocks, mocks, or replays traffic in flight (`flow.request.headers["Authorization"] = "..."`, `flow.response = http.Response.make(500, ...)`); supports HTTP/1, HTTP/2, HTTP/3, WebSocket, raw TCP/UDP and TLS variants of all of them, with content-views for JSON / protobuf / gRPC. Pick for "what is this third-party SDK / mobile app actually sending?" and "rewrite this header before it leaves my machine" — the canonical engineering MITM. Pair with [`xh`](clis/xh/) / [`httpie`](clis/httpie/) / [`curl`](clis/curl/) (those craft requests; mitmproxy intercepts traffic from clients you do *not* control), with [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) for ad-hoc gRPC authoring (mitmproxy intercepts existing gRPC). Skip on shared / production / personal-banking machines — trusting the local CA is security-relevant, and `.flows` capture files contain plaintext secrets (treat them like `.env`).
- [tailscale](clis/tailscale/) — zero-config WireGuard mesh VPN (tailscale, BSD-3-Clause, v1.96.4): `tailscaled` on each device authenticates against an identity provider (Google / GitHub / Okta / passkey / OIDC), gets a stable `100.x.y.z` address, and pairwise WireGuard tunnels are negotiated on demand with NAT traversal (DERP relays as fallback) so any node can `ssh laptop` / `curl http://nas:8080` from anywhere on the internet without a VPN concentrator or a single inbound port open at home; layered features include MagicDNS (resolve tailnet hostnames), Tailscale SSH (auth handled by tailnet identity, no `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` to maintain), Subnet routing (one node advertises `192.168.1.0/24` and the whole tailnet reaches the LAN behind it), Exit nodes (route all traffic through one tailnet member), Funnel (publish a tailnet HTTPS service to the public internet), Taildrop (file send between devices). The control plane is open-source as [`headscale`](https://github.com/juanfont/headscale) for fully self-hosted setups; the data plane is always pure WireGuard between peers (the control plane never sees packet payloads). Pick for "a flat private network across machines I own, without operating WireGuard configs by hand"; pair with [`mosh`](clis/mosh/) (mosh over a tailnet survives wifi roaming + sleep) and [`sshuttle`](clis/sshuttle/) only as the ad-hoc-one-bastion alternative (sshuttle for situations where you cannot install anything on the destination; tailscale for a fleet you control). Skip for vendor-isolated zero-trust to a single appliance (use raw WireGuard) and for one-port public tunnels of a local service (Funnel solves it inside tailscale, but a dedicated tunneller like [`bore`](clis/bore/) is lighter when *all* you want is one port public).

## Previously added (+3)

- [sqlx-cli](clis/sqlx-cli/) — companion CLI to the `sqlx` async pure-Rust SQL toolkit (launchbadge, dual Apache-2.0/MIT, v0.8.6): `sqlx migrate add -r <name>` scaffolds reversible up/down `.sql` files, `sqlx migrate run` applies pending migrations against `$DATABASE_URL` with checksum + execution-time recorded in a `_sqlx_migrations` ledger table, `sqlx database create|drop|reset` short-circuits the per-engine bootstrap dance, and the headline `cargo sqlx prepare` walks every `query!` / `query_as!` macro call in the crate, type-checks each SQL string against a live Postgres / MySQL / SQLite / MSSQL connection, and writes the resulting metadata to `.sqlx/` so subsequent `SQLX_OFFLINE=true cargo build` runs in CI compile **without** a database connection. Pick when the Rust service uses `sqlx` and the team wants compile-time SQL verification without making a live DB a hard build dependency; pair with [`dbmate`](clis/dbmate/) / [`atlas`](clis/atlas/) when the migration story has to span multiple non-Rust languages (sqlx-cli's migration table is `sqlx`-specific). Skip for `diesel` (use `diesel_cli`) or for projects that talk to the DB through a generated ORM rather than raw SQL.
- [cargo-watch](clis/cargo-watch/) — Rust file-watcher Cargo subcommand (watchexec, CC0-1.0, v8.5.3): `cargo watch -x check` debounces filesystem events for 500 ms and reruns `cargo check` on every save so type errors surface in under a second, `-x` is repeatable to chain `check → test → run` with stop-on-first-failure, `-s '<shell>'` runs arbitrary non-Cargo commands in the same loop, `-i 'fixtures/**'` excludes globs beyond `.gitignore`, `-c` clears the screen each iteration. The decade-old muscle memory in countless `Justfile`s and `Makefile`s for "save and see results" Rust development. Pick when the editor's rust-analyzer surface is not enough — you want a real `cargo test` or `cargo run` retriggered automatically. Pairs with [`bacon`](clis/bacon/) (TUI alternative that surfaces errors in a panelled UI rather than serial command output) and [`just`](clis/just/) / [`mask`](clis/mask/) (the `just watch` recipe usually shells out to `cargo watch`). Note: upstream is in maintenance mode and recommends `watchexec` directly for new projects, but `cargo watch` remains the default install in many existing toolchains.
- [rqlite](clis/rqlite/) — distributed, fault-tolerant SQLite (rqlite, MIT, v10.0.1): single Go binary `rqlited` wraps SQLite in a HashiCorp Raft log so a 3- or 5-node cluster elects a leader, replicates every write through Raft, and applies committed entries to each node's local SQLite file. Clients talk plain HTTP/JSON — `POST /db/execute` for writes, `POST /db/query` for reads with consistency knob `none|weak|linearizable|strong`, `POST /db/request` for parameterised mixed batches — and an interactive `rqlite` shell ships in the same binary for `psql`-style sessions. TLS for HTTP + inter-node Raft, basic-auth + permission file, `GET /db/backup` produces a binary SQLite snapshot, read-only "non-voter" nodes serve geo-distributed reads. Pick when "I love SQLite but I need more than one machine and I cannot lose writes when one dies" describes the workload — small-to-medium relational data with write durability across node failure. Pair with [`litestream`](clis/litestream/) when the requirement is single-writer with continuous replication to object storage instead of multi-node Raft, or [`dolt`](clis/dolt/) when versioned-branchable SQL matters more than HA. Skip for sharded multi-writer OLTP (rqlite's write throughput is capped by the leader's local SQLite) and for embedded single-process use cases where vanilla SQLite already wins.

## Previously added (+3)

- [jira-cli](clis/jira-cli/) — single-binary Atlassian Jira Cloud / Data Center client (ankitpokhrel, MIT, v1.7.0): one-time `jira init` then every common verb is a flag (`jira issue list -a$(jira me) --plain` for TSV, `jira issue create -tBug -s"…" --no-input` for CI, `jira issue move PROJ-123 "In Review"` to walk transitions, `jira sprint list --current --plain`), plus a TUI board on the same command without `--plain`, plus a JQL escape hatch (`--jql "project=X AND status!=Done AND updated>=-7d"`) that exposes the full advanced-search surface. Per-project `.jira/.config.yml` auto-switches active project / board / sprint when you `cd` between repos. Pick when LLM agents or shell scripts need to read a real ticket queue and post structured updates without writing a Jira REST wrapper; pair with [`gh`](clis/gh/) / [`glab`](clis/glab/) for the PR side of the same loop. Skip if the team is on Linear / Shortcut / GitHub Issues only (use [`linear-cli`](https://github.com/evangodon/lr) / `gh issue`) or if the workflow is fully UI-driven and humans never want a CLI shape.
- [teller](clis/teller/) — universal secrets CLI (tellerops, Apache-2.0, v2.0.7 Rust rewrite) that reads a `.teller.yml` mapping each needed secret to one of ~20 backends (Vault, AWS SM/SSM, GCP SM, Azure KV, 1Password Connect, Doppler, Consul, etcd, dotenv, LastPass, Conjur, Heroku config, Cloudflare Workers env, GCP KMS) and materialises them into a child process via `teller run -- <cmd>` (env-only, no disk artefact, exits with the child) or any of `env` / `dotenv` / `yaml` / `json` / `k8s` / `terraform` exports for downstream tools. `teller scan` is a pre-commit gate against literal copies of provider-managed secrets, `teller redact` is a streaming log filter that masks known values out of CI output, `teller copy` / `teller mirror` move secrets between providers without a custom script. Pick over a hand-rolled `direnv` + `.envrc` + `op read` chain when the org spans more than one secret backend; pick over raw `vault read` / `aws ssm get-parameter` shell calls when an LLM agent's credentials should live for one process invocation only. Skip when one provider's native CLI plus dotenv covers the workload, or when [`sops`](clis/sops/) / [`age`](clis/age/) on git-committed encrypted YAML is the deployment model (teller is for live fetch; sops is for at-rest encryption).
- [pls](clis/pls/) — `prettier` `ls` (pls-rs Rust rewrite, GPL-3.0, v0.0.1-beta.9) where each directory's `.pls.yml` declares which entries to surface, hide, group, and annotate — so a repo's `pls` invocation shows `src/` and `tests/` first, hides `node_modules/` / `target/` / `__pycache__/`, and tags `Cargo.lock` with a "lockfile" badge without any per-user shell config. Detail columns are configurable per directory (`loc` / `lang` / `tests-passing` via hooks for code, `created` / `words` / `headings` for docs); filter / sort / group are real expressions (`--filter "name ~ '\\.rs$' and size > 1k"`, `--sort modified-desc`, `--group ext`) instead of `ls -ltrSh` flag soup; git status badges and Nerd Font icons are out-of-the-box. Pick when a repo wants to ship an authored "first-look" view that humans *and* LLM agents see when they `pls` the root — the curated table-of-contents costs the agent fewer context tokens than the raw alphabet-soup `ls` dump. Pair with [`onefetch`](clis/onefetch/), [`tokei`](clis/tokei/), [`scc`](clis/scc/) for richer first-impression columns, complementary to [`eza`](clis/eza/) / [`lsd`](clis/lsd/) (those are universal `ls` replacements; pls is a per-repo curated view). Skip as a global `ls` alias (eza/lsd fit better) or when no one will write the `.pls.yml` (the value collapses to "another modern ls").

## Latest additions (+3)

- [qrtool](clis/qrtool/) — single-binary Rust QR code encode + decode (sorairolake, MIT/Apache-2.0, v0.11.6) that covers ~95% of QR workflows from one CLI: `qrtool encode -t terminal "$URL"` renders a scannable QR straight to the terminal as Unicode half-blocks (Wi-Fi password to a guest's phone over SSH, no file ever created), `-l H -s 8 -o out.png` emits a high-EC PNG sized for print, `-t svg` writes resolution-independent vector output for the README, and `qrtool decode received.png` reads a QR screenshot back into its plain-text payload via the `rxing` ZXing port (handles rotation, blur, partial occlusion). Pick over `qrencode` (encode-only, C with PNG-lib build deps) and `zbarimg` (decode-only) when you want one static binary that does both; pick over `python-qrcode` + `Pillow` on a server where dragging in CPython for one-shot QR generation is overkill. Skip when you already have a working `qrencode` + `zbarimg` pair scripted in (the win is consolidation, not capability) or when the workload is bulk barcode scanning (use `zbar` directly).
- [heh](clis/heh/) — vim-keybinding terminal hex editor in Rust (ndd7xv, MIT, v0.6.0) that closes the gap between read-only `hexyl` and the 1995-era `bvi`/`hexedit` UX: classic three-pane layout (address / hex bytes / ASCII rendering) with `hjkl` movement, `:1234` / `:0x1F00` to jump to decimal or hex offset, `i` to enter byte-overwrite edit mode, `/DEADBEEF` to search a hex sequence, `:w` to save in place. Edits are byte-level overwrites (file size never changes) which matches how you patch firmware images, save files, or binary protocol captures — accidental insertions that would desync every later offset are not a class of bug here. Pair with [`hexyl`](clis/hexyl/) for the pipeable read-only side of the same workflow (`hexyl in.bin | less`, then drop into `heh in.bin` to actually change a byte). Pick over `bvi` / `hexedit` when vim muscle memory should map 1:1; pick over `ImHex` when you don't want a 100 MB GUI for a one-byte fix on a remote box. Skip for serious reverse engineering (use [`radare2`](https://github.com/radareorg/radare2) / `rizin` / `ghidra`), for read-only hex dumping in pipelines (use [`hexyl`](clis/hexyl/)), or when you need pattern-language structured parsing (ImHex's niche).
- [sc-im](clis/sc-im/) — ncurses spreadsheet TUI with vim modal editing (andmarti1424, BSD-4-Clause, v0.8.5), the modern descendant of the venerable `sc` from 1981: opens CSV / TSV / XLSX into a real cell grid where `=@sum(A1:A100)` typed into B1 recomputes live as you edit the column, `h/j/k/l` moves between cells, visual mode (`v`) selects ranges, `:w! out.xlsx` exports to Excel via libxlsxwriter, and `:lua` runs Lua snippets against the live sheet for transforms that don't fit a formula. Pick over [`visidata`](clis/visidata/) when the workflow is "type a formula into a cell and see it recalc" rather than column-stream exploration (visidata is great for "what's in this 4 GB CSV" — sc-im is for "let me actually fill in the budget"); pick over [`mlr`](clis/mlr/) / [`miller`](clis/miller/) when interactive cell-by-cell editing beats a verb pipeline; pick over [`harlequin`](clis/harlequin/) / [`lazysql`](clis/lazysql/) when the data lives in a flat file, not a database. Skip for SQL-shaped workloads (use a SQL TUI), for reproducible pipelines that should live in git (use [`mlr`](clis/mlr/) or a notebook), or when [`visidata`](clis/visidata/)'s exploratory verbs already cover the question.

## Previously added (+3)

- [skopeo](clis/skopeo/) — daemonless container-registry plumber from the containers project: one Go binary inspects, copies, lists tags, deletes, and syncs OCI / Docker v2 images directly between every transport that matters (`docker://`, `oci:`, `dir:`, `containers-storage:`, `docker-daemon:`, `docker-archive:`) without a running engine. `skopeo copy A B` streams registry-to-registry with no local pull, `skopeo inspect` reads only the manifest + config blob (peeking at a 3 GiB image is sub-second), `skopeo sync --src yaml` mirrors a curated upstream set into an air-gapped registry idempotently, multi-arch manifest lists preserved with `--multi-arch all`. Pick when CI image-promotion (build → staging → smoke tests → prod) needs to be a sequence of `copy` calls instead of `pull && tag && push` round-trips through a daemon, or when you want to interrogate a remote image's labels / size / layers / signatures before paying for the layer download. Pairs with [`cosign`](clis/cosign/) (sign / verify after copy), overlaps with [`crane`](clis/crane/) (Google's leaner counterpart, fewer transports) and [`regctl`](clis/regctl/) / [`oras`](clis/oras/) on the artifact side. Skip when a `docker pull` / `push` cycle is already fine and the daemon is running — the win is *not needing the daemon*.
- [goss](clis/goss/) — server validation as YAML: one declarative file enumerates every `package` / `service` / `port` / `user` / `group` / `file` / `command` / `dns` / `http` / `process` / `mount` / `interface` / `kernel-param` assertion the host should satisfy, `goss validate` checks them in parallel against the live system in milliseconds with TAP / JUnit / JSON / nagios output, `goss autoadd nginx` bootstraps the YAML by introspecting what is already running, `dgoss` wraps the same flow around `docker run` for image testing in CI, and `goss serve` exposes the rolled-up result as an HTTP healthcheck a load balancer can poll. Pick over a pile of `curl /health` checks when "is this host actually serving traffic" needs to cover process + port + file + DNS + HTTP simultaneously, or as the outside-in CI gate that catches what Ansible / Packer / Dockerfile converge missed. Pairs with [`hadolint`](clis/hadolint/) + [`dockle`](clis/dockle/) + [`trivy`](clis/trivy/) — four orthogonal gates for one image build (lint Dockerfile / audit image config / scan CVEs / assert runtime behaviour). Skip when a single `curl /health` covers the question or when the host is so dynamic that the assertions you would write are mostly "this thing exists in some form" — at that point a metrics-based readiness probe wins.
- [benthos](clis/benthos/) — stream processor where the whole pipeline is one YAML file (originally `Jeffail/benthos`, continued as `warpstreamlabs/bento` Apache-2.0 and as `redpanda-data/connect`): N inputs (Kafka / NATS / SQS / Redis / Pulsar / MQTT / webhook / Postgres CDC) → declarative processors (filter / map / `bloblang` transforms / HTTP enrich / `branch` parallel side-effects / `workflow` DAG) → N outputs, with batching, backpressure, at-least-once delivery, retries with exponential backoff, dead-letter routing, distributed tracing, and Prometheus metrics shipped by the engine on every input/output. Stateless by design — the state lives in the brokers and stores it talks to — so a binary restart is free and horizontal scale is "run more pods". Pick over Flink / Spark / Beam when the team has Kafka → enrich → Postgres / S3 / Elasticsearch shaped work and not the operational appetite for a JVM cluster; pick over a custom Python worker fleet when at-least-once + DLQ + parallel branch fan-out should be engine concerns, not application code. Complementary to [`vector`](clis/vector/) (vector excels at logs + metrics observability piping, bento at event-shaped JSON business pipelines) and [`kcat`](clis/kcat/) (poke topics by hand while iterating on the config). Skip for stateful stream joins / windowed aggregations across millions of keys (use Flink / Materialize / RisingWave) and for one-shot batch ETL ([`duckdb`](clis/duckdb/) / [`dlt`](clis/dlt/) / a script).

## Previously added (+3)

- [grpcui](clis/grpcui/) — interactive web UI for any gRPC server, built on the same descriptor stack as [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) by the same author: point it at a host with reflection enabled (or feed it a `.proto` / `protoset`), it spins up a local browser form where every RPC and every message field is a typed input you can fill in and fire, including streaming RPCs (server / client / bidi) where you send N request messages from the page and watch the response stream arrive live. Same descriptor pipeline as `grpcurl` but the ergonomics are click-driven, request metadata (auth headers, tenant, trace IDs) sit in dedicated boxes, and the form remembers your last inputs per method across reloads. Pick when you are demoing a gRPC service to someone who will not learn protobuf JSON encoding, when on-call debugging needs varying field values without rebuilding a `grpcurl` JSON blob each time, or when QA needs a zero-config explorer against staging without any `.proto` files on their machine. Pairs with [`grpcurl`](clis/grpcurl/) (scriptable counterpart for CI), [`buf`](clis/buf/) (lint + breaking-change + descriptor generation feeding `-protoset`), and [`bombardier`](clis/bombardier/) / [`vegeta`](clis/vegeta/) (drive load once grpcui has proved the request shape). Skip for fully-scripted workflows (use `grpcurl`), for HTTP/REST (use [`httpie`](clis/httpie/) / [`xh`](clis/xh/) / [`posting`](clis/posting/)), or when the server has no reflection *and* no protoset *and* the proto sources are not nearby.
- [kubie](clis/kubie/) — `kubectx` / `kubens` reimagined so each context is a real subshell, not a global edit of `~/.kube/config`. Single static Rust binary: `kubie ctx prod` opens a new shell whose `KUBECONFIG` points at an isolated temporary file, prompt segment shows `[cluster|namespace]`, `exit` drops you back to the parent context, and the original kubeconfig is never mutated — so two terminal tabs can sit on prod and staging simultaneously without one stray tab silently switching the global active context. Reads multiple kubeconfig files via glob (per-cluster issued by your platform team, GKE / EKS auth plugins, ephemeral kind / k3d clusters), fuzzy picker when called bare, `kubie exec '*-prod' default kubectl get nodes` fans the same command across a class of clusters in parallel. Pick over [`kubectx`](clis/kubectx/) when you have ever nuked the wrong resource because some background tab quietly switched the active context, when you manage dozens of kubeconfig files you do not want to merge into one giant file, or when you want a prompt that always tells you which cluster + namespace this shell is on without configuring `kube-ps1` / starship segments. Pair with [`k9s`](clis/k9s/) / [`kdash`](clis/kdash/) launched *inside* a kubie subshell (they inherit the right context), [`stern`](clis/stern/) / [`kubeshark`](clis/kubeshark/) (multi-pod log + traffic tools that benefit from clean per-shell `KUBECONFIG`), [`kubefwd`](clis/kubefwd/) (tunnel into a cluster scoped to one shell). Skip if you only ever talk to one cluster from one terminal (vanilla `kubectx` is fine), or if your team's tooling assumes the *global* current context — kubie's per-shell isolation is the whole point and will surprise those tools.
- [dolphie](clis/dolphie/) — real-time MySQL / MariaDB / ProxySQL TUI dashboard you can point at any host and read like a flight deck. Textual-based Python app that reuses the same `information_schema` / `performance_schema` / `SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS` queries you would otherwise paste into a `mysql` shell, but presents them as live panels updated every second: top-N queries by latency, running threads with full statement text, replication lag and SBM, InnoDB row-lock waits and deadlock counter, buffer pool hit rate, history of QPS / TPS / network / temp-tables. Hotkeys per panel for filter / sort / kill / EXPLAIN so the workflow "see a slow thread → expand it → EXPLAIN → kill" stays inside one window. `--record-file` captures the metric stream as a session-scoped flight recorder, `--replay-file` lets you scrub it back during the post-mortem without the live host changing under you, `--read-only` disables every mutating action for on-call engineers who need visibility but should not be one keystroke from `KILL`-ing the wrong session. Pair with [`mycli`](clis/mycli/) (interactive REPL with completion for the SQL you decide to run after dolphie shows what is hot), [`harlequin`](clis/harlequin/) / [`lazysql`](clis/lazysql/) (cross-engine TUIs for ad-hoc queries against the same host), [`pgcli`](clis/pgcli/) (Postgres sibling for the other engine). Skip for Postgres (use `pgcli` + your APM), for long-term metric storage ("everything must go through Grafana / PMM" — dolphie is point-in-time tactical visibility, not a replacement for a query analyzer like Percona PMM's QAN).

## Previously added (+3)
